Remarks on cotorsion theories
Alexandru Stanculescu

Abstract
. We present some results from classical homological algebra using the language of cotorsion theories in abelian categories. The results are a couple of foundational facts about homological dimension, the Künneth formula and the universal coefficients formula. We also present some “totally acyclic” cotorsion theories and revisit a couple of results from the literature concerning resolutions of chain complexes in abelian categories.



We regard cotorsion theories as an organizational principle in homological algebra.
The main purpose of this paper is to illustrate this point of view by presenting some
results from classical homological algebra using the language of cotorsion
theories in abelian categories. The results are a couple of foundational facts
about homological dimension
[
4
, §8 n
o
1,3]
,
the Künneth formula [
loc. cit.
, §4 n
o
7] and
the universal coefficients formula [
loc. cit.
, §5 n
o
6].
Presenting homological dimension (resp. the universal coefficients formula)
in the language of cotorsion theories means here replacing the pair injective-projective
module by a pair of comparable cotorsion theories (resp. by an arbitrary cotorsion theory)
in an abelian category; the Künneth formula in abelian categories roughly means
the usual Künneth formula where the flat cotorsion theory has been extended
from modules to abelian categories that fit in an abelian Tensor-Hom-Cotensor
situation
[
11
]
and the
\mathsf{Tor}
bifunctors are constructed using
(complete) flat resolutions. A Tensor-Hom-Cotensor situation is also known under
the name adjunction of two variables. In linear algebra, two basic examples
of THC-situations are the one that has as Hom bifunctor the abelian group of
homomorphisms between two modules and the one that has as
Tensor bifunctor the tensor product between right and left modules.
The idea of homological algebra in an abelian THC-situation is suggested
by Bourbaki’s functorial treatment of classical homological algebra.
Overall, extending the triad injective-projective-flat module to more general
objects is, in the case of the above mentioned results, formal.

Another purpose of the paper is to present some “totally acyclic” cotorsion
theories in categories of chain complexes and revisit some results from the
literature concerning resolutions of complexes. The construction of
resolutions may help establishing that a certain cotorsion theory in
complexes is complete.

All the arguments use standard properties of abelian categories only and
we do not use the bifunctors
\mathsf{Ext}
since the very notion
of cotorsion theory can be formulated without them. Also, given the level
of generality that we want to work and with the aim of making the paper
as simple as possible, we felt that the absence of
\mathsf{Ext}
would
not make the results or proofs artificial. The approach to establishing
that a class of objects is part of a cotorsion theory emphasizes the choice
of (co)generating classes and adjoint functors.

Many of our results that involve cotorsion theories assume that these are
complete. We do not address here the question of completeness of the
cotorsion theories that we construct as this seems to require different
techniques.

In section 1 we introduce and give some basic properties of cotorsion
theories in abelian categories without using
\mathsf{Ext}
. We spend
some time studying stability properties of the classes

{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L})
and

{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

(whatever they mean).

In section 2 we study the possibility of “pulling back” cotorsion
theories along a functor that has an adjoint (
2.1.2
) and
of “pulling back-and-restricting” cotorsion theories
(
2.2.2
,
2.2.4
,
2.2.5
) in
abelian categories that fit in an abelian Tensor-Hom-Cotensor
situation.

In section 3 we generalize (
3.1.4
) the flat cotorsion theory

[
8
, Lemma 7.1.4]
from modules to abelian categories that fit in
an abelian THC-situation. The resulting generalized flat cotorsion theory
has the usual properties (
3.1.5
,
3.1.6
,
3.1.7
).

In section 4 we recall some facts about complexes in abelian categories.
In section 5 we associate (
5.2.1
,
5.2.4
) to a pair of
comparable cotorsion theories two new ones; the left class of
the first consists of objects of “projective” dimension
\leqslant n

and the right class of the second consists of objects of
“injective” dimension
\leqslant n
.

In section 6 we revisit the standard and the bar THC-situations for categories of
complexes in abelian categories
[
22
, Section 4.3]
. The former is an enriched
situation (
6.1.9
). The flat complexes associated to the two
THC-situations have the usual description (
6.1.3
,
6.2.3
).

In section 7 we very briefly review (
7.1
), following Bourbaki

[
4
, §3,4]
, the construction of the torsion product associated
to the Tensor bifunctor of the standard THC-situation. The only
difference with
loc. cit.
is that we do not use functorial
free resolutions but complete flat resolutions; for this reason we
require that the generalized flat cotorsion theory is complete.
The torsion product is used to prove a Künneth type theorem for
complexes in abelian categories (
7.3.10
,
7.3.11
). The
proof follows Bourbaki [
loc. cit.
, §4 n
o
7],
except that we work with the second argument of the Tensor bifunctor;
this choice requires some small sign considerations.

In section 8 we revisit some elementary facts about the enriched and
homotopy orthogonality relations for complexes in abelian categories
and the formal theory of
K
-flat complexes associated to the standard
THC-situation (
[
23
, Sections 1,5]
,
[
14
, Proposition 3.7]
).

In section 9 we prove (
9.1.4
) a universal coefficients theorem
for complexes in an abelian category. The proof follows Bourbaki
[
loc. cit.
, §5 n
o
6].

In section 10 we present (
10.2.1
,
10.2.2
,
10.3.1
)
a couple of “totally acyclic” cotorsion theories and the “dg-flat” cotorsion
theory in categories of complexes in abelian categories.

In section 11 we study the compatibility between some cotorsion
theories and corner morphisms for categories of complexes

[
13
, Theorem 5.1]
.

In section 12 we revisit (
12.1.3
,
12.1.9
)
a couple of results from the literature
[
17
]
,
[
23
]

concerning resolutions of complexes.

1.
Cotorsion theories

1.1.

Monomorphisms and epimorphisms in abelian categories will be denoted by
the symbols
\rightarrowtail
and
\twoheadrightarrow
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. For our purposes a composable
pair
u:A^{\prime}\to A
,
v:A\to A^{\prime\prime}
of morphisms of
\mathcal{A}
will be called

sequence
and denoted by
(u,v)
. A sequence
(u,v)
in

\mathcal{A}
is
semi-exact
if
vu=0
,
exact
if
{\rm Im}\,u={\rm Ker}\,v
.
An exact sequence
A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}A\to A^{\prime\prime}
is
left split

if
u
has a retraction and an exact sequence

A^{\prime}\to A\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}
is
right split

if
v
has a section.

1.1.1.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has a set
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
of cogenerators.
Let
A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}

be a sequence in
\mathcal{A}
.

(1) If the sequence
\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime\prime},C_{i})\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A,C_{i})\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime},C_{i})
is
semi-exact (exact) for all
i\in I
then
(u,v)
is semi-exact (exact).

(2) If
\bar{u}
is a monomorphism for all
i\in I
then
u
is an epimorphism and
if
\bar{v}
is an epimorphism for all
i\in I
then
v
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

(1) Suppose that
(\bar{v},\bar{u})
is exact for each
i\in I
.
Consider the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{p}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{r}
\displaystyle{A^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}

There is a morphism
\delta:{\rm Coker}\,i\to{\rm Im}\,v
such that
\delta r=q
and

j\delta=k
. Let
f:B\to{\rm Coker}\,i
be such that
\delta f=0
but
f\neq 0
.
There is then
h_{i}:{\rm Coker}\,i\to C_{i}
such that
h_{i}f\neq 0
.
We have
\bar{u}(h_{i}r)=0
, hence there is
g_{i}:A^{\prime\prime}\to C_{i}
such
that
g_{i}v=h_{i}r
. We have
g_{i}kr=h_{i}r
, so
g_{i}k=h_{i}
.
This implies that
h_{i}f=g_{i}kf=g_{i}j\delta f=0
, contradiction.
Therefore
\delta
is an isomorphism, hence
(u,v)
is exact.
∎

1.1.2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}
is a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}

such that for every
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is a
monomorphism
A\to X
with
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
Let
A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}

be a semi-exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
.

(1) If the sequence
\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime\prime},X)\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A,X)\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime},X)
is
exact for all
X\in\mathcal{K}
then
(u,v)
is exact.

(2) If
\bar{u}
is an epimorphism for all
X\in\mathcal{K}

then
u
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

(1) Consider the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{p}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{q}
\displaystyle{A^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Ker}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}

Let
\alpha:{\rm Ker}\,v\to B
be a morphism such that
\alpha f=0
. Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Ker}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}}

where
PO
means pushout. We can find a monomorphism
k:PO\to X
, where
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
We have
k\alpha^{\prime}u=ki^{\prime}\alpha fp=0
, therefore there is
h:A^{\prime\prime}\to X
such that
hv=k\alpha^{\prime}
.
Then
ki^{\prime}\alpha=hvi=0
, so
\alpha=0
and therefore
f
is an epimorphism.
∎

1.2.

We denote by
\mathrm{Ab}
the category of abelian groups. Let
\mathcal{A}

be an abelian category and
\mathcal{K}
a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
We define

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\{X\in Ob(\mathcal{A}),\ \mathcal{A}(-,X):\mathcal{A}^{op}\to\mathrm{Ab}{\rm\ preserves\ epimorphisms\ with\ kernel\ in}\ \mathcal{K}\}

and

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}=\{X\in Ob(\mathcal{A}),\ \mathcal{A}(X,-):\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{Ab}{\rm\ preserves\ epimorphisms\ with\ kernel\ in}\ \mathcal{K}\}

When
\mathcal{K}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

consists of the injective objects of
\mathcal{A}

and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
of the projective objects.

1.2.1.

(1) An object
X
of
\mathcal{A}
is in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

if and only if for every exact sequence

A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}
with
A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v\in\mathcal{K}
the sequence

\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime\prime},X)\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A,X)\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(A^{\prime},X)

is exact.

(1bis) An object
X
of
\mathcal{A}
is in
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}

if and only if for every exact sequence

A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}
with
{\rm Ker}\,u\in\mathcal{K}
the sequence

\mathcal{A}(X,A^{\prime})\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(X,A)\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathcal{A}(X,A^{\prime\prime})

is exact.

(2) If
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
are two classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
,

(a)
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{L}
implies

\mathcal{L}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
;

(b)
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{L}^{\perp}=(\mathcal{K}\cup\mathcal{L})^{\perp}
.

(3)
\mathcal{K}\subset{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
and

({}^{\perp}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))^{\perp}=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

(4)
[
18
, Proposition 4.2]
If
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
are two
classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, the following are equivalent:

(a)
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
;

(b) every exact sequence
Y\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow X

with
Y\in\mathcal{L},X\in\mathcal{K}
splits;

(c)
\mathcal{K}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
;

(d) every commutative diagram in
\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{D}
\displaystyle{{X}}

with exact columns and with
Y\in\mathcal{L},X\in\mathcal{K}

has a diagonal filler;

(e) for all exact sequences

Y\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}C\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}D

and
A\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}B\twoheadrightarrow X
in

\mathcal{A}
with
Y\in\mathcal{L},X\in\mathcal{K}
,
the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(B,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,D)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(A,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,D)}}

is a pullback.

(5) The class
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
is closed under retracts
and extensions.

(6) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is has arbitrary products.
Let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
Then
\underset{i\in I}{\prod}X_{i}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

if and only if
X_{i}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
for each
i\in I
.

(7) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has arbitrary coproducts. Let

X:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{A}
be a functor with transition
morphisms
f_{m,n}:X_{m}\to X_{n}
for
m\leqslant n
, such
that

(a)
X_{n}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
for all
n\geqslant 0
;

(b) for all
m\geqslant 0
there is
n\geqslant m
such that for all

Y\in(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
and all
\alpha:X_{n}\to Y

there is
\beta:X_{n+1}\to Y
such that
\beta f_{m,n+1}=\alpha f_{m,n}
.

Then
\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{{\rm Colim}\,}X_{n}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
X\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. We show that

{\rm Ker}\,\bar{u}\subset{\rm Im}\,\bar{v}
. Let
f:A\to X
be such
that
\bar{u}(f)=0
. Factor
v
as
v=jq
, where

q:A\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Im}\,v
and
j:{\rm Im}\,v\rightarrowtail A^{\prime\prime}
;
there is then a morphism
g:{\rm Im}\,v\to X
such that
gq=f
.
Form the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v}}

where
PO
means pushout. By assumption the bottom exact
sequence splits, therefore there is
h:A^{\prime\prime}\to X
such that
hj=g
. Then

\bar{v}(h)=hv=hjq=gp=f
. Conversely, let
X\in Ob(\mathcal{A})

and apply the functor
\mathcal{A}(-,X)
to the exact sequence

0\overset{u}{\to}A\overset{v}{\to}A^{\prime\prime}

with
A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v\in\mathcal{K}
.

(4) It is easy to see that (a)
\Leftrightarrow
(b)
\Leftrightarrow
(c).
That (d)
\Leftrightarrow
(e) follows from the fact that

{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(B,u)=\{f:A\to D,{\rm\ there\ is\ g:A\to C\ such\ that}\ vg=f\}
. For (d)
\Rightarrow
(a) we use the
commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{0}
\displaystyle{{X}}

where
Y\in\mathcal{L},X\in\mathcal{K}
. We prove (a)
\Rightarrow
(d).
Pulling back the exact sequence
(u,v)
along

we obtain
the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}}

where
PB
means pullback and the sequence
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
is exact.
It suffices to show that the commutative square in the previous
diagram has a diagonal filler
d:B\to PB
. Applying the snake diagram to

\displaystyle{{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{q}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{0}}

we obtain an exact sequence
Y\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}{\rm Coker}\,h\overset{q^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}X
.
By assumption this sequence splits, so

has a retract
r
.
Then

is a retract of
u^{\prime}
, so
v^{\prime}

has a section
s
. We have
v^{\prime}(h-si)=0
, hence there is

l:A\to Y
such that
u^{\prime}l=h-si
.
Form the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{l}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{d^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}}

where
PO
means pushout. By assumption the bottom exact
sequence splits, therefore there is
d^{\prime}:B\to Y
such that
d^{\prime}i=l
.
Define
d=s+u^{\prime}d^{\prime}
; then
d
is the required diagonal filler.

(5) We prove the closure under extensions.
Let
Y^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}Y\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y^{\prime\prime}
be an exact sequence with

Y^{\prime},Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
A\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{p}{\twoheadrightarrow}X

an exact sequence with
X\in\mathcal{K}
. Consider
the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y^{\prime\prime})}}

The two morphisms
\bar{i}
are epimorphisms by assumption.
By part 4 the diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(B,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(A,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y^{\prime\prime})}}

is a pullback. We obtain the commutative diagram with exact
rows

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(B,Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(B,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(A,Y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathcal{A}(A,u)}}

which implies that
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

(6) Let
Y\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow B
be an exact sequence with

Y\in({}^{\perp}\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
; then by (a) we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(X_{n},Y)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(X_{n},A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(X_{n},B)
for all
n\geqslant 0
. Consider the inverse system of
abelian groups
\mathcal{A}(X_{n},Y)_{n\geqslant 0}

with transition morphisms
\overline{f_{m,n}}:\mathcal{A}(X_{n},Y)\to\mathcal{A}(X_{m},Y)
; by (b) it satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition,
hence the assertion.
∎

We note that
1.2.1
(7) is a variation on a classic result of Eklof.

1.2.2.

A pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}

is a
cotorsion theory
if
\mathcal{X}={}^{\perp}\mathcal{Y}
and

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\perp}
. We often say that
\mathcal{X}
is the
left class and
\mathcal{Y}
is the right class of the cotorsion theory.

If
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}

then
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
is a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}^{op}
.

Every abelian category has the minimal cotorsion theory
(projectives, all objects) and the maximal cotorsion theory (all objects, injectives).
The pair (
Ob(\mathcal{A}),Ob(\mathcal{A}))
is a cotorsion theory if and only
if all objects of
\mathcal{A}
are projective if and only if all objects
of
\mathcal{A}
are injective if and only all short exact sequences in

\mathcal{A}
split.

1.2.3.

Every class
\mathcal{S}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}

generates
a
cotorsion theory
(^{\perp}\mathcal{S},(^{\perp}\mathcal{S})^{\perp})
and

cogenerates
a cotorsion theory
(^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}),\mathcal{S}^{\perp})
(
1.2.1
(3)).

Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, the
pair
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory generated by

\mathcal{S}
if and only if
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{K}
. Dually, the pair

({}^{\perp}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K})
is a cotorsion theory cogenerated by

\mathcal{S}
if and only if
\mathcal{S}\subset{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}

and
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{K}
.

The minimal cotorsion theory is cogenerated by any family
of projective objects. See
1.2.16
for the finite cogeneration
of the maximal cotorsion theory.

If
(^{\perp}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K})
and

(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
are cotorsion theories then (
1.2.1
(2))

(^{\perp}(\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{L}),\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{L})

is a cotorsion theory cogenerated by
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cup^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.

1.2.4.

Let
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
be two classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
We define
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L})
to consist of
objects
A
of
\mathcal{A}
for which there is an exact sequence

A\rightarrowtail K\twoheadrightarrow L
with
K\in\mathcal{K},L\in\mathcal{L}
.
We define
{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{L})
to consist of objects

A
of
\mathcal{A}
for which there is an exact sequence

K\rightarrowtail L\twoheadrightarrow A
with
K\in\mathcal{K},L\in\mathcal{L}
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is closed under extensions then

{\rm Ker}\,({\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L})\subset{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L})

and if
\mathcal{K}
is closed under extensions then

{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}\rightarrowtail{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{L}))\subset{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{L})

1.2.5.

[
2
, Chapter V, Remark 3.2(1)]

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
that
is closed under retracts. If
{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{K})=Ob(\mathcal{A})
then
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

Let
A\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
. There is an exact sequence

Y\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}X\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}A
with

X\in\mathcal{K},Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. We have then the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(A,Y)\overset{\bar{u}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathcal{A}(A,X)\overset{\bar{v}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathcal{A}(A,A)
. It follows that
A
is isomorphic to a
retract of
X
.
∎

1.2.6.

Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
and

(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})

be two cotorsion theories in
\mathcal{A}
with

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{L}
.
Then
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}\cap{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

and
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{L}\cap{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}\cap{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.
Let
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
; then the short exact sequence

0\to X=X
shows that
X\in{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
. Conversely, if

X\in\mathcal{K}\cap{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
then
there is a short exact sequence
A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow X
with
A\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

and
B\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. It follows that
X
is a
retract of
B
, hence
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
∎

1.2.7.

A class of objects of an abelian category is
left exact

if it is closed under kernels of epimorphisms between its objects and

right exact
if it is left exact in the opposite category. One says that
a cotorsion theory is
left
(
right
)
exact
if its left (right)
class is left (right) exact and
exact
if it is left and right exact.

In every abelian category the minimal and maximal cotorsion theories are exact.

1.2.8.

A commutative square diagram

\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}}

in an abelian category is
exact
if it is a pullback and a pushout.
Given a class
\mathcal{K}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, the following are
equivalent:

(1) the class
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
is right exact ;

(2) for every exact square

\displaystyle{{A_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with

is exact.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) This follows from the exact sequence

A_{1}\rightarrowtail A_{2}\oplus A_{3}\twoheadrightarrow A_{4}

and
1.2.1
(5). (1)
\Rightarrow
(3) By
1.2.1
(4)
the diagram in (3) is a pullback. Since
Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

the morphism
\bar{i}
is an epimorphism, therefore the diagram

\Rightarrow
(1) Since
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

the morphism

.
∎

1.2.9.

Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.
One says that
\mathcal{A}
has
enough

\mathcal{K}

objects

if for each
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A
with
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
One says that
\mathcal{A}
has
enough

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

objects

if
\mathcal{A}^{op}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects.

1.2.10.

, Lemma 2.6]

1.2.11.

[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
Let
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
be two classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.
Suppose that

(1)
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
is right exact;

(2)
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\subset(^{\perp}\mathcal{L})^{\perp}
;

(3)
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\subset{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.

Then
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
is right exact
and
{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})\subset{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.
If
{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
then

{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
is thick.

Proof.

We first consider three preparatory steps.

Step 1

Consider a short exact sequence
Y\rightarrowtail\bullet\twoheadrightarrow X

with
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. By (2) we have a short exact
sequence
X\rightarrowtail\bullet\twoheadrightarrow Y
. By (3) we have a short exact sequence

with
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
X^{\prime}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
The commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime}}}

where
PO
means pushout. By (1)
PO\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
, hence

\bullet\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.

We next show that
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

is left exact. Let
\bullet\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}
be an exact sequence with

A,A^{\prime\prime}\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.
By (3) we have short exact sequences
A\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow X
and

A^{\prime\prime}\rightarrowtail Y^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow X^{\prime\prime}
with
Y,Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
X,X^{\prime\prime}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{X}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}}}

where
PO,PO^{\prime}
are pushouts. Then
PO^{\prime}\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

by step 1 and
PO\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
by step 2.
We can find an exact sequence
Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow PO
with

Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
X^{\prime}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. Form the diagram

\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}}

where
PB
means pullback. It follows that
PB\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
, hence

\bullet\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.

We show that
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

is closed under extensions. Let
A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\bullet\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}
be an exact

A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
.
We can find short exact sequences

and

with
Y^{\prime},Y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

and

. Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{A^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle{{X^{\prime}}}

Then
PB\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})

by step 1,
PO\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
by step 3
and
\bullet\in{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
by step 2.
∎

1.2.12.

Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a right exact cotorsion theory in

\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{K}\subset{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{K})

then
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{K})
is right exact and

{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{K})\subset{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{K})
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of
1.2.11
with
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
∎

1.2.13.

[
22
, Lemma 12.3.3]

Let
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
be two classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
with

\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{L}
.

(1)
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
and

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{K}
if and only if

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}=^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}
.

(2) Suppose
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{K}
is left exact
and
\mathcal{A}
has enough
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
objects, meaning that for every

A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A
with
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
Then
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}=^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}
.

(2bis) Suppose
\mathcal{L}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{K}
is right exact
and
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{L}^{\perp}
objects, meaning that for every

A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is a monomorphism
A\to X
with
X\in\mathcal{L}^{\perp}
.
Then
\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(2) Using
1.2.1
(2) we have
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}
. Let
X^{\prime\prime}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
We can find an epimorphism
f:X\to X^{\prime\prime}
with
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
; then
{\rm Ker}\,f\in\mathcal{K}
and so by
1.2.1
(1bis)
X^{\prime\prime}
is a retract of
X
.
∎

1.2.14.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.
We denote by
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
the class of objects
Y\in\mathcal{Y}

such that every exact sequence
Y\rightarrowtail A\rightarrow X
with
X\in\mathcal{X}
is left split (
1.1
). We denote by
\underline{\mathcal{X}}

the class of objects
X\in\mathcal{X}
such that every exact sequence

Y\rightarrow A\twoheadrightarrow X
with
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
is right split.
The class
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}
contains the injective objects of

\mathcal{A}
and
\underline{\mathcal{X}}
contains the projective
objects.

1.2.15.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.
The class
\mathcal{Y}
is closed under quotients if and only if

\underline{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{X}
. Dually, the class
\mathcal{X}

is closed under subobjects if and only if
\underline{\mathcal{Y}}=\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{Y}
is closed under quotients. Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
and

Y\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}X
be an exact sequence
with
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
. Factor
u
as
u=ip
, where
p:Y\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Im}\,u

and
i:{\rm Im}\,u\rightarrowtail A
. Then
{\rm Im}\,u\in\mathcal{Y}
, hence the exact sequence

{\rm Im}\,u\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}A\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}X
splits, so
v
has
a section.
Conversely, let
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow Y^{\prime}

with
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
and let
A\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}X
be an exact sequence with
X\in\mathcal{X}
.
Let
g:A\to Y^{\prime}
. Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}}

where
PO
means pushout. Then we have the exact sequence

Y\to PO\twoheadrightarrow X
which is right split by assumption,
hence
Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail PO
has a retraction.
It follows that the morphism
\bar{u}:\mathcal{A}(B,Y^{\prime})\to\mathcal{A}(A,Y^{\prime})

is an epimorphism, hence
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
.
∎

1.2.16.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck category with a generator

U
. Let
Sub(U)
be the set of subobjects of
U
and let
\mathcal{G}

be a subset of
Sub(U)
satisfying the following conditions:

(a) for every commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\displaystyle{{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\displaystyle{{U}}

in
\mathcal{A}
, if
r:R\to U
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
then so does

s:S\to U
.

(b) if
s:S\to U
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
then for all morphisms
f:U\to U/S
,

k:{\rm Ker}\,f\to U
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
.

Recall
[
10
]
that every class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
that is closed under
subquotients gives rise to a subset
\mathcal{G}
of
Sub(U)
satisfying (a) and (b).
Also, when
\mathcal{A}
is the category of left modules over a ring
R
, condition
(b) means: for every left ideal
I
of
R
that belongs to
\mathcal{G}
and every

r\in R
, the left ideal
(I:r)=\{x\in R,xr\in I\}
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
.
Define now

\mathcal{G}_{t}=\{X\in Ob(\mathcal{A}),\ k:{\rm Ker}\,f\to U{\rm\ belongs\ to}\ U\ {\rm for\ all}\ f:U\to X\}

The cotorsion theory
(^{\perp}(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\perp}),\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\perp})
is cogenerated by a set.

Proof.

We put
\mathcal{S}=\{U/S,\ s:S\to U{\rm\ belongs\ to}\ \mathcal{G}\}
;
by (b) we have
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{G}_{t}

and we show that
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\perp}
.
Let
Y\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}A\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}X
be an
exact sequence with
Y\in\mathcal{S}^{\perp},X\in\mathcal{G}_{t}
.

Step 1

Let
s:S\rightarrowtail X,l:S\to A
be morphisms such
that
vl=s
. Suppose that
v
is not an isomorphism. Then there is

\alpha:X\to B
such that
\alpha s=0
and
\alpha\neq 0
. In turn,
there is
f:U\to X
such that
{\rm Im}\,f\neq 0
. Let
U\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Im}\,f\overset{r}{\rightarrowtail}X
be the image factorization of
f
.
Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Ker}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Ker}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{U/PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{S\cap{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\displaystyle{{\rm Im}\,f/S\cap{\rm Im}\,f}
\displaystyle{{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\displaystyle{{X}}

where
PB
means pullback. By (a) we have that

PB\to U
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
. Since

{\rm Im}\,f/S\cap{\rm Im}\,f\cong S\cup{\rm Im}\,f/S
, by (b) we
have that
S\cup{\rm Im}\,f/S\in\mathcal{S}
. Consider
the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{S\cap{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\displaystyle{{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{s}
\displaystyle{{S\cup{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{X}}

where
s^{\prime}
is defined by the universal property of
pushout. We obtain then the commutative solid arrows
diagram

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{S\cup{\rm Im}\,f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{l^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{X}
\displaystyle{{S\cup{\rm Im}\,f/S}}

By
1.2.1
(4) there is
l^{\prime}:S\cup{\rm Im}\,f\to A
such that

l^{\prime}i=l
and
vl^{\prime}=s^{\prime}
.

Step 2

Let
P
be the set of all ordered pairs

((S,s),l)
, where
s:S\rightarrowtail X,l:S\to A
and
vl=s
.
The set
P
is nonempty since
((0,0),0)\in P
. The relation

((S,s),l)\leqslant((S^{\prime},s^{\prime}),l^{\prime})
if there is
i:S\to S^{\prime}
such that

l^{\prime}i=l
defines an ordering on
P
. We claim that
P
is inductive
with respect to this ordering. Let
((S_{i},s_{i}),l_{i})_{i\in I}

be a totally ordered set of elements of
P
. The element

((\underset{i\in I}{\sum}S_{i},s),l)
, where

\underset{i\in I}{\bigoplus}S_{i}\twoheadrightarrow\underset{i\in I}{\sum}S_{i}\overset{s}{\rightarrowtail}X

is the image factorization of the natural morphism

\underset{i\in I}{\bigoplus}S_{i}\to X
and

l:\underset{i\in I}{\sum}S_{i}\to A
is defined by the
universal property of colimits, is an upper bound. The
the claim is proved. Therefore by Zorn’s lemma there
exists a maximal element
((S,s),l)
in
P
. If
s
is
an isomorphism then
v
has a section and we are done.
If not, from Step 1 there is an element
((S\cup{\rm Im}\,f,s^{\prime}),l^{\prime})

of
P
such that
((S,s),l)<((S\cup{\rm Im}\,f,s^{\prime}),l^{\prime})
,
contradicting the maximality of
((S,s),l)
.
∎

2.
Cotorsion theories and adjoint functors

2.1.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
be abelian categories and

\displaystyle{F:{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}:G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

an adjoint pair. Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

2.1.1.

(1) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects, meaning that
for every
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A
with
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
If
F(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
then
G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in

\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
.

(1bis) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has enough

\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp}
objects, meaning that for every
A^{\prime}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

there is a monomorphism
A^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
with
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
. If

G(\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
then

F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}
.

(2) If
G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
and

G(\mathcal{K^{\prime}})\subset\mathcal{K}
then
F({}^{\perp}\mathcal{K})\subset{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{K^{\prime}})
.

(2bis) If
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}

and
F(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
then
G(\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

(3) Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
and
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp})

are cotorsion theories,
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}

and
G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp}
.
Then
F(\mathcal{K})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow G(\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp})\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}
be an exact sequence
with
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}^{\perp}
. We can find an epimorphism
f:X\to G(B^{\prime})
with
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
Form the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{F(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\#}}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B^{\prime}}}

where
f^{\#}
is the adjoint transpose of
f
and
PB
means pullback.
Since
F(X)\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
the top exact sequence splits, therefore
there is a dotted arrow that makes the lower triangular diagram involving

f^{\#}
commute. By adjunction, there is a dotted arrow in the
diagram

\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{G(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{G(A^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{G(B^{\prime})}}

that makes the triangular diagram commute, hence the
morphism
G(A^{\prime})\to G(B^{\prime})
is an epimorphism.

(2) Let
X\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
and
X^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}
be an exact
sequence with
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
. Applying the functor
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(F(X),-)
to the previous
exact sequence and using adjunction we obtain the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(X,G(X^{\prime}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(X,G(A^{\prime}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(X,G(B^{\prime}))

By hypothesis the right morphism of the previous exact sequence is an epimorphism,
therefore
F(X)\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.

(3) This follows from parts 2 and 2bis.
∎

The previous result contains the familiar statements

(1bis) if the target category of a left adjoint has enough injective objects and the right adjoint
preserves injective objects then the left adjoint is exact, and

(2bis) if a left adjoint is exact then the right adjoint preserves injective objects.

2.1.2.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
and
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp})

are cotorsion theories.

(1) If
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
F^{-1}(\mathcal{K^{\prime}})
and

G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
then

(F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.
If
(F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp})
is cogenerated (
1.2.3
)
by
\mathcal{S}
then
(\mathcal{K}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp})
is cogenerated by
F(\mathcal{S})
.

(1bis) If
G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
G^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

and
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}
then

({}^{\perp}G^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),G^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))

is a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

(1) We shall use
1.2.3
. We put
\mathcal{S}=\{G(Y^{\prime})\}
, where
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
.
We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp}
. Let
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}

and
A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow X
be an exact sequence with
F(X)\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.
By hypothesis and assumption we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(F(X),Y^{\prime})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(F(A),Y^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(F(B),Y^{\prime})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(X,G(Y^{\prime}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(B,G(Y^{\prime}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(A,G(Y^{\prime}))

which implies that
G(Y^{\prime})\in F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp}
. We now show that

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
. Let
X\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}

and let
Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow B^{\prime}
be an exact sequence with

Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
. Applying the functor
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(F(X),-)
to the
previous exact sequence and using adjunction we obtain the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(X,G(Y^{\prime}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(X,G(A^{\prime}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(X,G(B^{\prime}))

By hypothesis and assumption on
X
the right morphism of the previous exact sequence
is an epimorphism, therefore
F(X)\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
.

Suppose now that
(F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}),F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp})
is
cogenerated by
\mathcal{S}
. Let
X\in\mathcal{S}
and

Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A^{\prime}\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}B^{\prime}
be
an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\prime\perp}
.
Form the diagram with exact rows

\displaystyle{{G(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\displaystyle{{G(Y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{G(A^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G(v)}
\displaystyle{{GF(X)}}

where
\eta_{X}
is the unit of the adjunction and
PB
means pullback.
By
2.1.1
(2bis) we have
G(Y^{\prime})\in F^{-1}(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})^{\perp}

hence the top exact sequence splits. There is then
f:X\to G(A^{\prime})
such that

G(v)f=\eta_{X}
. The adjoint transpose
f^{\#}
of
f
is a section of
v
.
∎

The following observation
[
14
]
is “often rediscovered folklore”.

2.1.3.

(1) Suppose that
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}
. For each

X\in\mathcal{K}
and
A^{\prime}\in Ob(\mathcal{A^{\prime}})
we have a natural morphism

\Theta_{X,A^{\prime}}:\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(X,G(A^{\prime}))\to\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{1}(F(X),A^{\prime})

If, moreover,
F
is full and faithful then
\Theta_{X,A^{\prime}}
is a monomorphism.

(1bis) Suppose that
G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
. Then for each

A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
and
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
we have a natural morphism

\Psi_{A,X^{\prime}}:\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathcal{A^{\prime}}}^{1}(F(A),X^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Ext}_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}(A,G(X^{\prime}))

If, moreover,
G
is full and faithful then
\Psi_{A,X^{\prime}}
is a monomorphism.

(2) Suppose that
F
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}
and

G
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
. For each
X\in\mathcal{K}
and

X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K^{\prime}}
the morphism
\Theta_{X,X^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\Psi_{X,X^{\prime}}
.

2.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}

(1\leqslant i\leqslant 3)
be a category.
A
THC–situation

[
11
]
, or adjunction of two variables,
consists of three functors

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

and natural isomorphisms

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}),X_{3})\cong\mathcal{A}_{1}(X_{1},H(X_{2},X_{3}))\cong\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},C(X_{1},X_{3}))

For each object
X_{i}
of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
we have then adjoint pairs

T(-,X_{2}):\mathcal{A}_{1}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}_{3}:H(X_{2},-)

T(X_{1},-):\mathcal{A}_{2}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}_{3}:C(X_{1},-)

C(-,X_{3}):\mathcal{A}_{1}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}:H(-,X_{3})

An
abelian THC–situation
is a THC–situation as above with

\mathcal{A}_{i}
abelian. In this case
T,H
and
C
are biadditive,
meaning additive in each variable separately.

2.2.1.

We recall
[
9
]
,
[
10
]
that every complete and cocomplete abelian
category
\mathcal{A}
fits into the natural abelian THC-situation

T:\mathrm{Ab}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A},\ \mathcal{A}(-,-):\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{Ab},\ C:\mathrm{Ab}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

where
\mathrm{Ab}
is the category of abelian groups.
The Tensor functor can be constructed as follows. Let
M
be an abelian
group and
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
. Let
\sigma_{m}:A\to\underset{M}{\bigoplus}A

and
\sigma_{m,m^{\prime}}:A\to\underset{M\times M}{\bigoplus}A
be coprojection
morphisms. We define

a(M,A):\underset{M\times M}{\bigoplus}A\to\underset{M}{\bigoplus}A

to be the unique morphism such that

a(M,A)\sigma_{m,m^{\prime}}=\sigma_{m+m^{\prime}}-(\sigma_{m}+\sigma_{m^{\prime}})

for all
m,m^{\prime}\in M
; then
T(M,A)={\rm Coker}\,a(M,A)
. The construction of
Cotensor is dual.

2.2.2.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. Let
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})

be a class of objects,
(\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{3})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{3}
. We define

T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})=\{A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2}),\ T(X_{1},A_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{3}{\rm\ for\ all}\ X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}\}

Suppose that

(1)
(\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2})
is generated
by
\mathcal{S}_{2}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{3})

is generated by
\mathcal{S}_{3}
;

(2) for each object
X_{1}
of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
the functor
T(X_{1},-)

preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{X}_{2}
and the
functor
C(X_{1},-)
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{S}_{3}
.

Then
(T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2},(T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory. A sufficient condition for the functor
C(X_{1},-)

to preserve the epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{S}_{3}
is that the category

\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough
T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}

objects, meaning that for every
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
there is an epimorphism

X_{2}\to A_{2}
with
X_{2}\in T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Proof.

We shall use
1.2.3
. We put
\mathcal{S}=\{Y_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3})\}
,
where
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2},X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1},Y_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{3}
.

Step 1

We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset(T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})^{\perp}
.
Let
A_{2}\rightarrowtail B_{2}\twoheadrightarrow X_{2}
be an exact sequence with

X_{2}\in T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
Let
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
; then we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},Y_{2})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(B_{2},Y_{2})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(A_{2},Y_{2})
since
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}_{2}

and
X_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
. Let
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1},Y_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{3}
.
By assumption we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},A_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},B_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},X_{2})}}

and therefore the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},B_{2}),Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},A_{2}),Y_{3})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(B_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(A_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))

Step 2

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
Let
X_{2}\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}
. Since

\mathcal{S}_{2}\subset\mathcal{S}
we have (
1.2.1
(2))

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{2}=\mathcal{X}_{2}
, hence
it suffices to show that
T(X_{1},X_{2})\in^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{3}
whenever

X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Let
Y_{3}\rightarrowtail A_{3}\twoheadrightarrow B_{3}

be an exact sequence with
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{3}
. By assumption we have
the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},A_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},B_{3})}}

and therefore by the definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},C(X_{1},A_{3})))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2},C(X_{1},B_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}),A_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}),B_{3})

hence
T(X_{1},X_{2})\in^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{3}
and

(T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2},(T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory.
Let now
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
Y_{3}\rightarrowtail A_{3}\twoheadrightarrow B_{3}

be an exact sequence with
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{3}
. Applying
C(X_{1},-)
to the sequence
we obtain the exact sequence
C(X_{1},Y_{3})\rightarrowtail C(X_{1},A_{3})\rightarrow C(X_{1},B_{3})
. Let
f:X_{2}^{\prime}\to C(X_{1},B_{3})
be an epimorphism
with
X_{2}^{\prime}\in T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
Applying
\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime}),-)
to
Y_{3}\rightarrowtail A_{3}\twoheadrightarrow B_{3}

we obtain the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime}),A_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime}),B_{3})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime},C(X_{1},A_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}^{\prime},C(X_{1},B_{3}))

Therefore there is
g:X_{2}^{\prime}\to C(X_{1},A_{3})
such that the diagram

\displaystyle{{X_{2}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},A_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},B_{3})}}

commutes, hence its horizontal arrow is an epimorphism.
∎

2.2.3.

Under the assumptions
2.2.2
, if
\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{X}_{3}

are left exact (
1.2.7
) then so is

T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
X_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail X_{2}\twoheadrightarrow X_{2}^{\prime\prime}
be an exact sequence with

X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in T(\mathcal{K}_{1},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{X}_{3})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.
Then
X_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
since
\mathcal{X}_{2}
is left exact.
Let
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
; then we have by
2.2.2
(2) the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},X_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime\prime})}}

It follows that
T(X_{1},X_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}_{3}
since
\mathcal{X}_{3}
is left exact.
∎

2.2.4.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. Let
\mathcal{K}_{2}\subset Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})

be a class of objects,
(\mathcal{X}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{3})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{3}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. We define

H(\mathcal{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\{A_{3}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{3}),\ H(K_{2},A_{3})\in\mathcal{Y}_{1}{\rm\ for\ all}\ K_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}\}

Suppose that

(1)
(\mathcal{X}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{3})
is cogenerated by
\mathcal{S}_{3}
and

(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})
is cogenerated by
\mathcal{S}_{1}
;

(2) for each object
K_{2}
of
\mathcal{K}_{2}
the functor
H(K_{2},-)
preserves
epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
;

(3) the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has enough

H(\mathcal{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{3}
objects,
meaning that for every
A_{3}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{3})
there is a monomorphism

A_{3}\to Y_{3}
with
Y_{3}\in H(\mathcal{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{3}
.

Then
(^{\perp}(H(\mathcal{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{3}),H(\mathcal{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{Y}_{3})
is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
2.2.2
, with
\mathcal{S}=\{X_{3},T(X_{1},K_{2})\}
,
where
X_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{3},X_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}
.
∎

2.2.5.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. Let
\mathcal{K}_{3}\subset Ob(\mathcal{A}_{3})

be a class of objects,
(\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})

a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. We define

H(-,\mathcal{K}_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})=\{A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2}),\ H(A_{2},K_{3})\in\mathcal{Y}_{1}{\rm\ for\ all}\ K_{3}\in\mathcal{K}_{3}\}

Suppose that

(1)
(\mathcal{X}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2})
is generated by
\mathcal{S}_{2}
and

(\mathcal{X}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})
is cogenerated by
\mathcal{S}_{1}
;

(2) for each object
K_{3}
of
\mathcal{K}_{3}
the functor
H(-,K_{3})
preserves
epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{X}_{2}
;

(3) the category
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough

H(-,\mathcal{K}_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
objects,
meaning that for every
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
there is an epimorphism

X_{2}\to A_{2}
with
X_{2}\in H(-,\mathcal{K}_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2}
.

Then
(H(-,\mathcal{K}_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2},(H(-,\mathcal{K}_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{Y}_{1})\cap\mathcal{X}_{2})^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
2.2.2
, with
\mathcal{S}=\{Y_{2},C(X_{1},K_{3})\}
,
where
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2},X_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1},K_{3}\in\mathcal{K}_{3}
.
∎

3.
The flat cotorsion theory associated to an abelian tensor-hom-cotensor situation

3.1.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. An object
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
) of

\mathcal{A}_{2}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{1}
) is called
flat
if

T(-,P_{2})
(resp.
T(P_{1},-)
) preserves monomorphisms.
We denote by
\mathcal{F}
the class of flat objects of
\mathcal{A}_{2}

and by
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
the class of flat objects of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.1.1.

An object
P_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is flat if and only if
for every exact sequence

A_{1}^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A_{1}\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A_{1}^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
the sequence

\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(v,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime\prime}_{1},P_{2})}}

is exact.

Proof.

Suppose
P_{2}
is flat. Consider the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{p}
\displaystyle{{A_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\scriptstyle{q}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{A^{\prime\prime}_{1}/{\rm Im}\,v}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,u}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}

Since
T(-,P_{2})
is a left adjoint we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(q,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T({\rm Im}\,v,P_{2})}}

By assumption we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T({\rm Im}\,v,P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(j,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime\prime}_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(p^{\prime\prime},P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v,P_{2})}}

By splicing we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(jq,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T({\rm Im}\,v,P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(p^{\prime\prime},P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v,P_{2})}}

hence the claim. Conversely, one uses the exact sequence

0\overset{u}{\to}A\overset{v}{\to}B
.
∎

3.1.2.

(Lambek) (1) Let
P_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

(i) If
P_{2}
is flat and
E_{3}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}_{3}

then
H(P_{2},E_{3})
is injective.

(ii) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has a set
(E_{i})_{i\in I}

of injective cogenerators. If
H(P_{2},E_{i})
is injective for each
i\in I

then
P_{2}
is flat.

(2) Let
P_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
.

(i) If
P_{1}
is flat and
E_{3}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}_{3}

then
C(P_{1},E_{3})
is injective.

(ii) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has a set
(E_{i})_{i\in I}

of injective cogenerators. If
C(P_{1},E_{i})
is injective for each
i\in I

then
P_{1}
is flat.

Proof.

(1) Let
A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}
be
an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. We prove (i).
Since
E_{3}
is injective we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{1}(C_{1},H(P_{2},E_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{1}(B_{1},H(P_{2},E_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(A_{2},E_{3}))

showing that
H(P_{2},E_{3})
is injective. We prove (ii).
The sequence

T(A_{1},P_{2})\to T(B_{1},P_{2})\twoheadrightarrow T(C_{1},P_{2})

is short exact if

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},P_{2}),E_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}),E_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}),E_{i})

is short exact for each
i\in I
(
1.1.1
).
By adjunction and assumption the sequence
(
1
) is short exact. The proof of part 2 is similar.
∎

3.1.3.

Let
E_{3}
be an injective object of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

(1) If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has enough flat objects, meaning that for each

A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
there is an epimorphism
P_{1}\to A_{1}

with
P_{1}
flat, then
H(-,E_{3})
is exact.

(2) If
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough flat objects, meaning that for each

A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
there is an epimorphism
P_{2}\to A_{2}

with
P_{2}
flat, then
C(-,E_{3})
is exact.

Proof.

(1) We use
2.1.1
(1) with
F=C(-,E_{3}),\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{F}

and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
equal to the class of injective objects of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
It suffices to show that if
P_{1}
is a flat object of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
then

C(P_{1},E_{3})
is injective. But this is
3.1.2
(2).
The proof of part 2 is similar.
∎

3.1.4.

Suppose that

(1) the category
\mathcal{A}_{i}

(1\leqslant i\leqslant 2)
has enough
flat objects, meaning that for each
A_{i}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{i})
there
is an epimorphism
P_{i}\to A_{i}
with
P_{i}
flat;

(2) the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has enough injective objects.

Then
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory. The
objects of
\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
are usually called cotorsion objects.

Proof.

We shall use
1.2.3
. We put
\mathcal{S}=\{C(A_{1},E_{3})\}
, where

A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
E_{3}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

Step 1

We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.
Let
A_{2}\rightarrowtail B_{2}\twoheadrightarrow P_{2}
be
an exact sequence with
P_{2}
flat, let
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
E_{3}
an
injective object of
\mathcal{A}_{3}
. Applying the functor
\mathcal{A}_{2}(-,C(A_{1},E_{3}))

to the previous exact sequence and using adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(P_{2},E_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(B_{2},E_{3}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(A_{2},E_{3}))

Since the functor
T(-,P_{2})
is exact, its right adjoint
H(P_{2},-)
preserves injective objects
(
2.1.1
(2bis)), hence
H(P_{2},E_{3})
is injective. Since
H(-,E_{3})
is exact
(
3.1.3
) the right arrow in the previous exact sequence is an epimorphism,
showing that
C(A_{1},E_{3})\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.

Step 2

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{F}
.
Let
P_{2}\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}
and
A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}

be an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. There is a monomorphism
T(A_{1},P_{2})\to E_{3}

with
E_{3}
injective. Since
C(-,E_{3})
is exact (
3.1.3
) we have the exact sequence

C(C_{1},E_{3})\rightarrowtail C(B_{1},E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow C(A_{1},E_{3})

hence by the assumption on
P_{2}
the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(C_{1},E_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(B_{1},E_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(A_{1},E_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})

This implies that
T(A_{1},P_{2})\to T(B_{1},P_{2})
is a monomorphism.
∎

3.1.5.

The class
\mathcal{F}
is left exact (
1.2.7
).

Proof.

Let
P_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail P_{2}\twoheadrightarrow P_{2}^{\prime\prime}

be an exact sequence with
P_{2},P_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
and

A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}

an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. There is a monomorphism

T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime})\to E_{3}
with
E_{3}
injective.
By
3.1.3
and
2.1.1
(2bis) we have the exact sequence

H(P_{2}^{\prime\prime},E_{3})\rightarrowtail H(P_{2},E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow H(P_{2}^{\prime},E_{3})

with
H(P_{2}^{\prime\prime},E_{3}),H(P_{2},E_{3})
injective. Then

H(P_{2}^{\prime},E_{3})
is injective and therefore we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{1}(C_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},E_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{1}(B_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},E_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},E_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),E_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),E_{3})

This implies that
T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime})\to T(B_{1},P^{\prime}_{2})
is a monomorphism.
∎

3.1.6.

(1) For every
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
the functor
T(A_{1},-)

preserves the monomorphisms with flat cokernel.

(2) Let
P_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
. Suppose that for every

A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
the functor
T(A_{1},-)
preserves
the monomorphisms with cokernel
P_{2}
. Then
P_{2}
is flat.

(3) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has a set
(E_{i})_{i\in I}

of injective cogenerators and that the class of injective objects of

\mathcal{A}_{1}
is cogenerated by a set
\mathcal{S}_{1}
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is generated by the set

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{C(X_{1},E_{i})\}
, where
X_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
.

Proof.

(1) This is a consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{S}
and

2.1.1
(1bis) applied to the adjunction
(T(A_{1},-),C(A_{1},-))

and the classes
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{F}
,

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=Ob(\mathcal{A}_{3})
.

(2) Let
P_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
and

A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}

an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
There is a monomorphism

f:T(A_{1},P_{2})\to E_{3}
with
E_{3}
injective. Since
C(-,E_{3})

is exact we have the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{C(C_{1},E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\flat}}
\displaystyle{{C(C_{1},E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(B_{1},E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(A_{1},E_{3})}}

where
f^{\flat}
is the adjoint of
f
and
PB
means pullback.
By the first assumption we have the exact sequence

T(C_{1},C(C_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail T(C_{1},PB)\twoheadrightarrow T(C_{1},P_{2})
.
Since
E_{3}
is injective we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},P_{2}),E_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},PB),E_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(C_{1},C(C_{1},E_{3})),E_{3})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(C_{1},E_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(PB,C(C_{1},E_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(C(C_{1},E_{3}),C(C_{1},E_{3}))

This implies that the map
PB\to P_{2}
in the previous commutative diagram has a section,
therefore there is a dotted arrow that makes commutative the lower triangular diagram
containing
f^{\flat}
. An adjunction argument implies then
that
T(A_{1},P_{2})\to T(B_{1},P_{2})
is a monomorphism.

(3) From the proof of
3.1.4
we have
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
,
so
\mathcal{F}=^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Let
P_{2}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. By
3.1.2
it suffices to show that
H(P_{2},E_{i})

is injective. Let
A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}
be
an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
. By
3.1.3

we have the exact sequence
C(X_{1},E_{i})\rightarrowtail C(B_{1},E_{i})\twoheadrightarrow C(A_{1},E_{i})
. Applying
\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},-)
to it and using an adjunction
argument we obtain that
H(P_{2},E_{i})
is injective.
∎

3.1.7.

For a morphism
u_{1}:A_{1}\to B_{1}
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and a morphism

u_{2}:A_{2}\to B_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, we denote by
u_{1}Tu_{2}

the natural morphism

T(A_{1},B_{2})\bigcup_{T(A_{1},A_{2})}T(B_{1},A_{2})\to T(B_{1},B_{2})

If
u_{1},u_{2}
are monomorphisms with flat cokernel then

u_{1}Tu_{2}
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

We shall need the fact that
3.1.1
–
3.1.6
also hold, with suitable
modifications in proofs, for the class of flat objects of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.
Using this and the exact sequence

A_{i}\overset{u_{i}}{\rightarrowtail}B_{i}\twoheadrightarrow P_{i}
(
1\leqslant i\leqslant 2
) we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},A_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},B_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},A_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},B_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(P_{1},B_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
