\displaystyle{{T(P_{1},P_{2})}}

The arrows that form of the upper left square diagram are
monomorphisms by
3.1.6
(1). It follows then from

[
3
, Lemma 1.10.5 and Proposition 1.7.4]
that

T(A_{1},B_{2})\bigcup_{T(A_{1},A_{2})}T(B_{1},A_{2})\cong T(B_{1},A_{2})\cup T(A_{1},B_{2})

hence
u_{1}Tu_{2}
is a monomorphism.
∎

Here is another proof that
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory.

3.1.8.

Suppose that

(1) the category
\mathcal{A}_{i}

(1\leqslant i\leqslant 2)
has
enough flat objects, meaning that for each
A_{i}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{i})

there is an epimorphism
P_{i}\to A_{i}
with
P_{i}
flat;

(2) the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has an injective cogenerator
E_{3}
.

Then
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

Consider the adjoint pair

C(-,E_{3}):\mathcal{A}_{1}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}:H(-,E_{3})

By
3.1.3
both functors are exact. By
2.1.2
(1bis) applied to

\mathcal{K}=Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
we then obtain a cotorsion theory in

\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}
whose right class is the class of flat objects by
3.1.2
.
∎

3.2.

We shall repeat most of
3.1
replacing “preserves monomorphisms” by
“preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in a given class”. Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. Let
(\mathcal{X}_{3},\mathcal{Y}_{3})

be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}_{3}
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{1}\subset Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})

be a class of objects. An object
P_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
called
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat
if
T(-,P_{2})

preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}_{1}
. We denote by

\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
the class of
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat objects.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{2}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
that contains

\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
. An object
P_{1}
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is called

\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat
if
T(P_{1},-)
preserves monomorphisms with
cokernel in
\mathcal{K}_{2}
. We denote by

\mathcal{K}_{2}-\mathcal{F}
the class of
\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat objects.

3.2.1.

An object
P_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat if and only if
for every exact sequence

A_{1}^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A_{1}\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A_{1}^{\prime\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with
A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
the sequence

\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(v,P_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime\prime}_{1},P_{2})}}

is exact.

Proof.

If
P_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat the proof is the same as the first part of the proof of

3.1.1
. Conversely, use the exact sequence

0\overset{u}{\to}A\overset{v}{\to}B\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}
with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
.
∎

3.2.2.

Suppose that the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has a set
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
of
cogenerators that belong to
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
. Moreover, suppose that

T(\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
.
Then an object
P_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat if
and only if
H(P_{2},Y_{i})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\perp}
for each
i\in I
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as for
3.1.2
. Note that it also shows
that
H(P_{2},Y_{3})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\perp}
for all
P_{2}

\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat
and all
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
.
∎

3.2.3.

Suppose that

(1)
T(\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
;

(2) the category
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat objects,
meaning that for each
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
there is an
epimorphism
P_{2}\to A_{2}
with
P_{2}

\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat;

(3)
T(\mathcal{K}_{2}-\mathcal{F},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
;

(4) the category
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has enough
\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat objects,
meaning that for each
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
there is an
epimorphism
P_{1}\to A_{1}
with
P_{1}

\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat.

Then for every
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
the functor
C(-,Y_{3})

preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
the functor
H(-,Y_{3})
preserves epimorphisms
with kernel in
\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Let
A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}
be an
exact sequence with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Consider the solid arrows
diagram

\displaystyle{{P_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(B_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(A_{1},Y_{3})}}

with
P_{2}
flat. By adjunction we have a commutative solid arrow diagram

\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\#}}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(X_{1},P_{2})}}

where
f^{\#}
is the adjoint of
f
and
PO
means pushout. The
bottom exact sequence splits by (1), so there is a dotted arrow
that makes commutative the triangular diagram containing
f^{\#}
. By adjunction
there is a dotted arrow in the first diagram that makes commutative the triangular
diagram containing
f
. The argument for
H(-,Y_{3})
is dual, using (3) and (4).
∎

3.2.4.

Suppose that

(1)
T(\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
;

(2) the category
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat objects,
meaning that for each
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
there is an epimorphism

P_{2}\to A_{2}
with
P_{2}

\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat;

(3)
T(\mathcal{K}_{2}-\mathcal{F},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
;

(4) the category
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has enough
\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat objects,
meaning that for each
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
there is an epimorphism

P_{1}\to A_{1}
with
P_{1}

\mathcal{K}_{2}-
flat.

(5) the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has enough
\mathcal{Y}_{3}
objects.

Then
(\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F},\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

We shall use
1.2.3
. We put
\mathcal{S}=\{C(X_{1},Y_{3})\}
, where

X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1},Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
.

Step 1

We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.
Let
A_{2}\rightarrowtail B_{2}\twoheadrightarrow P_{2}
be
an exact sequence with
P_{2}

\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat and let

X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
,
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
.
By
3.2.3
we have the exact sequence

H(P_{2},Y_{3})\rightarrowtail H(B_{2},Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow H(A_{2},Y_{3})
.
By the proof of
3.2.2

we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{1}(X_{1},H(P_{2},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{1}(X_{1},H(B_{2},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}(X_{1},H(A_{2},Y_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(B_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(A_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))

hence
C(X_{1},Y_{3})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.

Step 2

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
.
Let
P_{2}\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}
and
A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}

be an exact sequence with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
. There is a monomorphism

T(A_{1},P_{2})\to Y_{3}
with
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
. By
3.2.3

we have the exact sequence
C(X_{1},Y_{3})\rightarrowtail C(B_{1},Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow C(A_{1},Y_{3})

hence by the assumption on
P_{2}
the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(B_{1},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(A_{1},Y_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},P_{2}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}),Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}),Y_{3})

This implies that
T(A_{1},P_{2})\to T(B_{1},P_{2})
is a monomorphism.
∎

3.2.5.

If
\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\perp}
is left exact
then
\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
is left exact.

Proof.

Let
P_{2}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail P_{2}\twoheadrightarrow P_{2}^{\prime\prime}

be an exact sequence with
P_{2},P_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
and

A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}

an exact sequence with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
. There is a monomorphism

T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime})\to Y_{3}
with
Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
.
By
3.2.3
and the proof of
3.2.2
we have the exact sequence

H(P_{2}^{\prime\prime},Y_{3})\rightarrowtail H(P_{2},Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow H(P_{2}^{\prime},Y_{3})

with
H(P_{2}^{\prime\prime},Y_{3}),H(P_{2},Y_{3})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\perp}
. Then

H(P_{2}^{\prime},Y_{3})\in\mathcal{K}_{1}^{\perp}
and therefore we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{1}(X_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{1}(B_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(P_{2}^{\prime},Y_{3}))

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(B_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime}),Y_{3})

This implies that
T(A_{1},P_{2}^{\prime})\to T(B_{1},P_{2}^{\prime})
is a monomorphism.
∎

3.2.6.

(1) For every
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
the functor
T(X_{1},-)
preserves
the monomorphisms with cokernel in
\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
.

(2) Let
P_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}
. Suppose that for every

X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
the functor
T(X_{1},-)
preserves the
monomorphisms with cokernel
P_{2}
. If

T(\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2})\subset\mathcal{X}_{3}
then

P_{2}
is
\mathcal{K}_{1}-
flat.

Proof.

(1) This is a consequence of the definition of
\mathcal{S}
and

2.1.1
(1bis) applied to the adjunction
(T(X_{1},-),C(X_{1},-))

and the classes
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}-\mathcal{F}
,

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}_{3}
.

(2) Let
P_{2}\in\mathcal{K}_{2}
and

A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X_{1}

be an exact sequence with
X_{1}\in\mathcal{K}_{1}
.
There is a monomorphism
f:T(A_{1},P_{2})\to Y_{3}
with

Y_{3}\in\mathcal{Y}_{3}
. By
3.2.3
we have the exact sequence

C(X_{1},Y_{3})\rightarrowtail C(B_{1},Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow C(A_{1},Y_{3})
.
Form the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\flat}}
\displaystyle{{C(X_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(B_{1},Y_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C(A_{1},Y_{3})}}

where
f^{\flat}
is the adjoint of
f
and
PB
means pullback.
By the first assumption we have the exact sequence

T(X_{1},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail T(X_{1},PB)\twoheadrightarrow T(X_{1},P_{2})
.
By the second assumption we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},P_{2}),Y_{3})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},PB),Y_{3})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(X_{1},C(X_{1},Y_{3})),Y_{3})

By adjunction we have the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{2},C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}_{2}(PB,C(X_{1},Y_{3}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{2}(C(X_{1},Y_{3}),C(X_{1},Y_{3}))

This implies that the map
PB\to P_{2}
in the previous commutative diagram has a section,
therefore there is a dotted arrow that makes commutative the lower triangular
containing
f^{\flat}
. An adjunction argument implies then that

T(A_{1},P_{2})\to T(B_{1},P_{2})
is a monomorphism.
∎

3.2.7.

In
3.2
we take
\mathcal{K}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{1}
, the class of
injective objects of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}=Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})

and
\mathcal{X}_{3}=Ob(\mathcal{A}_{3})
. We assume that

(1) the category
\mathcal{A}_{i}

(1\leqslant i\leqslant 2)
has enough flat objects,
meaning that for each
A_{i}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{i})
there is an epimorphism

P_{i}\to A_{i}
with
P_{i}
flat;

(2) the category
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has enough injective objects.

Then by
3.2.4

(\mathcal{I}_{1}-\mathcal{F},\mathcal{I}_{1}-\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

3.3.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and

T:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}

a THC–situation. We say that a class
\mathcal{K}
of objects of

\mathcal{A}
is
T
-
closed
if if
T(X,X^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}

whenever
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
. We say that a cotorsion theory

(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
in
\mathcal{A}
is
T
-closed if

\mathcal{X}
is
T
-closed.

3.3.1.

A sufficient condition for
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})

to be
T
-closed is to have, for all
X,Y,Z\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
, a
natural morphism

T(X,T(Y,Z))\to T(T(X,Y),Z)

that is an isomorphism.

3.3.2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{F}
and
that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{X}
objects.
The following are equivalent:

(1)
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is
T
-closed;

(2)
C(X,Y)\in\mathcal{Y}
for all
X\in\mathcal{X},Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Let
X\in\mathcal{X}
. The claim follows from

2.1.1
(2bis) applied to the adjunction
(T(X,-),C(X,-))
and

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{X}
, together with the assumption that

\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{F}
.
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Let
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
.
The claim follows from
2.1.1
(2) applied to the adjunction

(T(X^{\prime},-),C(X^{\prime},-))
and
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Y}
,
together with the assumption that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{X}

objects.
∎

3.4.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{3},\ H:\mathcal{A}_{2}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{1},\ C:\mathcal{A}_{1}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}_{3}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}

be an abelian THC–situation. An object
P_{2}
(resp.
P_{1}
) of

\mathcal{A}_{2}
( resp.
\mathcal{A}_{1}
)
is called
faithfully flat
if
T(-,P_{2})
(resp.
T(P_{1},-)
)
preserves monomorphisms and is faithful.
A monomorphism
s:B_{2}\to A_{2}
of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is
pure

if
T(A_{1},s)
is a monomorphism for all
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
.

3.4.1.

Let
P_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
. The following are equivalent:

(1)
P_{2}
is faithfully flat;

(2) a sequence
(u_{1},v_{1})
in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is exact if and
only if the sequence
(T(u_{1},P_{2}),T(v_{1},P_{2}))
is exact.

Proof.

The proof is standard, using
3.1.1
.
∎

3.4.2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has an injective cogenerator
E_{3}
and that

\mathcal{A}_{1}
has a faithfully flat object
P_{1}
. Suppose moreover that

\mathcal{A}_{2}
is complete. Then
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough injectives.

Proof.

The object
C(P_{1},E_{3})
is injective since
C(P_{1},-)
has a left adjoint
that is exact (
2.1.1
(2bis)). The object
C(P_{1},E_{3})
is a
cogenerator since
C(P_{1},-)
is the right adjoint of a faithful functor,
so it preserves cogenerators. Let
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
.
Consider the natural morphism

c_{A_{2}}:A_{2}\to\underset{\mathcal{A}_{2}(A_{2},C(P_{1},E_{3}))}{\prod}C(P_{1},E_{3})

We have
\mathcal{A}_{2}(A_{2},C(P_{1},E_{3}))\cong\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(P_{1},A_{2}),E_{3})\neq 0

if
A_{2}\neq 0
since
T(P_{1},-)
and
\mathcal{A}_{3}(-,E_{3})

are faithful. The target of
c_{A_{2}}
is injective and
c_{A_{2}}
is
a monomorphism since
C(P_{1},E_{3})
is a cogenerator.
∎

If
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is not complete, the following is a non-functorial
variant of
3.4.2
.

3.4.3.

We first show that
H(-,E_{3})
is faithful. Let
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
,

A_{2}\neq 0
. Suppose that
H(A_{2},E_{3})=0
. Then

0=\mathcal{A}_{1}(P_{1},H(A_{2},E_{3}))\cong\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(P_{1},A_{2}),E_{3})
, which implies that

T(P_{1},A_{2})=0
since
\mathcal{A}_{3}(-,E_{3})
is faithful.
But
T(P_{1},A_{2})\neq 0
by the assumption on
P_{1}
,
contradiction. Therefore
H(-,E_{3})
is faithful. It follows that for
each
A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
the natural morphism

\epsilon_{A_{2}}:A_{2}\to C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3})
is a monomorphism.
Let
P_{1}^{\prime}\to H(A_{2},E_{3})
be an epimorphism with
P_{1}^{\prime}
flat.
Then
C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3})\to C(P_{1}^{\prime},E_{3})
is a monomorphism
since
C(-,E_{3})
is a left adjoint. The object
C(P_{1}^{\prime},E_{3})
is
injective since
C(P_{1}^{\prime},-)
has a left adjoint that is exact (
2.1.1
(2bis)).
The composite monomorphism

A_{2}\to C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3})\to C(P_{1}^{\prime},E_{3})

shows that
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has enough injectives.

3.4.4.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has a faithfully flat
object
P_{1}
and that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has a set
(E^{i}_{3})_{i\in I}

of injective cogenerators. Let
(u_{2},v_{2})
be a sequence in

\mathcal{A}_{2}
. If the sequence
(H(v_{2},E^{i}_{3}),H(u_{2},E^{i}_{3}))

is exact and
{\rm Ker}\,H(v_{2},E^{i}_{3})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp}
for all
i\in I

then
(u_{2},v_{2})
is exact.

Proof.

Using
3.4.1
,
1.1.1
and adjunction we have
the equivalences

(u_{2},v_{2})\ {\rm exact}\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(P_{1},v_{2}),E^{i}_{3}),\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(P_{1},u_{2}),E^{i}_{3})))\ {\rm exact\ for\ all}\ i\in I

\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{1}(P_{1},H(v_{2},E^{i}_{3})),\mathcal{A}_{1}(P_{1},H(u_{2},E^{i}_{3}))\ {\rm exact\ for\ all}\ i\in I

The last sequence is exact by
1.2.1
(1bis).
∎

3.4.5.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has a faithfully flat
object
P_{1}
and that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has enough injectives.
Let
(u_{2},v_{2})
be a semi-exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
If the sequence
(H(v_{2},E_{3}),H(u_{2},E_{3}))

is exact for all injective objects
E_{3}
of
\mathcal{A}_{3}

and
{\rm Ker}\,H(v_{2},E^{i}_{3})\in\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp}

for all
i\in I
then
(u_{2},v_{2})
is exact.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the proof of
3.4.4
,
using
1.1.2
instead of
1.1.1
.
∎

3.4.6.

Consider in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
the commutative diagram
with top row exact

\displaystyle{{A_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{B_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{2}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{C_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}_{2}}
\displaystyle{{B^{\prime}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}_{2}}
\displaystyle{{C^{\prime}_{2}}}

(1) If
u^{\prime}_{2},f
and
h
are pure monomorphisms then so is
g
.

(2) If
(u^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{2})
is exact,
f,v^{\prime}_{2}
are epimorphisms
and
g
is a pure monomorphism then
h
is a pure monomorphism.

Proof.

Let
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})
. For part 1 we apply
T(A_{1},-)

to the diagram in the statement; we obtain the commutative diagram
with top row exact

\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},A_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},B_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},C_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},A^{\prime}_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},B^{\prime}_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})}}

Now the assertion follows from the kernel exact sequence. Part 2 is similar.
∎

3.4.7.

Let
S_{2}\to P_{2}
be a monomorphism in
\mathcal{A}_{2}

with
P_{2}
flat.

(1) The monomorphism
S_{2}\to P_{2}
is pure if and only if
P_{2}/S_{2}
is flat.

(2) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{A}_{3}
are
Grothendieck categories and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has a flat generator
U_{1}
.
The monomorphism
S_{2}\to P_{2}
is pure if and only if for all monomorphisms

S_{1}\to U_{1}
the natural morphism
f:T(S_{1},S_{2})\to T(U_{1},S_{2})\cap T(S_{1},P_{2})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1) If
P_{2}/S_{2}
is flat then we use
3.1.6
(1). Conversely, let

A_{1}\rightarrowtail B_{1}\twoheadrightarrow C_{1}
be an exact
sequence in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. The assertion follows upon applying
the snake diagram to

\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(C_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(B_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(C_{1},P_{2})}}

(2) The morphism
f:T(S_{1},S_{2})\to T(U_{1},S_{2})\cap T(S_{1},P_{2})
is obtained from the universal property
of pullback applied to the diagram

\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},P_{2})}}

Suppose
S_{2}\to P_{2}
is pure. Then
P_{2}/S_{2}
is flat by
part 1 and it follows that the previous diagram is a pullback, hence

f
is an isomorphism. Conversely, consider the commutative diagram
with exact rows

\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},P_{2}/S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},S_{2})\cup T(S_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(S_{1},P_{2}/S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},S_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},P_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(U_{1},P_{2}/S_{2})}}

The lower right square diagram is a pushout, hence

T(S_{1},P_{2}/S_{2})\to T(U_{1},P_{2}/S_{2})
is a monomorphism.
Using
3.1.2
and
1.2.16
with
\mathcal{G}=Sub(U_{1})

we then obtain that
P_{2}/S_{2}
is flat, hence
S_{2}\to P_{2}
is pure
by part 1.
∎

3.4.8.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{3}
has an injective cogenerator
E_{3}

and that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has a faithfully flat object
P_{1}
. For all

A_{2}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{2})
the monomorphism

\epsilon_{A_{2}}:A_{2}\to C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3})
(
3.4.3
)
is pure.

Proof.

Since
(C(-,E_{3}),H(-,E_{3}))
is an adjoint pair the composite

\displaystyle{{H(A_{2},E_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3}),E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\epsilon_{A_{2}},E_{3})}
\displaystyle{{H(A_{2},E_{3})}}

is the identity. Therefore for all
A_{1}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}_{1})

the morphism
\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(\epsilon_{A_{2}},E_{3}))

is an epimorphism. Using adjunctions we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3})),E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},\epsilon_{A_{2}}),E_{3})}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},A_{2}),E_{3})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(C(H(A_{2},E_{3}),E_{3}),E_{3}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(\epsilon_{A_{2}},E_{3}))}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}_{1}(A_{1},H(A_{2},E_{3}))}}

in which the vertical arrows are isomorphisms. It follows that

\mathcal{A}_{3}(T(A_{1},\epsilon_{A_{2}}),E_{3})
is an
epimorphism, hence (
1.1.1
(2))
T(A_{1},\epsilon_{A_{2}})

is a monomorphism.
∎

4.
Recollections on complexes

In this section we recall some facts about (chain) complexes in abelian categories;
for more details we refer to
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
20
]
,
[
22
]
.
We adopt Bourbaki’s notations, terminology and sign conventions, except that

homotopisme
is translated as homotopy equivalence and

homologisme
as quasi-isomorphism.

4.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. We denote by
Gr(\mathcal{A})

the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathcal{A}
and by

Ch(\mathcal{A})
the category of complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
p
be an integer. For a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded object
\mathbf{Z}
in

\mathcal{A}
we define the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded object
\mathbf{Z}(p)

as
\mathbf{Z}(p)_{n}=\mathbf{Z}_{n+p}
, and if
\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}

are
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathcal{A}
and

u:\mathbf{Z}\to\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
objects we define
u(p)_{n}=u_{n+p}
. For a complex
(\mathbf{C},d)
in

\mathcal{A}
we denote by
(\mathbf{C}(p),d(p))
the
p
-th
translate of
(\mathbf{C},d)
, where
d(p)_{n}=(-1)^{p}d_{n+p}
.
The differential
d
is a morphism
d:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}(-1)
and
a morphism
u:(\mathbf{C},d)\to(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is a morphism of

\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that

u(-1)d=d^{\prime}u
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},d)
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. We denote by

d^{\#}:\mathbf{C}(1)\to\mathbf{C}
the adjoint transpose of
d
,
by
B(\mathbf{C})
the complex of boundaries of
\mathbf{C}
and by

Z(\mathbf{C})
the complex of cycles. There are natural morphisms

\delta(1):\mathbf{C}(1)\to B(\mathbf{C}),i:B(\mathbf{C})\to Z(\mathbf{C}),j:Z(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C},p:Z(\mathbf{C})\to H(\mathbf{C})
and
exact sequences

Z(\mathbf{C})\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{C}\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}B(\mathbf{C})(-1)\ \ \ \ H(\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}/B(\mathbf{C})\overset{\bar{\delta}}{\twoheadrightarrow}B(\mathbf{C})(-1)

For each integer
n
we denote by
(-)_{n}:Ch(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}

the evaluation at
n
functor and by
D^{n}:\mathcal{A}\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

the
n
th disk functor; the functor
(-)_{n}
is left adjoint to
D^{n+1}

and right adjoint to
D^{n}
.

The complex of cycles is a functor
Z:Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

that has a left adjoint
-/B
which sends a complex
\mathbf{C}
to the
quotient object
\mathbf{C}/B(\mathbf{C})
. The functor
Z
preserves
epimorphisms with exact kernel and the functor
-/B
preserves
monomorphisms with exact cokernel. We have
-/BD^{n}=ZD^{n+1}

and the two composites are the
n
th sphere functor
S^{n}
, therefore

S^{n}
has
(-)_{n}/B_{n}
as left adjoint and
Z_{n}
as right adjoint.

The underlying
\mathbb{Z}
-graded object faithful functor
U:Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Gr(\mathcal{A})
has a left adjoint
L
defined as
L(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{Z}(1)\oplus\mathbf{Z}
, with differential

\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}0&1_{\mathbf{Z}}\\
0&0\\
\end{array}\right]
.
The functor
U
has also a right adjoint
R
defined as
R(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{Z}(-1)\oplus\mathbf{Z}
, with differential

\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}0&0\\
1_{\mathbf{Z}(-1)}&0\\
\end{array}\right]
. By construction,
L
and
R
are exact and the complexes

L(\mathbf{Z}),R(\mathbf{Z})
are exact with
B(L(\mathbf{Z}))=\mathbf{Z}(1),B(R(\mathbf{Z}))=\mathbf{Z}
.

4.1.1.

Let
u:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}
be a morphism. Then
H(u)
is a
monomorphism if and only if the diagram

\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{Z(\mathbf{C})}}

is a pullback and
H(u)
is an isomorphism if and only if the
previous diagram is exact (
1.2.8
).
If
u
is a monomorphism then
H(u)
is an isomorphism
if and only if
B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=B(\mathbf{C})\cap Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

and
Z(\mathbf{C})=B(\mathbf{C})\cup Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
.
Dually,
H(u)
is an epimorphism if and only if the diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}/B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\delta^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}/B(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\delta}}
\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C})(-1)}}

is a pushout and
H(u)
is an isomorphism if and only if the
previous diagram is exact.

4.1.2.

[
14
, Proposition 3.2]
For a class
\mathcal{K}
of objects of

\mathcal{A}
we denote by
Gr(\mathcal{K})
(resp.
Ch(\mathcal{K})
)
the class of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects (resp. complexes) that are
degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
. We denote by
ex[\mathcal{K}]
the
class of exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}
that have cycles in
\mathcal{K}
.

Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.
It is clear that
(Gr(\mathcal{K}),Gr(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
is a cotorsion theory
in
Gr(\mathcal{A})
. Then, using the adjoint pairs
(L,U)
and
(U,R)
(
4.1
)
we obtain from
2.1.2
(1) and
2.1.2
(1bis) the cotorsion theories

(Ch(\mathcal{K}),Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp})
and

(^{\perp}Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
.
See
8.2.5
(1) for a description of
Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
. The
cotorsion theory
(Ch(\mathcal{K}),Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp})
is generated
(
1.2.3
) by
\{D^{n}(Y)\}
, where
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and

n
is an integer; the cotorsion theory

(^{\perp}Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
is cogenerated
by
\{D^{n}(X)\}
, where
X\in\mathcal{K}
and
n
is an integer.
Suppose, moreover, that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects.
Then
Gr(\mathcal{A})
has enough
Gr(\mathcal{K})
objects
and
Ch(\mathcal{A})
has enough
ex[\mathcal{K}]
objects, meaning that
for each complex
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}
there is an epimorphism

\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
with
\mathbf{X}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
. In particular,

Ch(\mathcal{A})
has enough
Ch(\mathcal{K})
objects.

4.1.3.

Let
(\mathbf{C},d),(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.
Two morphisms
f,g
from
\mathbf{C}
to
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
are homotopic,
written
f\sim g
, if there is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects

s:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(1)
such that
g-f=d^{\prime}s+sd
. A complex is
homotopic to zero if its identity morphism is homotopic to the zero morphism.

Let
u:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}
be a morphism.
We denote by
Con(u)
(resp.
Cyl(u)
) the cone
(resp. cylinder) of
u
. There are exact sequences

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\overset{\tilde{u}}{\rightarrowtail}Cyl(u)\overset{\tilde{\pi}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Con(u)\ {\rm and}\ \mathbf{C}\overset{\pi}{\rightarrowtail}Con(u)\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(-1)

The morphism
u
factors into
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\overset{\tilde{u}}{\to}Cyl(u)\overset{\beta}{\to}\mathbf{C}
, where the morphism
\tilde{u}

is a monomorphism and the morphism
\beta
is a homotopy
equivalence that has a right inverse
\alpha
such that

\pi=\tilde{\pi}\alpha
.

If the complex
Con(u)
homotopic to zero then
u
is a
homotopy equivalence.

For every integer
p
we have a natural isomorphism

Con(u(p))\cong Con(u)(p)
defined as

((-1)^{p}1_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p+n-1}},1_{\mathbf{C}_{p+n}}):\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p+n-1}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{p+n}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p+n-1}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{p+n}

4.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and let

(\mathbf{C},d),(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.
We define the complex
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

in
\mathrm{Ab}
as
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})
with differential

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=D_{n}
defined as
D_{n}(f)=df-(-1)^{n}fd^{\prime}
.
There are natural isomorphisms

Ch(\mathrm{Ab})(S^{n}(\mathbb{Z}),\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\cong Z_{n}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\cong Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(n))

For every integer
r
we have

(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r),D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r))=(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r)),D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r)))

and a natural isomorphism

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(r),\mathbf{C})\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(-r)

constructed as follows. Let
n,p
be integers and
f_{p}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p+r}\to\mathbf{C}_{p+n}

a morphism. We define
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}((f_{p}))=((-1)^{nr}f_{p-r}))
.
One can check that
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is an isomorphism.

For every integer
n
we have a natural isomorphism

Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(n))/\sim\cong H_{n}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

Let
u:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}
be a morphism and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}

another complex. We have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C^{\prime\prime}},Con(u))=Con(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime},u))

and a natural isomorphism

\Sigma(u,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}):\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Con(u)(1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\cong Con(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

constructed as follows. We have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Con(u)(1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{p+1},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})

and

Con(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))_{n}=\underset{i}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{i+n-1})\oplus\underset{j}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{j},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j+n})

We define

\Sigma(u,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}((g_{p})_{p})=(((-1)^{n}g_{i-1}\sigma_{2})_{i},(g_{j}\sigma_{1})_{j})

where
\sigma_{1}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{p+1}

and
\sigma_{2}:\mathbf{C}_{p+1}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\oplus\mathbf{C}_{p+1}

are coprojection morphisms. One can check that
\Sigma(u,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
is a morphism.

Let
r
be an integer and
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
. Then

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{r}(A),\mathbf{C})=\mathcal{A}(A,\mathbf{C}_{r+})

where
\mathcal{A}(A,\mathbf{C}_{r+})_{n}=\mathcal{A}(A,\mathbf{C}_{r+n})
has differential
\mathcal{A}(A,d_{r+n})
,
and

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{r}(A))=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r-},A)

where
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r-},A)_{n}=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r-n},A)

has differential
\mathcal{A}((-1)^{n+1}d^{\prime}_{r-n+1},A)
.

4.2.3.

[
12
, Lemma 3.4]
Suppose that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
is right bounded,
that is, there is an integer
i
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}=0
for
n<i
.
For each integer
r\geqslant i
we define the subcomplex

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

of
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
as

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{i\leqslant p\leqslant r}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})

with differential induced by
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
. The complex

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(i)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{C})
has differential

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i},d)
. Let

t_{r}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r-1)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
be the
natural projection epimorphism
t_{r}((f_{p})_{i\leqslant p\leqslant r})=(f_{p})_{i\leqslant p\leqslant r-1}
; we have
{\rm Ker}\,t_{r}=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})
, where

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})_{n}=\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+n})
has
differential
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},d_{r+n})
.
We have a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\cong\underset{r\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant i})^{op}}{{\rm Lim}\,}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

hence an exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\underset{r\geqslant i}{\prod}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\underset{r\geqslant i}{\prod}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

Suppose, in addition, that the complexes
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})
and
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{C})

are exact. Induction over
r\geqslant i
implies then that

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

is exact for all
r\geqslant i
, therefore

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact.
In particular, let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}

and suppose
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{K}
for
n\geqslant i
and

\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
. By
1.2.1
(1bis)
the complexes
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})

and
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{C})
are exact, therefore

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact.

4.2.4.

Let
k
be an integer. We denote by
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
the complex

...\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{k+2}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{k+1}\to Z_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\to 0\to...

where
Z_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is in degree
k
. There are natural
morphisms
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\to\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

and for every integer
n
the direct system
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{n}\to\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{n}\to\tau_{k-2}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{n}\to...

is eventually equal to
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}
; hence

\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and we have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})=\underset{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k}}{{\rm Lim}\,}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})

For every integer
n
the inverse system

(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})_{n})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k}}

satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition.

Proof.

Let
l\leqslant i\leqslant k
and let

t_{i,l}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{l}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})_{n}\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})_{n}

be the natural morphism. We have
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{p\geqslant i}{\prod}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})
and

t_{i,l}((f^{l}_{p})_{p})=(f^{l}_{i}j^{\prime}_{i},f^{l}_{i+1},f^{l}_{i+2},...)

It follows that
{\rm Im}\,t_{i,i-1}={\rm Im}\,t_{i,i-2}=...
, hence the assertion.
∎

4.2.5.

[
16
, Prop. 13.2.3]
Let
F:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
be a left exact
functor between abelian categories. For every complex
\mathbf{D}

in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
there is a natural morphism

(2)

\displaystyle{{H(F(\mathbf{D}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\mathbf{D})}
\displaystyle{{F(H(\mathbf{D}))}}

Let
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that

F
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. If

B(\mathbf{D}),Z(\mathbf{D})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
then
(
2
) is an isomorphism. We note that

(i)
B(\mathbf{D})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
if
\mathbf{D}\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})

and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is closed under quotients, and

(ii)
Z(\mathbf{D})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})
if
H(\mathbf{D})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\prime})

and
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
is closed under extensions.

[

4.2.7.

[

]

\mathcal{A}
