We have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi(\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}\mathbf{C}^{i}}}

Let
\sigma^{H,i}:H(\mathbf{C}^{i})\to\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})
be coprojection morphisms and let
\Phi(H(\mathbf{C})):\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})\to\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})
be the
unique morphism such that
\Phi(H(\mathbf{C}))\sigma^{H,i}=\sigma^{H,i-1}H(u^{i})-\sigma^{H,i}
for
i\leqslant k
.
We have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(H(\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi(H(\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}}

Let
h:\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})\to H(\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i})
be the unique
morphism such that
h\sigma^{H,i}=H(\sigma^{i})
for
i\leqslant k
.
We have the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(H(\mathbf{C}))}
\scriptstyle{h}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi(H(\mathbf{C}))}
\scriptstyle{h}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}H(\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{H(\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\Phi(\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{H(\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\pi(\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{H(\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}\mathbf{C}^{i})}}

from which we obtain the unique dotted arrow
h^{\prime}
such that

h^{\prime}\pi(H(\mathbf{C}))=H(\pi(\mathbf{C}))h
. Suppose now that

\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4 and that
u^{i}
is a degreewise split
monomorphism for
i\leqslant k
. Then
\Phi(\mathbf{C})
is a
monomorphism. If, moreover,
H(u^{i})
is a degreewise split
monomorphism for
i\leqslant k
then
h^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
n
be an integer and
r^{i}_{n}:\mathbf{C}^{i-1}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}^{i}_{n}
a retract of
u^{i}_{n}
,
i\leqslant k
.
We inductively define

t^{i}_{n}:\mathbf{C}^{i}_{n}\to\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i}_{n}
as
t^{k}_{n}=0,t^{i-1}_{n}=(\sigma^{i}_{n}+t^{i}_{n})r^{i}_{n}
for
i<k
. Let

r(\mathbf{C})_{n}:\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i}\to\underset{i\leqslant k}{\bigoplus}\mathbf{C}^{i}
be the
unique morphism such that
r(\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma^{i}_{n}=t^{i}_{n}

for
i\leqslant k
. Then
r(\mathbf{C})_{n}\Phi(\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma^{i}_{n}=r(\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma^{i-1}_{n}u^{i}_{n}-r(\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma^{i}_{n}=t^{i-1}_{n}u^{i}_{n}-t^{i}_{n}=\sigma^{i}_{n}
,
therefore
r(\mathbf{C})_{n}
is a retract of
\Phi(\mathbf{C})_{n}
.
Suppose that
H(u^{i})
is a degreewise split monomorphism;
then the same reasoning as above shows that
\Phi(H(\mathbf{C}))

is a monomorphism. Let

\partial(\Phi(\mathbf{C}),\pi(\mathbf{C})):H(\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}\mathbf{C}^{i})\to\underset{i\leqslant k}{H}(\bigoplus\mathbf{C}^{i})(-1)

be the connecting morphism associated to

(\Phi(\mathbf{C}),\pi(\mathbf{C}))
. Since

h
is an isomorphism and
\Phi(H(\mathbf{C}))
is a
monomorphism we have that
H(\Phi(\mathbf{C}))
is a
monomorphism, hence
\partial(\Phi(\mathbf{C}),\pi(\mathbf{C}))=0

and
H(\pi(\mathbf{C}))
is an epimorphism. It follows that
h^{\prime}

is an isomorphism.
∎

5.
Cotorsion theories and dimension

5.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and

\mathcal{K}
a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.

5.1.1.

[
4
, §3 n
o
1 lemme 1]
(1)
Consider the solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{k^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{k}
\displaystyle{{B^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}}

with
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
,
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
semi-exact,
{\rm Ker}\,u\in\mathcal{K}
,
(u,v)
exact,

fu^{\prime}=vf^{\prime}
and
f=vk+k^{\prime\prime}v^{\prime}
. Then there is a morphism
k^{\prime}:X\to A^{\prime}
such that
f^{\prime}=uk^{\prime}+ku^{\prime}
.

[
4
, §3 n
o
1 lemme 2]
(2)
Consider the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{B^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}}

with
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
,
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
semi-exact,
{\rm Ker}\,u\in\mathcal{K}
and
(u,v)
exact.
Then there is a morphism
f^{\prime}:X\to A^{\prime}
such that
uf^{\prime}=fu^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove 1, part 2 is similar. We apply the functor
\mathcal{A}(X,-)
to the given diagram;
we obtain the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(X,X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(X,B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}}
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}(X,B^{\prime\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(X,A^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(X,A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\displaystyle{{\mathcal{A}(X,A^{\prime\prime})}}

with top row semi-exact and bottom row exact (
1.2.1
(1bis)).
We have
\bar{v}\overline{f^{\prime}-ku^{\prime}}=\overline{k^{\prime\prime}v^{\prime}u^{\prime}}=0
, hence there is

l:\mathcal{A}(X,X)\to{\rm Im}\,\bar{u}
such that
il=\overline{f^{\prime}-ku^{\prime}}
, where

i:{\rm Im}\,\bar{u}\to\mathcal{A}(X,A)
is the inclusion. Taking
1_{X}
we obtain
that there is
k^{\prime}:X\to A^{\prime}
such that
\bar{u}(k^{\prime})=f^{\prime}-ku^{\prime}
, that is,
f^{\prime}=uk^{\prime}+ku^{\prime}
.
∎

5.1.2.

[
4
, §3 n
o
1 prop. 1a)]
Let
(\mathbf{X},d^{\prime}),(\mathbf{C},d)
be
complexes in
\mathcal{A}
and
r
an integer. Let
(u_{i}:\mathbf{X}_{i}\to\mathbf{C}_{i})_{i\leqslant r}

be a family of morphisms such that
d_{i}u_{i}=u_{i-1}d^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\leqslant r
. Suppose that

\mathbf{X}_{i}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
for
i>r
,
H_{i}(\mathbf{C})=0
for
i\geqslant r
and

B_{i}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{K}
for
i>r
. Then the family
(u_{i})
extends to a morphism

v:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
; any two such extensions are homotopic.

5.1.3.

[
4
, §5 n
o
2 lemme 1a)]

Let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{C}
be complexes in
\mathcal{A}
with

\mathbf{X}\in Ch(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})
,
\mathbf{C}
exact with cycles
in
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathbf{C}_{n}=0
for
n<0
. Then

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{C})
is exact.

5.1.4.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.
A
\mathcal{K}
-resolution of an object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair

(\mathbf{C},p)
, where
\mathbf{C}
is a
complex that is degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
,
\mathbf{C}_{n}=0

for
n<0
and
p:\mathbf{C}\to S^{0}(A)
is a quasi-isomorphism.
A
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
-resolution of an object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
is a
pair
(\mathbf{C},e)
, where
\mathbf{C}
is a
complex that is degreewise in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
,
\mathbf{C}_{n}=0

for
n>0
and
e:S^{0}(A)\to\mathbf{C}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

5.1.5.

[
4
, §3 n
o
2 prop. 3]

Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism in
\mathcal{A}
. Let
p^{\prime}:\mathbf{X}\to S^{0}(A)

be a morphism with
\mathbf{X}_{n}=0
for
n<0
and
\mathbf{X}

degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
, and
p:\mathbf{C}\to S^{0}(B)
a

\mathcal{K}
-resolution of
B
with
B(\mathbf{C})
degreewise in

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. Then there is a morphism
\tilde{f}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
, unique up to homotopy, such that
p\tilde{f}=S^{0}(f)p^{\prime}
.

5.1.6.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a left complete cotorsion
theory, meaning that
{\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{K})=Ob(\mathcal{A})
(
1.2.4
). A complete
\mathcal{K}
-resolution
of an object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(\mathbf{C},p)
, where

(\mathbf{C},p)
is a
\mathcal{K}
-resolution of
A
and
B(\mathbf{C})
is
degreewise in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. Every object of
\mathcal{A}
has a
complete
\mathcal{K}
-resolution.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a right complete
cotorsion theory, meaning that
{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{K})=Ob(\mathcal{A})
. A complete
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
-resolution
of an object
A
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(\mathbf{C},e)
, where

(\mathbf{C},e)
is a
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
-resolution of
A
and
B(\mathbf{C})
is
degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
. Every object of
\mathcal{A}
has a
complete
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
-resolution.

5.1.7.

[
4
, §3 n
o
2 cor. à la prop. 3]

Let
(\mathbf{C},p)
and
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},p^{\prime})
be two complete

\mathcal{K}
-resolutions of
A
. There is a homotopy equivalence

\alpha:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}
, unique up to homotopy,
such that
p\alpha=p^{\prime}
.

5.1.8.

Let
(\mathbf{C},p)
and
(\mathbf{X},p^{\prime})
be two

\mathcal{K}
-resolutions of
A
with
(\mathbf{C},p)

complete. Then there is a morphism
\tilde{f}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
,
unique up to homotopy, such that
p\tilde{f}=p^{\prime}
.

5.1.9.

Let
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}

and
n
an integer. Every morphism
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
with
B_{n-1}(u)

an isomorphism factors as
\mathbf{C}\overset{u^{\prime}}{\to}\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\overset{v}{\to}\mathbf{C}
,
where:
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
is exact,
B_{n}(u^{\prime})
is an isomorphism,
B_{n-1}(v)=B_{n-1}(u)

and the diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}}}

is exact (
1.2.8
).

Proof.

The commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{n+2}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}_{n+1}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+1}\times_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}}\mathbf{C}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{n+1}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{n}}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B_{n-1}(u)}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}}

shows that we can put

\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{k},&k\geqslant n+2,\\
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n+1}\times_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}}\mathbf{C}_{n},&k=n+1,\\
\mathbf{C}_{n},&k\leqslant n\end{cases}

u^{\prime}_{k}=\begin{cases}u_{k},&k\geqslant n+2,\\
1_{\mathbf{C}_{k}},&k\leqslant n\end{cases}

and

v_{k}=\begin{cases}1_{\mathbf{C}_{k}},&k\geqslant n+2,\\
u_{k},&k\leqslant n\end{cases}

∎

5.1.10.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a left exact
cotorsion theory (
1.2.7
) and let
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

be a morphism as in
5.1.9
. If
\mathbf{C}_{k},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}

for all
k\geqslant n
then
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
for all
k\geqslant n
.

Proof.

This follows from the construction of
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{k}
and
1.2.8
.
∎

5.1.11.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a left
exact and left complete cotorsion theory. Let
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})

and
n
an integer
\geqslant 0
. The following are equivalent:

(1) there is a
\mathcal{K}
-resolution
(\mathbf{C},p)
of
A
with

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{K}
;

(2) any
\mathcal{K}
-resolution
(\mathbf{D},q)
of
A
has

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{D})\in\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2) Let
(\mathbf{D},q)
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{K}
-resolution of
A

and let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},p^{\prime})
be a complete
\mathcal{K}
-resolution of
A
. By
5.1.8

there are morphisms
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
w:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

such that
p^{\prime}u=p
and
p^{\prime}w=q
. The morphism
u
is a morphism between exact complexes
with
B_{-1}(u)=1_{A}
, hence (
5.1.9
) we can factor it as
u=vu^{\prime}
. We continue
this factorisation procedure with
u^{\prime}
in place of
u
; after
n-1
such factorizations we obtain
an exact diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n-1}}}

Applying
5.1.10
each time we factor we have that
\mathbf{X}_{n-1}\in\mathcal{K}
.
Since
\mathcal{K}
is closed under extensions and retracts we obtain that

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}
. Now, the morphism
w
is a morphism
between exact complexes with
B_{-1}(w)=1_{A}
, so after applying to it
the same procedure as to
u
we obtain an exact diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{D})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n-1}}}

with
\mathbf{X}_{n-1}\in\mathcal{K}
. By
1.2.8
we then obtain that

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{D})\in\mathcal{K}
.
∎

5.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
be cotorsion theories in

\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{L}
.

5.2.1.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects
and
(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
is left exact and left complete.
We inductively define an ascending sequence

(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L})_{n\geqslant 0}
and a descending sequence

(\mathcal{S}_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
of
classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}
such that

n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{K}
,

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}

and
n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
The argument will show that

(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L},(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L})^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory.

We put
0-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{L}
.
Having defined
n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, we set

(n+1)-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}\cap{\rm Coker}\,(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtail^{\perp}\mathcal{L})
and
\mathcal{S}_{n+1}={\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{S}_{n}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
.

We show that
\mathcal{S}_{n+1}\subset((n+1)-^{\perp}\mathcal{L})^{\perp}
.
Let
C\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow A
be an exact sequence with

C\in\mathcal{S}_{n+1}
and
A\in(n+1)-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
We can find an exact sequence
S_{n}\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow C

with
S_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}}

where
X_{0}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
and
PB,PB^{\prime}
are pullbacks.
Then (
5.1.11
)
PB\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
, hence the
sequence
S_{n}\rightarrowtail PB^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow PB

splits by the inductive hypothesis. Form now the commutative
solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{A}
\displaystyle{{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}}

where the composite
PB\to Y\to C
is
f
and
PO
means pushout.
There is then the dotted arrow that makes commutative the resulting
square diagram and such that the composite
X_{0}\to PO\to B
is
g
.
It follows that the diagram

\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}}

is a pushout. Since
A\in\mathcal{K}
the sequence
(u,v)
splits, hence

u
has a retract and therefore so does
C\to B
.

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{n+1}\subset(n+1)-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
Let
A\in^{\perp}\mathcal{S}_{n+1}
. We first show that
A\in\mathcal{K}
.
Let
Y\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow A
be an exact sequence with

Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
; then
Y\in\mathcal{S}_{n+1}
since we have the
exact sequence
0\to Y=Y
and
0\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Consequently the first
short exact sequence splits, so
A\in\mathcal{K}
. Now, we can find an exact sequence

K\rightarrowtail X_{0}\twoheadrightarrow A
where
X_{0}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
We show that
K\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. Given an exact sequence

S_{n}\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow K
with
S_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\displaystyle{{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}}

where
PO,PO^{\prime}
are pushouts and
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. Then
PO\in\mathcal{S}_{n+1}
,
hence the sequence
PO\rightarrowtail PO^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow A
splits.
We can form then the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}}

where
PB
means pullback and the composite
K\to X_{0}\to PO
is
f
.
There is then the dotted arrow that makes commutative the resulting square diagram
and such that the composite
B\to PB\to Y
is
g
. It follows that the diagram

\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}}

is a pullback. Since
X_{0}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\subset n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}

the sequence
(u,v)
splits, hence
v
has a section and therefore so does
B\to K
.

5.2.2.

Under the assumptions
5.2.1
, if
\mathcal{K}
is left exact then
so is
n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

We use induction over
n\geqslant 0
. The case
n=0
holds by assumption.
Suppose that
n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
is left exact and let

A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}
be an exact sequence with

A,A^{\prime\prime}\in(n+1)-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. Then
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
by assumption.
We can find an exact sequence
A^{\prime\prime}_{0}\rightarrowtail X^{\prime\prime}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}

with
X^{\prime\prime}_{0}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L},A^{\prime\prime}_{0}\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
(
5.1.11
).
Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{K^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{K^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}}

where
PB
and
PB^{\prime}
mean pullback,
X_{0}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
and

K^{\prime}
is the kernel of
X_{0}\to PB
. Then
PB^{\prime}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}

and we consider the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{K^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{K_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X^{\prime\prime}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A^{\prime\prime}}}

where
K_{0}
is the kernel of the composite morphism
X_{0}\to PB\to A
.
By the universal property of kernel we have the induced dotted arrows that make
the resulting square diagrams commute; moreover, by the snake diagram
the dotted sequence of the previous diagram is a short exact sequence.
By the assumption on
A
and
5.1.11
we have that

K_{0}\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
, therefore
K^{\prime}\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}

by the inductive hypothesis, hence the assertion.
∎

5.2.3.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives and projectives.
Putting
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}=Ob(\mathcal{A})

in
5.2.1
and
5.2.2
we obtain that the class of objects
of
\mathcal{A}
of projective dimension
\leqslant n
is the left class
of a left exact cotorsion theory.

5.2.4.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}

objects and
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is right exact
and right complete. We inductively define an ascending sequence

(n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})_{n\geqslant 0}
and a descending sequence

(\mathcal{T}_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
of classes of objects of
\mathcal{A}

such that
n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathcal{L}
,

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{T}_{n}

and
n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\perp}
.

We put
0-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{K}
. Having defined
n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
we set
(n+1)-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{L}\cap{\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\twoheadrightarrow n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
and
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}={\rm Ker}\,(^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{n})
.

An argument similar to
5.2.1
and
5.2.2
shows that

(^{\perp}(n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion
theory that is right exact if
\mathcal{L}
is moreover right exact.
If
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is cogenerated (
1.2.3
)
by
\mathcal{S}
and
(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
is
cogenerated by
\mathcal{T}
then
(^{\perp}(n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is cogenerated by
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, where

\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{S},\mathcal{C}_{1}={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{0}),...,\mathcal{C}_{n+1}={\rm Ker}\,(\mathcal{T}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n})

for
n\geqslant 0
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives and projectives.
Putting
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
we obtain that the
class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
of injective dimension
\leqslant n
is
the right class of a right exact cotorsion theory.

5.2.5.

Recall that
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{S}_{n+1}={\rm Coker}\,(\mathcal{S}_{n}\rightarrowtail\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
,
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{K}

and
\mathcal{T}_{n+1}={\rm Ker}\,(^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{n})
.

(1) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects
and
(^{\perp}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
is left exact and left complete. Then

n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\Rightarrow n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{L}

(1bis) Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
objects
and
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is right exact and right complete. Then

n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=\mathcal{L}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}\subset\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\Rightarrow n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}

Proof.

(1) The assertion is clear when
n=0
. Suppose that
n>0
and

n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}

and let
K_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}
. We can find an exact sequence

\displaystyle{{K_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{K}}

with
K\in\mathcal{K}
and
X_{0},...,X_{n-1}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
Since
K\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
it follows from
5.1.11

that
K_{n}\in^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
. Then

\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\perp}=n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
,
hence
\mathcal{L}=n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
. Conversely,
let
X\in\mathcal{K}
and let

\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{n-1}}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}}

be a
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
-resolution of
X
. Then

{\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1}\in\mathcal{T}_{n}\subset^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
,
hence
X\in n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}
.
∎

5.2.6.

Putting
\mathcal{K}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
in
5.2.5
and
assumming that
\mathcal{A}
has enough injectives we obtain
that
n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
if and only if
all objects of
\mathcal{L}
have injective dimension
\leqslant n
.
Putting
\mathcal{L}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
in
5.2.5
and
assumming that
\mathcal{A}
has enough projectives we obtain
that
n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
if and only if
all objects of
\mathcal{K}
have projective dimension
\leqslant n
.
Putting
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{L}=Ob(\mathcal{A})
in
5.2.5

and assumming that
\mathcal{A}
has enough projectives and injectives
we obtain that all objects of
\mathcal{A}
have projective dimension

\leqslant n
if and only if all objects of
\mathcal{A}
have injective
dimension
\leqslant n
.

5.2.7.

Suppose that there is
n\geqslant 0
such that

n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
This happens if
\underset{n\geqslant 0}{\bigcup}n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\underset{n\geqslant 0}{\bigcup}n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and the
sequences
(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L})_{n\geqslant 0}
and

(n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})_{n\geqslant 0}
are eventually constant.
In this case (
1.2.13
)

n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap(n-^{\perp}\mathcal{L})^{\perp}=\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and

{}^{\perp}(n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap n-\mathcal{K}^{\perp}=^{\perp}\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{L}
.

5.3.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC–situation.
We denote by
\mathcal{F}
the class of flat objects (
3.1
) of
\mathcal{A}
.
We assume that the cotorsion theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})

(
3.1.4
) is left complete and that
\mathcal{A}
has enough injective objects.
Then, using
5.2.1
and
5.2.2
with
\mathcal{K}=Ob(\mathcal{A})

and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{F}
, we obtain that
n-\mathcal{F}
is the left class
of a left exact cotorsion theory, as already noticed in
[
22
, Proposition 10.2.9]
.
The objects of
n-\mathcal{F}
are said to have flat dimension
\leqslant n
, or
to be
n
-flat [
loc. cit.
, Definition 3.3.3]. From
5.2.4
we obtain
that the class of cotorsion objects of
\mathcal{A}
of injective dimension

\leqslant n
is the right class of a right exact cotorsion theory. From
5.2.5

we obtain that all objects of
\mathcal{A}
have flat dimension
\leqslant n
if
and only if all cotorsion objects of
\mathcal{A}
have injective dimension
\leqslant n
.
If
\mathcal{A}
has enough projectives we obtain from
loc. cit.
that all
flat objects of
\mathcal{A}
have projective dimension
\leqslant n
if and only
if
Ob(\mathcal{A})=n-\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
the class of flat objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

and we further assume that the cotorsion theory
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp})

is left complete and that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has enough injective objects.

5.3.1.

[
22
, Proposition 14.2.3(2)]
Suppose, moreover, that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has
a set
(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators. Then an object
A
of

\mathcal{A}
is
n
-flat if and only if
H(A,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is (a cotorsion object)
of injective dimension
\leqslant n
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Suppose that
A
is
n
-flat. We can find an exact sequence

\displaystyle{{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}}

with
P_{0},...,P_{n-1},K
flat. Since
H(-,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is exact (
3.1.3
)
we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{H(A,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(P_{0},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(P_{n-1},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(K,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}}

where
H(P_{0},E^{\prime\prime}_{i}),...,H(P_{n-1},E^{\prime\prime}_{i}),H(K,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
are injective by
3.1.2
.
This implies that
H(A,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is
n
-injective. Conversely, let

\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{n}}
\displaystyle{{P_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{n-1}}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{P_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}}

be an
\mathcal{F}
-resolution of
A
. Then we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{H(A,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(P_{0},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(P_{n-1},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(d_{n},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}
\displaystyle{{H(P_{n},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{...}}

where
H(P_{n},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is injective for all
n\geqslant 0
. Since

H(A,E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is
n
-injective it follows from
5.1.11
that

{\rm Im}\,H(d_{n},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})=H({\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1},E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is injective, hence

{\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1}
is flat by
3.1.2
.
∎

6.
Tensor-hom-cotensor situations for categories of complexes

6.1.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC–situation. We assume that

\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}
have all limits and

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has all colimits. We revisit the
standard THC–situation

T:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

H:Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

C:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

associated to the given one. Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
(resp.
(\mathbf{C},d),(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime})
)
be complexes in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
).

(Tensor)

We define
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{p+q=n}{\bigoplus}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})
.
Let
\sigma_{p,q}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}

be coprojection morphisms. The differential

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=D_{n}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n-1}

is the unique morphism that satisfies

D_{n}\sigma_{p,q}=\sigma_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})+\sigma_{p,q-1}(-1)^{p}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},d_{q})

whenever
p+q=n
. The universal property that defines
D
implies that
D^{2}=0
.
We omit the details that
T
assembles into a bifunctor.

(Hom)

We define
H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}H(\mathbf{C}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
. Let
\pi_{p}:H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\to H(\mathbf{C}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
be projection morphisms. The differential

D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=D_{n}:H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\to H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n-1}

is the unique morphism that satisfies

\pi_{p}D_{n}=H(\mathbf{C}_{p},d^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})\pi_{p}-(-1)^{n}H(d_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n-1})\pi_{p-1}

for all integers
p
. The universal property that defines
D
implies that
D^{2}=0
.

(Cotensor)

We define
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
. Let
\pi_{p}:C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
be projection morphisms. The differential

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=D_{n}:C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n-1}

is the unique morphism that satisfies

\pi_{p}D_{n}=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},d^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})\pi_{p}-(-1)^{n}C(d^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n-1})\pi_{p-1}

for all integers
p
. The universal property that defines
D
implies that
D^{2}=0
.

(Tensor
\Leftrightarrow
Hom)

To give a morphism

u:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
is to give
morphisms
u_{p,q}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+q}
such that

d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q}u_{p,q}=u_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})+(-1)^{p}u_{p,q-1}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},d_{q})

To give a morphism
v:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
is to give
morphisms
v_{n,p}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}\to H(\mathbf{C}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
such that

v_{n-1,p}d^{\prime}_{n}=H(\mathbf{C}_{p},d^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})v_{n,p}-(-1)^{n}H(d_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n-1})v_{n,p-1}

Given a morphism
u:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}

we define
v_{n,p}
to be the adjoint transpose of
u_{n,p}
; conversely,
given a morphism
v:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})

we define
u_{n,p}
to be the adjoint transpose of
v_{n,p}
.

(Tensor
\Leftrightarrow
Cotensor)

To give a morphism
w:\mathbf{C}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
is to give
morphisms
w_{n,p}:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
such that

w_{n-1,p}d_{n}=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},d^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})w_{n,p}-(-1)^{n}C(d^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n-1})w_{n,p-1}

Given a morphism

u:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
we define
w_{n,p}=(-1)^{np}u_{p,n}^{\flat}
,
where
u_{p,n}^{\flat}:\mathbf{C}_{n}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})

is the adjoint transpose of
u_{p,n}
; conversely, given a morphism

w:\mathbf{C}\to C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
we define
u_{p,n}=(-1)^{pn}w_{n,p}^{\#}
,
where
w_{n,p}^{\#}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{n})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n}
is the
adjoint transpose of
w_{n,p}
.

6.1.1.

Consider the bifunctors
T,H
from
6.1
.
For every integer
r
we have

(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r),D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r))=(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(r),\mathbf{C}),D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(r),\mathbf{C}))

and

(H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})(r),D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})(r))=(H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)),D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)))

For every integer
r
we have a natural isomorphism

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r))\cong T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r)

constructed as follows. For each integer
n
let
\sigma_{p,q}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q+r})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r))_{n}
, where
p+q=n
, and

\sigma^{\prime}_{a,b}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{a},\mathbf{C}_{b})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r)_{n}
, where
a+b=n+r
, be coproduct coprojections.
Consider the morphism
(-1)^{pr}1_{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q+r})}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q+r})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q+r})
;
there is then a unique morphism

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r))_{n}\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r)_{n}

such that
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma_{p,q}=(-1)^{pr}\sigma^{\prime}_{p,q+r}
.
One can check that
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is a morphism. The inverse of

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}
is the unique morphism

\Sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r)_{n}\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r))_{n}

such that
\Sigma^{-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma^{\prime}_{a,b}=(-1)^{ar}\sigma_{a,b-r}
.
Naturality of
\Sigma
in both arguments is easy to see.

We note that if
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
has zero differential then the following diagram commutes

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d)}
\scriptstyle{D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(-1)}}

6.1.2.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and

u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
a morphism of complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.
We define

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u):T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Con(u))\to Con(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u))

as
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)=\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})\oplus 1_{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
, where
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}):T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}(-1))\cong T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})(-1)

has been defined in
6.1.1
. One can check that
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)

is a morphism, hence an isomorphism. Moreover, we have the following
commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\pi)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Con(u))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\delta)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\displaystyle{{Con(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})(-1)}}

If
u^{\prime}:\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
is a morphism of complexes
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}
is a complex in
\mathcal{A}

we similarly have an isomorphism

T(Con(u^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})\cong Con(T(u^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))
.

6.1.3.

[
22
, Proposition 4.5.2]
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in

\mathcal{A}
. If
T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves monomorphisms
then
\mathbf{C}
is degreewise flat (
3.1
). Conversely,
if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4 and
\mathbf{C}
is degreewise flat
then
T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves monomorphisms.

6.1.4.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4. Then
6.1.3
says that
the class of flat objects of
Ch(\mathcal{A})
is
Ch(\mathcal{F})
, hence
(
4.1.2
) in this case they form the left class of a cotorsion theory.

6.1.5.

[
4
, §4 n
o
2 cor. 1]

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4 and that
\mathbf{C}
is a complex in

\mathcal{A}
that is degreewise flat with zero differential. Then

T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves exact complexes.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be an exact complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. The differential
of
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is
T(d^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
; we have then
a natural isomorphism
T(B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})\cong B(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

and by
6.1.3
a natural isomorphism

T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})\cong Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))
.
The assertion follows from
6.1.3
.
∎

6.1.6.

[
4
, §4 n
o
3 lemme 1 et prop. 4]

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4.
If
\mathbf{C}
is a degreewise flat complex in
\mathcal{A}
with

\mathbf{C}_{n}=0
for
n<0
, then
T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves
exact complexes and quasi-isomorphisms.
Similarly, if
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
is a complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that is degreewise
flat with
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}=0
for
n<0
, then
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)

preserves exact complexes and quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be an exact complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
We have
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{q\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n-q},\mathbf{C}_{q})
.
For each integer
r\geqslant 0
we define the subcomplex

T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
of
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
by

T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=\underset{0\leqslant q\leqslant r}{\bigoplus}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n-q},\mathbf{C}_{q})

Let
u^{r}:T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to T^{(r+1)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

be the natural monomorphism; we have
{\rm Coker}\,u^{r}=T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{r+1}(\mathbf{C}_{r+1}))
.
By
6.1.5
the complex
{\rm Coker}\,u^{r}
is exact, hence
u^{r}
is
a quasi-isomorphism. Since
T^{(0)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact
(
3.1.1
), induction on
r\geqslant 0
shows that

T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact for each

r\geqslant 0
. We have a natural isomorphism

\underset{r\in\mathbb{N}}{{\rm Colim}\,}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\cong T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

Let
\sigma_{r}:T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\underset{r\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

be coprojection morphisms. Let

c:\underset{r\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\underset{r\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

be the unique morphism such that
c\sigma_{r}=\sigma_{r+1}u^{r}-\sigma_{r}

for
r\geqslant 0
; then
{\rm Coker}\,c=\underset{r\in\mathbb{N}}{{\rm Colim}\,}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
. Since
u^{r}_{n}
is a split
monomorphism for
r\geqslant 0
and all integers
n
, the morphism

c
is a monomorphism (
4.2.7
). Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4
the homology exact sequence associated to

\underset{r\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\overset{c}{\rightarrowtail}\underset{r\geqslant 0}{\bigoplus}T^{(r)}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

implies that

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact. Let now
u^{\prime}:\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

be a quasi-isomorphism in
Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
We factor (
4.1.3
)
u^{\prime}
into
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\overset{\tilde{u^{\prime}}}{\to}Cyl(u^{\prime})\overset{\beta^{\prime}}{\to}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, where
\tilde{u^{\prime}}
is a monomorphism
and
\beta^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence. Since
T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves
homotopy equivalences, we can assume that
u^{\prime}
is a monomorphism.
Then
{\rm Coker}\,u^{\prime}
is exact and we have an exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T({\rm Coker}\,u^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}}

By the first part
T({\rm Coker}\,u^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact, therefore
T(u^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

is a quasi-isomorphism. The case of
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
is dealt with similarly.
∎

6.1.7.

We denote by
\mathfrak{TC}
the class of complexes
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}

for which
T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves monomorphisms with exact cokernel.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}
are cocomplete and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}

satisfies AB4. Then
(\mathfrak{TC},\mathfrak{TC}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

Proof.

We shall use
3.2.4
. We take
\mathcal{K}_{1}=ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
, the class
of exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}=Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A}))

and
\mathcal{K}_{3}=Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}))
. It suffices to show that

Ch(\mathcal{A})
and
Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
have enough flat objects and that

Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
has enough injective objects. This follows from
4.1.2
.
∎

6.1.8.

The category
Ch(\mathrm{Ab})
is a closed symmetric monoidal category
with monoidal product
\otimes=T
(
6.1
) and unit
S^{0}(\mathbb{Z})
.
A category enriched over
(Ch(\mathrm{Ab}),\otimes,S^{0}(\mathbb{Z}))

will be called dg-category and a

(Ch(\mathrm{Ab}),\otimes,S^{0}(\mathbb{Z}))
-functor will be called
dg-functor. If
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian category then
Ch(\mathcal{A})

is a dg-category whose dg-hom is
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
4.2
).

6.1.9.

The standard THC-situation is an enriched THC-situation.
By this we mean that
T,H,C
are dg-functors and there are isomorphisms

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

which are dg-natural in
\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}

[
3
, Chapter 6, definition 6.7.1]
.

Proof.

We will show in detail only a part of the assertion.

Step 1

We sketch that
T
is a dg-functor. For
complexes
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}),(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},d^{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}})

in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
(\mathbf{C},d^{\mathbf{C}}),(\mathbf{D},d^{\mathbf{D}})

in
\mathcal{A}
we need to define a morphism

T_{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\mathbf{D}}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})\otimes\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))

This is equivalent to defining a morphism

u_{p,q}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q}\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))_{p+q}

for all integers
p,q
such that

D(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))_{p+q}u_{p,q}=u_{p-1,q}(D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\otimes 1)+(-1)^{p}u_{p,q-1}(1\otimes D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q})

Let
\pi_{i}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))_{p+q}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i},T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q})

be projection morphisms. We define a morphism

\mu_{i,p,q}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i},T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q})

as follows. Let
(f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l}\in\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime})_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q}
;
then
\mu_{i,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})
is the unique morphism

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i}\to T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q}
such that

\mu_{i,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=(-1)^{qx}\sigma_{x+p,y+q}T(f^{\prime}_{x},f_{y})

for all
x+y=i
, where
\sigma_{x,y}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{x},\mathbf{C}_{y})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i}

and
\sigma_{x+p,y+q}:T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime}_{x+p},\mathbf{D}_{y+q})\to T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q}

are coprojection morphisms. We now define
u_{p,q}
to be the
unique morphism such that
\pi_{i}u_{p,q}=\mu_{i,p,q}
.

We calculate
u_{p-1,q}(D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\otimes 1)
.
For
x+y=i
we have

\displaystyle M\overset{def}{=}\pi_{i}u_{p-1,q}(D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\otimes 1)((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

\displaystyle\mu_{i,p-1,q}((d^{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}_{k+p}f^{\prime}_{k}-(-1)^{p}f^{\prime}_{k-1}d^{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{qx}\sigma_{x+p-1,y+q}T(d^{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}_{x+p}f^{\prime}_{x}-(-1)^{p}f^{\prime}_{x-1}d^{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}_{x},f_{y})=

(-1)^{qx}\sigma_{x+p-1,y+q}T(d^{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}_{x+p}f^{\prime}_{x},f_{y})-(-1)^{qx+p}\sigma_{x+p-1,y+q}T(f^{\prime}_{x-1}d^{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}_{x},f_{y})

We calculate
(-1)^{p}u_{p,q-1}(1\otimes D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q})
.
For
x+y=i
we have

\displaystyle N\overset{def}{=}(-1)^{p}\pi_{i}u_{p,q-1}(1\otimes D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})_{q})((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{p}\mu_{i,p,q-1}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(d^{\mathbf{D}}_{l+q}f_{l}-(-1)^{q}f_{l-1}d^{\mathbf{C}}_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

(-1)^{p+(q-1)x}\sigma_{x+p,y+q-1}T(f^{\prime}_{x},d^{\mathbf{D}}_{y+q}f_{y})-(-1)^{p+q+(q-1)x}\sigma_{x+p,y+q-1}T(f^{\prime}_{x},f_{y-1}d^{\mathbf{C}}_{y})

We calculate
D(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))_{p+q}u_{p,q}
. For
x+y=i
we have

P\overset{def}{=}\pi_{i}D(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),T(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))_{p+q}u_{p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(1,D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q})(\mu_{i,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l}))\sigma_{x,y}-

(-1)^{p+q}\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i},1)(\mu_{i-1,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l}))\sigma_{x,y}

Now

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(1,D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q})(\mu_{i,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l}))\sigma_{x,y}=

\displaystyle D(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})_{i+p+q}\mu_{i,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})\sigma_{x,y}=

(-1)^{qx}\sigma_{x+p-1,y+q}T(d^{\mathbf{D}^{\prime}}_{x+p}f^{\prime}_{x},f_{y})+(-1)^{qx+x+p}\sigma_{x+p,y+q-1}T(f^{\prime}_{x},d^{\mathbf{D}}_{y+q}f_{y})

and

\displaystyle(-1)^{p+q}\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i},1)(\mu_{i-1,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l}))\sigma_{x,y}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{p+q}\mu_{i-1,p,q}((f^{\prime}_{k})_{k}\otimes(f_{l})_{l})D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i}\sigma_{x,y}=

(-1)^{p+q+q(x-1)}\sigma_{x+p-1,y+q}T(f^{\prime}_{x-1}d^{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}_{x},f_{y})-(-1)^{p+q+x+qx}\sigma_{x+p,y+q-1}T(f^{\prime}_{x},f_{y-1}d^{\mathbf{C}}_{y})

We obtain
M+N=P
, as needed.

Step 2

We have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}=\underset{i}{\prod}\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(\underset{p+q=i}{\bigoplus}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{i+n})

and

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\underset{q}{\prod}H(\mathbf{C}_{q},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}))

We define
\Theta_{n}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))_{n}

as

\Theta_{n}((u_{p,q}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}))=(v_{p}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\to H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{p+n})

where
v_{p}
is defined as the unique morphism such that
\pi_{q}v_{p}

is the adjoint transpose
v_{p,q}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\to H(\mathbf{C}_{q},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n})
of
u_{p,q}
and where
\pi_{q}

is the projection morphism. It is clear that
\theta_{n}
is an isomorphism of
graded objects. We show that
\Theta
is a morphism. We denote by

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C};\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
the differential of

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})

and by
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime};\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
the differential of

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))
.
We calculate
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C};\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
.
Let
f_{i}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{i+n}
be a
morphism and
\sigma_{p,q}:T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{i}
, where
p+q=i
, be coprojection
morphisms. If
u_{p,q}=f_{p+q}\sigma_{p,q}
then

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C};\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}((u_{p,q}))=d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}u_{p,q}-(-1)^{n}u_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},1)-(-1)^{n+p}u_{p,q-1}T(1,d_{q})

We calculate
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime};\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
. Let
g_{p}:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p}\to H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{p+n}
be a morphism and

\pi_{q}:H(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{p+n}\to H(\mathbf{C}_{q},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n})

be projection morphisms. If
v_{p,q}=\pi_{q}g_{p}
then

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime};\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}((v_{p,q}))=H(1,d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n})v_{p,q}-(-1)^{n}v_{p-1,q}d^{\prime}_{p}-(-1)^{p+n}H(d_{q},1)v_{p,q-1}

We have

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime};\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}\Theta_{n}((u_{p,q}))=(H(1,d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n})v_{p,q}-(-1)^{n}v_{p-1,q}d^{\prime}_{p}-(-1)^{p+n}H(d_{q},1)v_{p,q-1})

and

\Theta_{n-1}D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C};\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}((u_{p,q}))=

\Theta_{n-1}((d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}u_{p,q}-(-1)^{n}u_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},1)-(-1)^{n+p}u_{p,q-1}T(1,d_{q}))=(v^{\prime}_{p})

where
\pi_{q}v^{\prime}_{p}
is the adjoint transpose of

d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}u_{p,q}-(-1)^{n}u_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},1)-(-1)^{n+p}u_{p,q-1}T(1,d_{q})

The adjoint transpose of
d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n}u_{p,q}
is
H(1,d^{\prime\prime}_{p+q+n})v_{p,q}
,
the adjoint transpose of
u_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},1)
is
v_{p-1,q}d^{\prime}_{p}
and
the adjoint transpose of
u_{p,q-1}T(1,d_{q})
is
H(d_{q},1)v_{p,q-1}
.
The desired equality follows.
∎

6.2.

6.2.1.

For all integers
r
we have natural isomorphisms

\overline{T}(D^{r}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})\cong T(S^{r}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}){\rm\ and}\ \overline{H}(\mathbf{C},D^{r}(A^{\prime\prime}))\cong H(\mathbf{C},S^{r-1}(A^{\prime\prime}))

6.2.2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has a set
(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators.
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. Then
\overline{T}(-,\mathbf{C})

preserves monomorphisms if and only if
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i}))
is an injective
complex for all integers
r
and all
i\in I
.

Proof.

The set
(D^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i}))_{i\in I}
, where
r
is an integer, is a set of injective cogenerators
of
Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
. The assertion follows then from
3.1.2
and
6.2.1
.
∎

Recall (
3.1
) that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
denotes the class of flat objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

6.2.3.

[
7
, Proposition 2.4]

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has a set
(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators.
For a complex
(\mathbf{C},d)
in
\mathcal{A}
consider the following statements:

(1)
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
(
4.1.2
);

(2)
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))
is injective for all integers
r
and all injective objects

E^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
;

(3)
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i}))
is exact and
Z(H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})))

is degreewise in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp}
for all integers
r
and all
i\in I
.

We have
(1)\Rightarrow(2)\Rightarrow(3)
. If
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has a faithfully flat
object (
3.4
) then part 3 implies that
\mathbf{C}
is exact. If, moreover,

Z(H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})))
is degreewise injective for all integers
r
and all

i\in I
then
(3)\Rightarrow(1)
.

Proof.

We have
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))_{n}=H(\mathbf{C}_{r-n},E^{\prime\prime})
and the differential of

H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))
is
D(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))_{n}=(-1)^{n-1}H(d_{r-n+1},E^{\prime\prime})
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Consider the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{r-n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n-1}d_{r-n+1}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{r-n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n}d_{r-n}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}_{r-n-1}}
\displaystyle{{B_{r-n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{B_{r-n-1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the sequence
(i,p)
is exact. Applying the functor
H(-,E^{\prime\prime})
to the sequence
(i,p)

we obtain (
3.1.3
) the exact sequence

H(B_{r-n-1}(\mathbf{C}),E^{\prime\prime})\rightarrowtail H(\mathbf{C}_{r-n},E^{\prime\prime})\twoheadrightarrow H(B_{r-n}(\mathbf{C}),E^{\prime\prime})

hence
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))
is exact. In particular we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{C}_{r-n},E^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{n-1}H(d_{r-n+1},E^{\prime\prime})}
\displaystyle{H(\mathbf{C}_{r-n+1},E^{\prime\prime})}
\displaystyle{{H(B_{r-n}(\mathbf{C}),E^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Since
B_{r-n}(\mathbf{C})
is flat, by
3.1.2
we have that

H(B_{r-n}(\mathbf{C}),E^{\prime\prime})
is injective, hence
H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}))
is injective.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
is clear. Let now
P^{\prime}
be a faithfully flat object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
By
3.4.4
applied to the sequence
(d_{r-n+1},d_{r-n})
we obtain that

\mathbf{C}
is exact. Then
Z_{n}(H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime})))\cong H(B_{r-n-1}(\mathbf{C}),E^{\prime\prime}_{i})
(cf.
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
), hence if

Z_{n}(H(\mathbf{C},S^{r}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})))
is injective it follows from
3.1.2

that
B_{r-n-1}(\mathbf{C})
is flat.
∎

6.2.4.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. For any two complexes
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}
we define the complex

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
of abelian
groups as

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}=Ch(\mathrm{Ab})(S^{n}(\mathbb{Z}),\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

with differential defined as
\overline{D}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}(u)=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d)u
,
where
d
is the differential of
\mathbf{C}
. The morphism

\overline{D}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}
is well defined since

S^{n}(\mathbb{Z})(1)=S^{n-1}(\mathbb{Z})
. We have

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}\cong Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(n))
, hence

\overline{D}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}(u)=d(n)u
, where
d(n)

is the differential of
\mathbf{C}(n)
. Thus,

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{Ch(\mathcal{A})}(S^{0}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}(-))

where
(\mathbf{C}(-),d(-))
is the complex in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
defined
as
\mathbf{C}(-)_{n}=\mathbf{C}(n),d(-)_{n}=d(n)
.

6.2.5.

[
7
, Proposition 2.1.1)]

There are natural isomorphisms

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(\overline{T}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\cong\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(\overline{T}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\cong\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\overline{C}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

Proof.

We prove the first isomorphism. We note that from
6.1.1
and the
construction of
\overline{H}
we have

(\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})(r),\overline{D}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})(r))=(\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)),\overline{D}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)))

Moreover,

(10)

\overline{D}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)))=\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},d^{\prime\prime}(r))

where
d^{\prime\prime}
is the differential of
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
and
d^{\prime\prime}(r)
is the differential of

\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(r)
. We then have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(\overline{T}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{n}

\displaystyle=Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})(\overline{T}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(n))

\displaystyle\cong Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(n))

\displaystyle=Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})(n))

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathrm{Homgr}}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\overline{H}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))_{n}

By (
10
) this isomorphism is compatible with the differentials,
hence we have the required isomorphism.
The second isomorphism is proved similarly.
∎

7.
The Künneth theorem for complexes in abelian categories

7.1.

Consider the standard THC–situation (
6.1
).
We assume that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4. Recall (
3.1
)
that
\mathcal{F}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
) denotes the class of flat
objects of
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
). We assume
that
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
and
(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp})
are left complete (
5.1.6
).
We simply say flat resolution (resp. complete flat resolution)
for an
\mathcal{F}
-resolution (resp. complete
\mathcal{F}
-resolution)
of an object of
\mathcal{A}
; same for the objects of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in place of
\mathcal{F}
.

7.1.1.

Let
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
.
For all integers
n
we define
\mathsf{Tor}_{n}(A^{\prime},A)=H_{n}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}},\mathbf{C}_{A}))
,
where
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}
(resp.
\mathbf{C}_{A}
) is a
complete flat resolution of
A^{\prime}
(resp.
A
).

Let
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathbf{P}
) be a flat
resolution of
A^{\prime}
(resp.
A
). By
5.1.5
applied to

1_{A^{\prime}}
and
1_{A}
we have commutative diagrams

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{P}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{S^{0}(A^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\rm and}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{S^{0}(A)}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

in which the vertical arrows are quasi-isomorphisms. Applying
the bifunctor
T
to the two diagrams we obtain the
commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}_{A})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},S^{0}(A))}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}},\mathbf{P})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}},\mathbf{C}_{A})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}},S^{0}(A))}
\displaystyle{T(S^{0}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})}
\displaystyle{T(S^{0}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}_{A})}

By
6.1.6
all arrows of the previous diagram are quasi-isomorphisms.
In particular,
\mathsf{Tor}_{n}(A^{\prime},A)
can be computed using flat resolutions.

7.2.

For an exact sequence

\mathbf{D}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{C}\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{E}

of complexes in an abelian category we denote by

\partial(u,v):H(\mathbf{E})\to H(\mathbf{D})(-1)

the connecting morphism associated to
(u,v)
. We construct here

\partial(u,v)
when
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{E}
have zero differential.
We follow a method due to Lambek
[
21
, Section 5.5]
, which constructs
the connecting morphism for arbitrary short exact sequences by giving its
image factorization. In this way the naturality of the connecting morphism
is immediate. We refer to
4.1
for notations.

7.2.1.

We have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\displaystyle{{Z(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{p}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{j}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\delta(1)}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}/B(\mathbf{C})}}

\bar{j}
is induced by

. The sequence

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{i(-1)\bar{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{E}(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

[
4
, §2 n

Claim 1

{\rm Coker}\,H(v)=B(\mathbf{C})(-1)
. We have

{\rm Coker}\,H(v)={\rm Coker}\,H(v)p={\rm Coker}\,vj
. Consider the morphism

; we have

so there

such that

.

\displaystyle{{Z(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

it follows that
{\rm Coker}\,vj=B(\mathbf{C})(-1)
.

Claim 2

{\rm Ker}\,H(u)=B(\mathbf{C})
. We have
{\rm Ker}\,H(u)={\rm Ker}\,\bar{j}H(u)={\rm Ker}\,\pi u
.
Since
vji=H(v)pi=0
there is a unique
m:B(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{D}
such that
um=ji
.
From the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
