\scriptstyle{\pi}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}/B(\mathbf{C})}}

it follows that
{\rm Ker}\,\pi u=B(\mathbf{C})
.

From the two claims we obtain that
\partial(u,v)=m(-1)e
.

7.2.2.

When the sequence
(u,v)
is
(j,\delta)
we have
H(j)=p

and
H(\delta)=0
, so
e
is the identity and
m=i
,
therefore
\partial(j,\delta)=i(-1)
.

7.3.

Consider the standard THC–situation (
6.1
).
Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
(resp.
(\mathbf{C},d)
)
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
).
Recall that we denote by
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

the differential of
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
.
We denote by

\displaystyle i(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):B(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\to Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

\displaystyle j(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\to T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\to H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

\displaystyle\delta(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\to B(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))(-1)

the natural morphisms.

7.3.1.

We have
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(j^{\prime},j)=0
.

Proof.

Let
n
be an integer and let
\sigma^{\prime}_{p,q}:T(Z_{p}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z_{q}(\mathbf{C}))\to T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))_{n}
be coproduct coprojections, where
p+q=n
.
We have

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}T(j^{\prime},j)_{n}\sigma^{\prime}_{p,q}=D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}\sigma_{p,q}T(j^{\prime}_{p},j_{q})=

(\sigma_{p-1,q}T(d^{\prime}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{q})+\sigma_{p,q-1}(-1)^{p}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{p},d_{q}))T(j^{\prime}_{p},j_{q})=0

∎

7.3.2.

From
7.3.1
it follows that there is a unique morphism

z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))\to Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

such that
j(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})=T(j^{\prime},j)
.
One can check that this morphism is natural in both arguments,
so that we obtain a natural transformation

z:T(Z(-),Z(-))\Rightarrow Z(T(-,-)):Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

7.3.3.

If
f:X\to A,g:Y\to A
are morphisms in an abelian category, we denote by
(f,g)

the induced morphism
X\oplus Y\to A
. One has
{\rm Im}\,(f,g)\cong{\rm Im}\,f\cup{\rm Im}\,g
.

7.3.4.

We claim that the diagram

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(1),Z(\mathbf{C}))\oplus T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(T(i^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}(1),Z(\mathbf{C})),T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i\delta(1)))}
\scriptstyle{(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(1),j),T(j^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(1)\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(j^{\prime},j)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})^{\#}}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}}

where
\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})
has been defined in
6.1.1
,
is commutative.

Step 1

We show that
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})^{\#}T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(1),j)=T(j^{\prime},j)T(i^{\prime}\delta^{\prime}(1),Z(\mathbf{C}))
. It suffices to show
that
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},j)=T(d^{\prime},j)
.
Since
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},j)
is a morphism we have (
4.1
)

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},j)=T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},j)(-1)D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))
.
Since
Z(\mathbf{C})
has zero differential we have

D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))=T(d^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))

and the equality follows.

Step 2

We show that
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})^{\#}T(j^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(1)\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})=T(j^{\prime},j)T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i\delta(1))
. Since
T(j,\mathbf{C})
is a morphism
we have
D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})^{\#}T(j^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(1)=T(j^{\prime},\mathbf{C})D(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})^{\#}
. Since
Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

has zero differential we have
D(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})^{\#}\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})=T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),d^{\#})

and the equality follows. Combining now the two steps we obtain the
commutativity of the diagram.

7.3.5.

Let
u^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
(resp.
u:A\to B
) be an epimorphism
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}
). Then the diagram

\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(A^{\prime},u)}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},A)}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},B)}
\displaystyle{{T(B^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(B^{\prime},u)}
\displaystyle{{T(B^{\prime},B)}}

is a pushout. Consequently, if
i^{\prime}:{\rm Ker}\,u^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}
and
i:{\rm Ker}\,u\to A

are the natural morphisms, there is an exact sequence

(11)

\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,T(i^{\prime},A)\cup{\rm Im}\,T(A^{\prime},i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},u)}
\displaystyle{{T(B^{\prime},B)}}

Proof.

Let
f:T(B^{\prime},A)\to A^{\prime\prime}
and
g:T(A^{\prime},B)\to A^{\prime\prime}
be
such that
fT(u^{\prime},A)=gT(A^{\prime},u)
. By adjunction we
have a commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{H(B,A^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(u,A^{\prime\prime})}
\displaystyle{{B^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\flat}}
\displaystyle{{H(A,A^{\prime\prime})}}

where
f^{\flat}
and
g^{\flat}
are the adjoints of
f
and
g
, respectively.
The map
H(u,A^{\prime\prime})
is a monomorphism since
H(-,A^{\prime\prime})
is a right adjoint.
Since in an abelian category we have the factorization system
(epimorphisms, monomorphisms) we have a unique dotted diagonal filler
h^{\flat}

in the previous diagram. The adjoint of this diagonal filler is a morphism

h:T(B^{\prime},B)\to A^{\prime\prime}
having the property that
f=hT(B^{\prime},u)
and
g=hT(u^{\prime},B)
.
Since
h^{\flat}
is unique, so is
h
with this property. Therefore
the required diagram is a pushout. For the second part, consider the diagram

\displaystyle{{T({\rm Ker}\,u^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(i^{\prime},A)}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},{\rm Ker}\,u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(A^{\prime},i)}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(A^{\prime},u)}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},A)}
\displaystyle{{T(A^{\prime},B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},B)}
\displaystyle{{T(B^{\prime},A)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(B^{\prime},u)}
\displaystyle{{T(B^{\prime},B)}}

We have

T(A^{\prime},B)=T(A^{\prime},A)/{\rm Im}\,T(A^{\prime},i)\ {\rm and}\ T(B^{\prime},A)=T(A^{\prime},A)/{\rm Im}\,T(i^{\prime},A)

The existence of the exact sequence (
11
) is then a
consequence of the first part and of
[
3
, Corollary 1.7.5]
.
∎

The exact sequence
7.3.5
(
11
) is a familiar one in
the theory of modules over a ring where it says that, if
A
is a ring,

M^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}M\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}M^{\prime\prime}

an exact sequence of right
A
-modules and

N^{\prime}\overset{s}{\rightarrow}N\overset{t}{\twoheadrightarrow}N^{\prime\prime}

an exact sequence of left
A
-modules,
then
v\otimes t
is surjective and its kernel is equal to

{\rm Im}\,(u\otimes 1_{N})+{\rm Im}\,(1_{M}\otimes s)
.

7.3.6.

We apply the naturality of the image factorization to the
commutative diagram
7.3.4
; from
7.3.3

we obtain a unique morphism

b(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):{\rm Im}\,T(i^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))\cup{\rm Im}\,T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i)\to B(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

such that
j(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})i(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})b(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})=T(j^{\prime},j)k(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
, where

k(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):{\rm Im}\,T(i^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))\cup{\rm Im}\,T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i)\rightarrowtail T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))

is the monomorphism of the image factorization of the top arrow of diagram
7.3.4
.
From
7.3.5
the top row of the commutative solid arrow diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Im}\,T(i^{\prime},Z(\mathbf{C}))\cup{\rm Im}\,T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\scriptstyle{b(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\scriptstyle{T(p^{\prime},p)}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{B(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}}

is exact, therefore there is a unique morphism

(12)

\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}):T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))\to H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))

such that
\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(p^{\prime},p)=p(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
.
This morphism is natural in both arguments. For example, let
u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
be a morphism.
Form the cube diagram

\displaystyle{{T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{D}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(p^{\prime},p_{\mathbf{D}})}
\scriptstyle{z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(u)))}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{D}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(u)))}
\displaystyle{{Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}
\displaystyle{{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u))}
\displaystyle{{T(Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(p^{\prime},p_{\mathbf{C}})}
\scriptstyle{z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{{Z(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}
\displaystyle{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}

The left face of the cube commutes by the naturality of
z
(
7.3.2
), the top and
bottom faces commute by the construction of
\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})
and

\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
, the back face commutes because
T
is a bifunctor.
Since
T(p^{\prime},p_{\mathbf{D}})
is an epimorphism, it follows that the right face commutes.
Thus, we obtain a natural transformation

\gamma:T(H(-),H(-))\Rightarrow H(T(-,-)):Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

For every integer
r
the diagram

\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}(r)))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r))}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}(r)))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))(r)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(r)}
\displaystyle{{H(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))(r)}}

commutes, where
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
has been defined in
6.1.1
.

7.3.7.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4. The morphism

\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
(
7.3.6
(
12
)) is
an isomorphism if
\mathbf{C}
is degreewise flat with zero differential.

Proof.

From the proof of

we have that the morphisms

(

) and

hence so is

from the way it was constructed.
∎

7.3.8.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4,
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
has zero differential
and there is an exact sequence
\mathbf{D}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{C}\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{E}
,
where
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{E}
have zero differential and

\mathbf{E}
is degreewise flat. Then

\partial(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u),T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v))=\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\partial(u,v))

Proof.

By
3.1.6
(1) we have the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{E})}}

where the first and last complexes have zero differential.
Using the naturality of the morphism
\Sigma
(
6.1.1
)
and
7.2.1
we have

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)(-1)\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\partial(u,v))T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)=

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u(-1))T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\partial(u,v))T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)=

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u(-1))T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},m(-1)e)T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)=

\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u(-1)m(-1)ev)=\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d)

and

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)(-1)\partial(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u),T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v))T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)=

j(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(-1)i(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})(-1)\delta(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})=D(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

By the diagram in
6.1.1
and the fact that
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)
is a
monomorphism and
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},v)
is an epimorphism we obtain the desired equality.
∎

[

, §4 n

Suppose that

\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

.

.

({\rm Ker}\,T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i(-1)))_{n}=\underset{p+q=n}{\bigoplus}{\rm Ker}\,T(H_{p}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i_{q-1})

.

7.3.14.

[
4
, §4 n
o
7 cor. 4]

Suppose that
\mathbf{C},H(\mathbf{C})
are degreewise flat and

\mathbf{C}_{n}=0
for

; then
\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the exact sequences

B_{n}(\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow H_{n}(\mathbf{C})

and

Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})

Since

is flat we have, using
3.1.5
,
the implications (

flat)
\Rightarrow
(
Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})
flat)

\Rightarrow
(
B_{n}(\mathbf{C})
flat); we obtain that

B(\mathbf{C})
and
Z(\mathbf{C})
are degreewise flat.
We apply
T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),-)
to the exact sequence

B(\mathbf{C})(-1)\overset{i(-1)}{\rightarrowtail}Z(\mathbf{C})(-1)\twoheadrightarrow H(\mathbf{C})(-1)
; we obtain from

(1)
the exact sequence

\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),B(\mathbf{C})(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),i(-1))}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C})(-1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{T(H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C})(-1))}}

From

we conclude that
\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

is an isomorphism.
∎

7.3.15.

[
4
, §4 n
o
7 cor. 5]
The functor

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
preserves quasi-isomorphisms between complexes
that are degreewise flat and zero in negative degrees.

Proof.

Let
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
be a quasi-isomorphism between complexes
that are degreewise flat and zero in negative degrees. Then
Con(u)
is exact,
degreewise flat and zero in negative degrees, hence by
7.3.14

\gamma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Con(u))
is an isomorphism. This implies that

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},Con(u))
is exact, therefore so is
Con(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u))
by

6.1.2
.
∎

.

8.
Orthogonality relations for complexes

8.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category,
\mathcal{K}
a class
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathfrak{K}
a class of objects of

Ch(\mathcal{A})
. We denote by
\mathcal{P}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}
)
the class of projective (resp. injective) objects of
\mathcal{A}
. We denote
by
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
(resp.
{}^{\perp_{e}}\mathfrak{K}
)
the class of complexes
\mathbf{C}
for which

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,\mathbf{C}):Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\to Ch(\mathrm{Ab})

(resp.

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},-):Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathrm{Ab}))

preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathfrak{K}
. We have

Ob(\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{e}}=Ch(\mathcal{I})
,

{}^{\perp_{e}}Ob(\mathcal{A})=Ch(\mathcal{P})
and

{}^{\perp_{e}}(Ob(\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{e}})=Ob(\mathcal{A})=(^{\perp_{e}}Ob(\mathcal{A}))^{\perp_{e}}
. From
4.2

the classes
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
and
{}^{\perp_{e}}\mathfrak{K}

are closed under translates.

8.1.1.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
.

(1) The following are equivalent:

(a)
\mathbf{C}\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
;

(b)
\mathbf{C}\in Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{e}}
;

(c) for every exact sequence of complexes

\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{A}\overset{v}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}

with
\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v\in Ch(\mathcal{K})
the sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{C})\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

is exact, where
\bar{u}=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{C})
and

\bar{v}=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(v,\mathbf{C})
.

(1bis) The following are equivalent:

(a)
\mathbf{C}\in Ch(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})
;

(b)
\mathbf{C}\in^{\perp_{e}}Ch(\mathcal{K})
;

(c) for every exact sequence of complexes

\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{A}\overset{v}{\rightarrow}\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}

with
{\rm Ker}\,u\in Ch(\mathcal{K})
the sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{A}^{\prime})\overset{\bar{u}}{\to}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{A})\overset{\bar{v}}{\to}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime})

is exact, where
\bar{u}=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},u)
and

\bar{v}=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},v)
.

Proof.

(1) (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) Let
\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{X}
be an exact sequence with
\mathbf{X}

degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
. For all integers
n,p
we have then the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{X}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{B}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{A}_{p},\mathbf{C}_{p+n})

The claim follows from the construction of
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
4.2
).

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c) We factor
v
as
\mathbf{A}\overset{q}{\twoheadrightarrow}{\rm Im}\,v\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime}
. By assumption

\bar{j}:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{C})\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}({\rm Im}\,v,\mathbf{C})

is an epimorphism. It then suffices to show that the sequence

(\bar{q},\bar{u})
is exact. Since exactness can be checked degreewise,
the argument is the same as for
1.2.1
(1).

(c)
\Rightarrow
(a) Let
n
be an integer and
A^{\prime}\overset{u}{\rightarrow}A\overset{v}{\rightarrow}A^{\prime\prime}
an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
with

A^{\prime\prime}/{\rm Im}\,v\in\mathcal{K}
. We have then the exact sequence
of complexes

S^{0}(A^{\prime})\overset{u}{\rightarrow}S^{0}(A)\overset{v}{\rightarrow}S^{0}(A^{\prime\prime})

with
S^{0}(A^{\prime\prime})/{\rm Im}\,v\in Ch(\mathcal{K})
. The claim follows from

4.2
and
1.2.1
(1).
∎

8.1.2.

The pair
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory if and only if

Ch(\mathcal{K})=^{\perp_{e}}(Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{e}})
.

8.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
\mathfrak{K}

a class of objects of
Ch(\mathcal{A})
. We define

\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}=\{\mathbf{D},\ H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}))=0{\rm\ for\ all}\ \mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}\}

and

{}^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K}=\{\mathbf{D},\ H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{D},\mathbf{C}))=0{\rm\ for\ all}\ \mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}\}

We denote by
Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A}))_{0}
the class of complexes with zero differential.
Then
Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A}))^{\perp_{h}}=Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A}))_{0}^{\perp_{h}}

consists of complexes that are homotopic to zero.

8.2.1.

(1) If
\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{L}
are two classes of objects of
Ch(\mathcal{A})
,

\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{L}

implies
\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{h}}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
.

(2)
\mathfrak{K}\subset{}^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}})
,
with equality if and only if there is a class
\mathcal{S}
of complexes such that

\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
and

{}^{\perp_{h}}\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{K}
.

(3)
{}^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K}=^{\perp_{h}}((^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K})^{\perp_{h}})
.

(4)
\mathfrak{K}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
is the class of complexes
that are homotopic to zero.

(5) The class
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
is closed under homotopy
equivalences, translates and retracts.

(6) Let
u:\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{D}
be a morphism.

(a) If any two of
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
and
Con(u)
belong to

\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
then so does the third.

(b) If
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
and

Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}
then
u
is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Part 3 follows from 2 by taking
\mathcal{S}=\mathfrak{K}
.

(6) (a) The exact sequence (
4.1.3
)

\mathbf{D}\overset{\pi}{\rightarrowtail}Con(u)\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(-1)
is degreewise split, therefore
(
4.2.1
) for each
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
we have
the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},Con(u))\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}(-1))

We concluse using
4.2
and part 5. For (b), using (a) and
part 4 we obtain that
Con(u)
is homotopic to zero, hence
(
4.1.3
)
u
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

8.2.2.

[
23
, Proposition 1.4]
Suppose that
\mathfrak{K}
is closed
under translates. Recall (
4.1.3
) that
\sim
denotes the
homotopy relation for complexes. For a complex

\mathbf{D}
in
\mathcal{A}
consider the following statements:

(1)
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
;

(2) for every morphism
u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{A}
with
Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}

there is a morphism
l:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{D}
such that
lu\sim 1_{\mathbf{D}}
;

(3) for every exact sequence

\mathbf{A}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}

with
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and every morphism
f:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{D}

there is
l:\mathbf{B}\to\mathbf{D}
such that
lu\sim f
;

(4) for every morphism
u
with cone in
\mathfrak{K}
,

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{D})
is a quasi-isomorphism.

We have
(1)\Leftrightarrow(4),(1)\Rightarrow(2)
and
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
.
In the case
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
the morphism
l
is unique up to homotopy.
If the cone of a monomorphism with cokernel in
\mathfrak{K}
is in
\mathfrak{K}

then the reverse implications hold.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(4)

This follows from the natural isomorphism (
4.2
)

\Sigma(u,\mathbf{D}):\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Con(u)(1),\mathbf{D})\cong Con(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{D}))

(4)\Rightarrow(1)

Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and consider the zero morphism

0:0\to\mathbf{C}
. Then
Con(0)=\mathbf{C}
, hence

0:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\to 0
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
Let
u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{A}
be a morphism with

Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}
. Consider the exact sequence
\mathbf{A}\overset{\pi}{\rightarrowtail}Con(u)\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{D}(-1)
. Then
\delta
is homotopic to zero;
let
s
be a homotopy between
0
and
\delta
, so that

s_{n}:Con(u)_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n}
. One can check that

s\pi:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{D}
is a morphism. We define

t_{n}:\mathbf{D}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n+1}
as the composite

\mathbf{D}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n}\oplus\mathbf{A}_{n+1}\overset{-s_{n+1}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{D}_{n+1}
, where the first arrow is the
coprojection morphism. One can check that
t
is a homotopy
between
(-s\pi)u
and
1_{\mathbf{D}}
.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
(uniqueness of
l
) We apply
4.2.2
to

u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{D}
; we obtain
that
-H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{D})(-1))
is an isomorphism.
Therefore (
4.2
) for each integer
n
the morphism

Ch(\mathcal{A})(u,\mathbf{D}(n-1))/\sim:Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D}(n-1))/\sim\to Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}(n-1))/\sim

induced by
l\mapsto lu
, is an isomorphism. Taking
n=1
we obtain the
uniqueness of
l
.

(3)\Rightarrow(2)
Let
u:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{A}
be a morphism with

Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}
. From
4.1.3
we have the factorization

\mathbf{D}\overset{\tilde{u}}{\to}Cyl(u)\overset{\beta}{\to}\mathbf{A}

of
u
and the exact sequence
\mathbf{D}\overset{\tilde{u}}{\rightarrowtail}Cyl(u)\overset{\tilde{\pi}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Con(u)
. Then
there is
l:Cyl(u)\to\mathbf{D}
such that
l\tilde{u}\sim 1_{\mathbf{D}}
.
If
\alpha
is a homotopy inverse of
\beta
(
loc. cit.
)
then
l\alpha u=l\alpha\beta\tilde{u}\sim l\tilde{u}\sim 1_{\mathbf{D}}
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and

u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a morphism. Consider the exact
sequence
\mathbf{D}\overset{\pi}{\rightarrowtail}Con(u)\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{C}(-1)
. By
assumptions
Con(\pi)\in\mathfrak{K}
, therefore there is

l:Con(u)\to\mathbf{D}
such that
l\pi\sim 1_{\mathbf{D}}
.
Since (
4.1.3
)
u=\beta\tilde{u}
and
\pi=\tilde{\pi}\alpha

we have
u\sim l\pi u=l\tilde{\pi}\alpha\beta\tilde{u}\sim l\tilde{\pi}\tilde{u}=0
.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
Let
\mathbf{A}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
be an exact sequence
with
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
f:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{D}

a morphism. Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}}}

where
PO
means pushout. By assumption there is

l:PO\to\mathbf{D}
such that
lu^{\prime}\sim 1_{\mathbf{D}}
; then
lf^{\prime}u\sim f
.
∎

8.2.3.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{K}={}^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}})
.

(1) Let
u:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}
be a morphism such that

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{D})
is a quasi-isomorphism
for all
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
; then
Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}
.

(2) The class of morphisms with cone in
\mathfrak{K}
has the two
out of three property.

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
. From the natural
isomorphism (
4.2
)

\Sigma(u,\mathbf{D}):\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Con(u)(1),\mathbf{D})\cong Con(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(u,\mathbf{D}))

and the assumption it follows that
Con(u)(1)\in{}^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}})
, hence by
8.2.1
(5) we have

Con(u)\in\mathfrak{K}
. Part 2 follows from 1, the equivalence

(1)\Leftrightarrow(4)
of
8.2.2
and the two out
of three property of quasi-isomorphisms.
∎

We denote by
ex(\mathcal{A})
the class of exact complexes in
an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
.

8.2.4.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has either enough injective objects or a set

(E_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators. Then
ex(\mathcal{A})=^{\perp_{h}}(ex(\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{h}})
.

Proof.

We shall use
8.2.1
(2). Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
injective objects. We put
\mathcal{S}=\{S^{r}(E)\}
, where
r
is an
integer and
E
is injective. We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset ex(\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{h}}
. Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be an exact complex
and
E
and injective object; then by
4.2
and
1.2.1
(1)
the complex
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r-},E)
is exact.
We show that
{}^{\perp_{h}}\mathcal{S}\subset ex(\mathcal{A})
.
Let
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})\in^{\perp_{h}}\mathcal{S}
; then by
4.2

the complex
\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r-},E)
is exact for all injective objects

E
of
\mathcal{A}
and all integers
r
, hence (
1.1.2
)

(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is exact. When
\mathcal{A}
has a set
(E_{i})_{i\in I}

of injective cogenerators we put
\mathcal{S}=\{S^{0}(E_{i})\}
.
∎

8.2.5.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{K}
is closed under translates.

(1)
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
; when

\mathfrak{K}=Ch(\mathcal{K})
, where
\mathcal{K}
is a class of objects
of
\mathcal{A}
, we have
Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{e}}\cap Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{h}}=Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
.

(2) If
{}^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})\subset\mathfrak{K}
then

\mathfrak{K}={}^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}})
and

(\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory.

(3) Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}
consist of those
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}

for which the functor

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,\mathbf{D}):Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\to Ch(\mathrm{Ab})

preserves epimorphisms with kernel in
\mathfrak{K}
; then

\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}

and
\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
be an
exact sequence with
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
. We have then the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D})

Since
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is exact
and
Z_{0}
preserves epimorphisms with exact kernel we have the exact sequence

Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}))\rightarrowtail Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D}))\twoheadrightarrow Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D}))

From
4.2
it follows that
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
.
Let now
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp},\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}

and
u:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
a morphism. Then the exact sequence

\mathbf{D}\overset{\pi}{\rightarrowtail}Con(u)\overset{\delta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{C}(-1)
splits, therefore
\delta
has a
section
v
. We define
s_{n-1}:\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\to\mathbf{D}_{n}

as the composite
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\overset{v_{n}}{\to}\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\oplus\mathbf{D}_{n}\to\mathbf{D}_{n}
,
where the second arrow is the projection morphism. The fact that

v
is a retract implies that
s
is a homotopy between
0
and
u
,
hence
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{K}=Ch(\mathcal{K})
. Since

Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}\subset Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

we obtain from
8.1.1
(1) and the first part the desired equality.
Part 2 follows from 1 and
8.2.1
(2) by taking

\mathcal{S}=\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
. Part 3 is similar to 1.
∎

8.2.6.

[
14
, Proposition 3.7]
Let
(\mathfrak{L},\mathfrak{L}^{\perp})
be a
cotorsion theory in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathfrak{L}
closed under translates.

(1) If
\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{L}\cap^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
and

\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}

then
(\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory and

\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
.
If
\mathfrak{L}
is left exact then so is
\mathfrak{K}
.

(2) Suppose that
\mathfrak{L}=^{\perp_{e}}(\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}})
,

\mathfrak{K}
is closed under translates,
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{L}
and
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}\subset\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}
.
If
(\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory then

\mathfrak{K}=\mathfrak{L}\cap^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}){\rm\ and}\ \mathfrak{K}^{\perp}=\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}

Proof.

(1) One can check that
\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
is closed under translates; it
then follows from
8.2.5
(1) that
{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})\subset^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
. Also, since

\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{L}
we have
{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})\subset^{\perp}(\mathfrak{L}^{\perp})=\mathfrak{L}
, hence

(\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
is a cotorsion theory. For the second
part it suffices, by
8.2.5
(1), to show that

\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
.
Let
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}\cap\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}

and
\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}

be an exact sequence with
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
.
Since
\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}

have the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D})

Since
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is exact
and
Z_{0}
preserves epimorphisms with exact kernel we have the exact sequence

Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}))\rightarrowtail Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D}))\twoheadrightarrow Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D}))

and we conclude by
4.2
. Suppose now that
\mathfrak{L}
is left exact.
Let
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{D}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}

be an exact sequence with
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}

and let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
. Since
\mathbf{D}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}

and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}
we have the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{D},\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{D}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

Since
\mathfrak{K}\subset^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})

the two left complexes of the previous exact sequence are exact,
therefore so is the third.

(2) By part 1 it suffices to show the first equality. The inclusion

\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{L}\cap^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})

follows as in the proof of part 1. Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{L}\cap^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp})
and

\mathbf{D}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{A}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{B}

be an exact sequence with
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp}
.
Since
\mathfrak{L}=^{\perp_{e}}(\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}})

have the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{A})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{B})

Since
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D})
is exact
and
Z_{0}
preserves epimorphisms with exact kernel we have the exact sequence

Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}))\rightarrowtail Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{A}))\twoheadrightarrow Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{B}))

and we conclude by
4.2
.
∎

For an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{K}
a class
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
we denote by
ex(\mathcal{K})
the class of
exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}
that are degreewise in
\mathcal{K}

and by
ex[\mathcal{K}]
the class of exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}

that have cycles in
\mathcal{K}
.

8.2.7.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a nonempty class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
.

(1)
ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]=ex(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap\{S^{0}(X),\ X\in\mathcal{K}\}^{\perp_{h}}
.

(2)
[
24
, Theorem 2.5]
,
[
6
, Proposition 3.7]

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects, meaning
that for each
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A

with
X\in\mathcal{K}
, and that
\mathcal{K}
is closed under extensions.
Then
\{S^{0}(X),\ X\in\mathcal{K}\}^{\perp_{h}}\subset ex(\mathcal{A})\cap ex[\mathcal{K}]^{\perp_{h}}
. If, moreover,

\mathcal{K}
is left exact then we have equality and

ex[\mathcal{K}]\cap Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex[\mathcal{K}]^{\perp_{h}}=ex[\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
and
X\in\mathcal{K}
.
Then
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(X),\mathbf{C})
is exact
by
1.2.1
((3) and (1bis)). Conversely, let
\mathbf{C}
be an
exact complex that is degreewise in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and such
that
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(X),\mathbf{C})
is
exact for all
X\in\mathcal{K}
. Since
ex(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap\{S^{0}(X),\ X\in\mathcal{K}\}^{\perp_{h}}
is closed under translates
it suffices to show that
Z_{0}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
Let
A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow X
be an
exact sequence with
X\in\mathcal{K}
. Applying the functor

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,\mathbf{C})

to the exact sequence
S^{0}(A)\rightarrowtail S^{0}(B)\twoheadrightarrow S^{0}(X)
and using
8.1.1
(1)
we obtain the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(X),\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(B),\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(A),\mathbf{C})

Since
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(X),\mathbf{C})
is exact
and
Z_{0}
preserves epimorphisms with exact kernel we have the exact sequence

Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(X),\mathbf{C}))\rightarrowtail Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(B),\mathbf{C}))\twoheadrightarrow Z_{0}(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{0}(A),\mathbf{C}))

and we conclude by
4.2
and adjunction (
4.1
).

(2) Let
\mathbf{C}\in\{S^{0}(X),\ X\in\mathcal{K}\}^{\perp_{h}}
.
By
4.2
and
1.1.2
(1) we obtain that
\mathbf{C}
is
exact. Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
. Let
i
be an integer
and consider the complex
\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
(
4.2.4
).
The complexes
\mathcal{A}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})

and
\mathcal{A}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})_{i},\mathbf{C})
(
4.2.3
)
are exact, hence
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C})

is exact. Using
4.2.4
we conclude that
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact. Assume now that
\mathcal{K}

is left exact. Let
\mathbf{C}\in ex(\mathcal{A})\cap ex[\mathcal{K}]^{\perp_{h}}

and
f:\mathbf{X}_{0}\to B_{0}(\mathbf{C})
be a morphism with

\mathbf{X}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
. Choose an epimorphism
\mathbf{X}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\times_{B_{0}(\mathbf{C})}\mathbf{X}_{0}
with
\mathbf{X}_{1}\in\mathcal{K}

and put
B_{1}(\mathbf{X})={\rm Ker}\,(\mathbf{X}_{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{X}_{0})
.
Then repeat this with the natural morphism
B_{1}(\mathbf{X})\to B_{1}(\mathbf{C})

in place of
f
; continuing in this way we obtain
\mathbf{X}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
with

\mathbf{X}_{n}=0
for
n<0
and a morphism
u:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
.
Then
u
is homotopic to zero, therefore there is
s_{0}:\mathbf{X}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}_{1}

such that
\delta_{1}s_{0}=f
. This implies that the adjoint transpose
f^{\#}
of

f
is homotopic to zero, which is what we wanted to prove. The last part of 2
follows from the preceding considerations and part 1.
∎

8.3.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC–situation. Consider the
standard THC–situation (
6.1
)

T:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

H:Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

C:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

associated to it. Let
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}
) be a
class of complexes in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
) that is
closed under isomorphisms and translates. We define

\mathfrak{K}=T(\mathfrak{K}^{\prime},-)^{-1}(\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime})=\{\mathbf{C}\in Ob(Ch(\mathcal{A})),\ T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}{\rm\ for\ all}\ \mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}\}

8.3.1.

(1) If
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}
is invariant under homotopy equivalences then so is
\mathfrak{K}
.

(2)
\mathfrak{K}
is closed under translates. If, moreover,
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}
is closed under
extensions then
\mathfrak{K}
is closed under cones of morphisms between its objects.

(3) For a complex
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}
the following are equivalent:

(a)
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
;

(b)
T(-,\mathbf{C})
sends a monomorphism with cone in
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}
to a morphism
with cone in
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}
.

(4) If
\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime}=^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime\perp_{h}})
then

\mathfrak{K}=^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}})
.

Proof.

(2) The closure of
\mathfrak{K}
under cones of morphisms between its objects
follows from
6.1.2
.

(3) (a)
\Rightarrow
(b) This follows from
6.1.2
. (b)
\Rightarrow
(a)
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}
and consider the zero morphism
0:0\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
;
then
Con(0)=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and the assertion follows since
T(-,\mathbf{C})
is additive.

(4) We shall use
8.2.1
(2). We put
\mathcal{S}=\{C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime\perp_{h}}\}
.
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime\perp_{h}}
.
We show that
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{h}}
. Let

\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
. From
6.1.9
we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime}))

and the inclusion follows from this. We show that

{}^{\perp_{h}}\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{K}
. If

\mathbf{C}\in^{\perp_{h}}\mathcal{S}
then the previous
isomorphism implies that
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\in^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\prime\prime\perp_{h}})
, therefore
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
.
∎

8.3.2.

A complex
\mathbf{C}
(resp.
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
) in
\mathcal{A}

(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
) is called
K
-
flat

[
23
]
,
or h-flat, if
T(-,\mathbf{C})
(resp.
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
)
preserves exact complexes. By
8.3.1
(3) this is
equivalent to
T(-,\mathbf{C})
sending monomorphisms
that are quasi-isomorphisms to quasi-isomorphisms.
Let
u^{\prime}
be a quasi-isomorphism in
Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
Factor it (
4.1.3
) into
u^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}\tilde{u^{\prime}}
,
where
\beta^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence and
\tilde{u^{\prime}}

is a monomorphism. Since
T(-,\mathbf{C})
is additive it
preserves homotopy equivalences, therefore by the
two out of three property of quasi-isomorphisms we
have that
\mathbf{C}
is h-flat if and only if

T(-,\mathbf{C})
preserves quasi-isomorphisms.
We denote by
\mathfrak{F}_{h}
the class of
h-flat complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.

8.3.3.

(1) Suppose
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has either
enough injectives or a set
(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators.

(a)
\mathfrak{F}_{h}=^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{F}_{h}^{\perp_{h}})
.

(b) Let
\mathbf{P}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. When

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has enough injectives then
\mathbf{P}
is h-flat if and only if

H(\mathbf{P},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}))\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{\perp_{h}}
for all injective
objects
E^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has
a set
(E^{\prime\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of injective cogenerators then
\mathbf{P}
is
h-flat if and only if
H(\mathbf{P},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}_{i}))\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{\perp_{h}}
for all
i\in I
.

(2) If
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies AB4 and
\mathcal{A}
has enough flat objects
then
\mathfrak{F}_{h}^{\perp_{h}}\subset ex(\mathcal{A})
.

(3) If
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
hs enough injectives and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}

have enough flat objects then

{}^{\perp_{h}}ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]\subset\mathfrak{F}_{h}=^{\perp_{h}}C(ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),S^{0}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}))

where
C(ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),S^{0}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime\prime}))
consists of objects of the form

C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}))
, with
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
exact in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and

E^{\prime\prime}
injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

(1) Part (a) follows from
8.2.4
and
8.3.1
(4). We prove (b).
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be an exact complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
.
By
6.1.9
and
4.2
we have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P},S^{0}(A^{\prime\prime})))\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}),S^{0}(A^{\prime\prime}))=\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})_{0-},A^{\prime\prime})

The required equivalences follow from this by putting
A^{\prime\prime}=E^{\prime\prime}
or
A^{\prime\prime}=E^{\prime\prime}_{i}

and using
1.1.2
(1) or
1.1.1
(1).

(2) Let
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{F}_{h}^{\perp_{h}}
. From
6.1.5

we have that
S^{0}(P)
is h-flat for every flat object
P
of
\mathcal{A}
,
therefore (
4.2
)
\mathcal{A}(P,\mathbf{D})
is exact.
Since
\mathcal{A}
has enough flat objects, it follows (
1.1.2
)
that
\mathbf{D}
is exact.

(3) We show that
{}^{\perp_{h}}ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]\subset\mathfrak{F}_{h}
. Let
\mathbf{P}\in^{\perp_{h}}ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
and
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.
By
1.1.2
(1) it suffices to show that

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}),S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}))

is exact for all injective objects
E^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
. By
6.1.9

it suffices to show that
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}))=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{0-},E^{\prime\prime})\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
, where
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{0-},E^{\prime\prime})_{n}=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{-n},E^{\prime\prime})
has differential
C((-1)^{n+1}d^{\prime}_{-n+1},E^{\prime\prime})
.
By
3.1.3
the complex
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{0-},E^{\prime\prime})
is exact, so

B_{n}(C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{0-},E^{\prime\prime}))=C(B_{-n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),E^{\prime\prime})
for all
integers
n
. Then from the proof of
3.1.4
we have that

C(B_{-n-1}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),E^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
. The
displayed equality is straightforward.

∎

9.
The universal coefficients theorem for complexes in abelian categories

9.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
\mathfrak{K}

a class of complexes in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathfrak{K}=^{\perp_{e}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}})
(
8.1
).
Let
(\mathbf{C},d),(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be complexes in
\mathcal{A}
.
Recall (
4.2
) that we denote by
D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

the differential of
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
.
We denote by

\displaystyle i(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):B(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle j(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

the natural morphisms. We have a natural in both arguments morphism

h(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

defined as
h(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(f)=(H_{p}(f))_{p}
. One checks

[
4
, §5 n
o
1 p. 82]
that

h(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})i(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=0
, hence there
is a unique morphism

\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

such that
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})p(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=h(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
. Since
h
is natural and
p(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

is an epimorphism it follows that
\lambda
is natural in both arguments.

9.1.1.

Suppose
d=0
. Then
D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},d^{\prime})
and we identify

(Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})),j(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))=(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})),\bar{j^{\prime}})

so that we have
h(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},p^{\prime})(=\bar{p^{\prime}})
.
Also, the adjoint transpose
D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})^{\#}
of

D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},d^{\prime\#})
.
From the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{Z(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\delta^{\prime}(1)}}
\scriptstyle{D(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})^{\#}=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},d^{\prime\#})}
\displaystyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i^{\prime}}}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{j^{\prime}}}

we obtain that there is a morphism

b_{2}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}):B(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

such that
\overline{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}b_{2}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=j(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})i(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
; it follows
that
\bar{i^{\prime}}b_{2}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=i(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
.

(1) Suppose
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
;
then
b_{2}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism.

(2) Suppose
\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}
and
B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
; then
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(1) Applying
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},-)
to the exact sequence

(j^{\prime}(1),\delta^{\prime}(1))
(
4.1
) we obtain that
\overline{\delta^{\prime}(1)}

is an epimorphism, hence so is
b_{2}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
.

(2) Applying
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},-)
to the exact
sequence
B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{p^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
we obtain the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\overset{\bar{i^{\prime}}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\overset{\bar{p^{\prime}}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

Using part 1, this implies that
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

is an isomorphism.
∎

9.1.2.

Suppose
B(\mathbf{C})\in\mathfrak{K}
,
d^{\prime}=0
and

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
. Applying

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
to the
exact sequence
(j,\delta)
we obtain the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{\bar{\delta}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{\bar{j}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

where the outer complexes have zero differential. We want to calculate the connecting
morphism

\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j}):H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)

associated to the previous exact sequence. From the standard construction

[
4
, §2 n
o
3 p. 29]
of the connecting
morphism it follows that we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})}
\scriptstyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(1))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}}

where
\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
has been defined in
4.2
.

9.1.3.

Suppose
B(\mathbf{C})\in\mathfrak{K}
and
Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
. As in
9.1.2
we want to calculate
the connecting morphism

\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j}):H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))\to H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)

associated to the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{\bar{\delta}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\overset{\bar{j}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

Using the morphism
j^{\prime}:Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and the naturality
of the connecting morphism we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})}
\scriptstyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),j^{\prime}))(-1)}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}}

By the naturality of the morphism
\Sigma(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

we have the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))(-1)}
\scriptstyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),j^{\prime}))(-1)}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(1))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime})(1))(-1)}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(1))(-1)}}

We claim that
H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})=H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))
. Putting the two previous
diagrams side by side we obtain a diagram whose top horizontal composite morphism
is
H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))
by
9.1.2
.
By
9.1.1
we have
j(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime})
, hence

H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))=p(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is an epimorphism. Using
9.1.2

we have

H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))=

H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))(-1)\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})=

H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})))=

H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),j^{\prime}))

hence the claim.

9.1.4.

Suppose that
B(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C})\in\mathfrak{K}

and
B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}
.
Then we have an exact sequence

\displaystyle{{{\rm Coker}\,\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i(-1),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}}

Proof.

Applying
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
to
the exact sequence
(j,\delta)
we obtain the exact sequence

(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})
(
9.1.3
). Consider then the top
diagram on the next page, where the unlabeled top horizontal arrow
is
H(\Sigma(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)
.

\scriptstyle{H(\bar{\delta})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\bar{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))(-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(H(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(p,H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i,H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(Z(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(1))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})^{\#}}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(\bar{\delta})}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}
\displaystyle{H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(Z(\mathbf{C})(-1),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i(-1),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(B(\mathbf{C})(-1),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))}}

The top sequence is exact from the homology exact sequence associated
to
(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})
and the bottom sequence is exact by the
construction of
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}
(
4.2
).
The left square diagram commutes by the naturality of
\lambda

and the right rectangular diagram commutes by
9.1.3
.
The morphisms
\lambda(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
and

\lambda(B(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
are isomorphisms by

9.1.1
. It follows that
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})

is an epimorphism with
{\rm Ker}\,\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})={\rm Im}\,H(\bar{\delta})
.

Consider now the bottom diagram on the next page,
where the unlabeled top horizontal arrow
is
H(\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))
.
The top sequence is exact from the homology exact sequence
associated to
(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})
. By
9.1.3
we
have
\partial(\bar{\delta},\bar{j})^{\#}H(\Sigma(Z(\mathbf{C}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))=H(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i(-1),\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))
, hence
the rectangular diagram commutes. By
9.1.1
the vertical
morphisms are isomorphisms. It follows that

{\rm Im}\,H(\bar{\delta})={\rm Coker}\,\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(i(-1),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))

∎

9.1.5.

Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a cotorsion theory in

\mathcal{A}
.

(1)
[
4
, §5 n
o
6 cor. 4]

Suppose that
B(\mathbf{C}),H(\mathbf{C})\in Ch(\mathcal{K})

and
B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
;
then
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism.

(2) The complex

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is exact
if either (a)
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
(
4.1.2
) and

B(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Z(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\in Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

or (b)
B(\mathbf{C}),Z(\mathbf{C})\in Ch(\mathcal{K})
and

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
.

Proof.

(1) Put
\mathfrak{K}=Ch(\mathcal{K})
in
9.1.4
; then

\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}}=Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
(
8.1.1
)
and
\mathfrak{K}=^{\perp_{e}}(\mathfrak{K}^{\perp_{e}})
(
8.1.2
),
hence we have the exact sequence
9.1.4
. Applying

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}))
to the
exact sequence
B(\mathbf{C})(-1)\overset{i(-1)}{\rightarrowtail}Z(\mathbf{C})(-1)\overset{p(-1)}{\twoheadrightarrow}H(\mathbf{C})(-1)

we obtain that
\overline{i(-1)}
is an epimorphism, hence its cokernel
is
0
.

(2) We prove (a). As in part 1, it follows that under the given assumptions
we have the exact sequence
9.1.4
. Moreover, the target
of
\lambda(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
is
0
and
\overline{i(-1)}

is an isomorphism, hence the assertion.
∎

10.
Some cotorsion theories in categories of complexes

10.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and
\mathcal{K}
a class
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
. Recall that
ex(\mathcal{K})
is the class of
exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}
that are degreewise in
\mathcal{K}

and
ex[\mathcal{K}]
is the class of exact complexes in
\mathcal{A}

that have cycles in
\mathcal{K}
. We define
dg(\mathcal{K})=^{\perp}ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
and
dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=ex[\mathcal{K}]^{\perp}
.

10.1.1.

[
12
, Proposition 3.6]
Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

be a cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
.

(1) If
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects
then
(ex[\mathcal{K}],dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
is a
cotorsion theory and
dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex[\mathcal{K}]^{\perp_{h}}
.

(2) If
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects then

(dg(\mathcal{K}),ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])
is a cotorsion theory
and
dg(\mathcal{K})=Ch(\mathcal{K})\cap^{\perp_{h}}ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
.

Proof.

(1) We only sketch the argument. One uses
1.2.3
with

\mathcal{S}=\{S^{n}(Y)\}
, where
n
is an integer and

Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
, together with the exact sequences

S^{n}(Y)\rightarrowtail D^{n+1}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow S^{n+1}(Y)

and
S^{n}(Y)\rightarrowtail S^{n}(A)\twoheadrightarrow S^{n}(B)
.
The description of
dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
follows from
8.2.6
(2).
The proof of part 2 is similar.
∎

10.1.2.

In the situation
10.1.1
, if
\mathcal{K}
is left exact then
(
8.2.7
)
ex[\mathcal{K}]\cap dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=ex[\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]=dg(\mathcal{K})\cap ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
.

10.1.3.

[
14
, Proposition 3.3]
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian
category and
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
a cotorsion theory
in
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

is generated by
\mathcal{S}
and that
\mathcal{A}
has enough

\underline{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}
objects (
1.2.14
),
meaning that for each
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is a monomorphism

A\to Y
with
Y\in\underline{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}
. Then

(ex(\mathcal{K}),ex(\mathcal{K})^{\perp})

is a cotorsion theory and
ex(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}=Ch(\mathcal{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}})\cap ex(\mathcal{K})^{\perp_{h}}
.

Proof.

We shall use
1.2.3
. We put
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{D^{m}(Y),S^{n}(\underline{Y})\}
,
where
Y\in\mathcal{S},\underline{Y}\in\underline{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}

and
m,n
are integers.

Step 1

We show that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset ex(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
.
We fix an exact sequence of complexes

(14)

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}

with
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\in ex(\mathcal{K})
. Applying the functor

Ch(\mathcal{A})(-,D^{m}(Y))
to (
14
) we obtain by
adjunction an exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{m-1},Y)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{m-1},Y)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{m-1},Y)

with the last morphism an epimorphism since

\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{m-1}\in\mathcal{K}={}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}
.
Applying the functor
Ch(\mathcal{A})(-,S^{n}(\underline{Y}))

to the exact sequence (
14
) we obtain by
adjunction the exact sequence

(15)

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}),\underline{Y})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}),\underline{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),\underline{Y})

Let
f:\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\to\underline{Y}
.
Form the commutative solid arrow diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\underline{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{n-1}}}

where
PO
means pushout and the two monomorphisms in the top row
are so since
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
is exact. By assumption the map

\underline{Y}\to PO
has a retraction, therefore there is a dotted arrow
that makes commutative the upper triangular diagram containing
f
.
This implies that the right arrow of the exact sequence (
15
)
is an epimorphism.

Step 2

We show that
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset ex(\mathcal{K})
.
Let
\mathbf{C}\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
Y\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow B

be an exact sequence with
Y\in\mathcal{S}
. Applying the functor

Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C},-)
to the exact sequence

D^{m}(Y)\rightarrowtail D^{m}(A)\twoheadrightarrow D^{m}(B)

and using adjunction we obtain the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{m-1},Y)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{m-1},A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{m-1},B)

This implies that
\mathbf{C}_{m-1}\in{}^{\perp}\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{K}
,
hence
\mathbf{C}
is degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
. Let now
n
be an integer.
We can find a monomorphism
\mathbf{C}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C})\to\underline{Y}

with
\underline{Y}\in\underline{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}
.
Applying the functor
Ch(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C},-)
to the exact sequence

S^{n-1}(\underline{Y})\rightarrowtail D^{n}(\underline{Y})\twoheadrightarrow S^{n}(\underline{Y})

and using adjunction we obtain the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n-1}/B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C}),\underline{Y})\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n-1},\underline{Y})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C}),\underline{Y})

This implies that there is a dotted arrow in the diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}/B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}/B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}}
\displaystyle{{\underline{Y}}}

that makes the triangular diagram commute. Therefore
\mathbf{C}
is exact.
The description of
ex(\mathcal{K})^{\perp}
follows from
8.2.6
(2).
∎

The previous result was proved in
[
14
]
under the assumptions
(1)
\mathsf{Ext}^{n}(X,Y)=0
for all
n\geqslant 1
and all

X\in\mathcal{K},Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and (2)
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

contains a cogenerator of finite injective dimension.

10.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a complete and cocomplete abelian category.
We consider the natural abelian THC-situation (
2.2.1
)
associated to
\mathcal{A}
and the standard THC-situation

T:Ch(\mathrm{Ab})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}:Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathrm{Ab})

C:Ch(\mathrm{Ab})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

associated to the natural one (
6.1
). Let

(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a cotorsion theory
in
\mathcal{A}
. We consider the cotorsion
theories
(Ch(\mathcal{K}),Ch(\mathcal{K})^{\perp})
and

(^{\perp}Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))

in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
(
4.1.2
). Let
\mathfrak{K}_{2}

be a class that consists of right bounded complexes that are
degreewise in
\mathcal{K}
.

10.2.1.

We define

(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap\mathfrak{K}_{2}^{\perp_{h}}
; in the notations
2.2.4

we have

(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathfrak{K}_{2},-)^{-1}(ex(\mathrm{Ab}))\cap Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

If
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects then

(^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
is a cotorsion theory and

{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})=Ch(\mathcal{K})\cap^{\perp_{h}}(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
.

Proof.

We shall use
2.2.4
. We take
\mathcal{K}_{2}=(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),\mathcal{Y}_{3}=Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),\mathcal{Y}_{1}=ex(\mathrm{Ab}),\mathcal{S}_{3}=\{D^{m}(X)\}
, where

m
is an integer and
X\in\mathcal{K}
,
\mathcal{S}_{1}=S^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
,
where
n
is an integer. Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{K}_{2}
.
By
8.1.1
(1bis) the functor
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
preserves the epimorphisms with kernel in
Ch(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
.
We claim that
Ch(\mathcal{A})
has enough
(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

objects. Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. By
4.1.2

there is a monomorphism
\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{Y}
with

\mathbf{Y}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
and by
4.2.3

the complex
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{Y})

is exact, therefore
\mathbf{Y}\in(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
.
The description of
{}^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

follows from
8.2.6
(2).
∎

10.2.2.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

objects and enough
\underline{\mathcal{K}}
objects, the latter
meaning that for each
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an
epimorphism
X\to A
with
X\in\underline{\mathcal{K}}
. Then

(^{\perp}((\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex(\mathcal{K})),(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex(\mathcal{K}))

is a cotorsion theory and

{}^{\perp}((\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex(\mathcal{K}))=Ch(\mathcal{K})\cap^{\perp_{h}}((\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp})\cap ex(\mathcal{K}))
.

Proof.

This follows from
1.2.3
applied to the cotorsion
theories
(^{\perp}(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),(\mathfrak{K}_{2};\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))

and
(^{\perp}ex(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}),ex(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))

(
10.1.3
).
∎

10.3.

We recall
[
1
]
that complex in
\mathcal{A}
is said to be dg-injective
if it is degreewise injective and it belongs to
ex(\mathcal{A})^{\perp_{h}}
.
Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC-situation with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}

complete and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
satisfying AB4. We consider the
standard THC-situation associated to it (
6.1
).
We recall that
\mathcal{F}
is the class of flat objects of

\mathcal{A}
(
3.1
) and
\mathfrak{F}_{h}
the
class of h-flat complexes in
\mathcal{A}

(
8.3.2
). We define
\mathfrak{F}_{dg}=\mathfrak{F}_{h}\cap Ch(\mathcal{F})
; the objects of

\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
are called dg-flat complexes
[
1
]
.

10.3.1.

(1)
[
1
]
For a complex
\mathbf{P}
in
\mathcal{A}

the following are equivalent:

(a)
\mathbf{P}
is dg-flat;

(b)
H(\mathbf{P},-)
preserves dg-injective complexes;

(c)
T(-,\mathbf{P})
sends monomorphisms with exact
cokernel to monomorphisms with exact cokernel.

(2) The pair
(\mathfrak{F}_{dg},\mathfrak{F}_{dg}^{\perp})

is a left exact cotorsion theory and

\mathfrak{F}_{dg}^{\perp}=Ch(\mathcal{F}^{\perp})\cap\mathfrak{F}_{dg}^{\perp_{h}}
.

(3) For every exact complex
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

the functor
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
sends monomorphisms with
dg-flat cokernel to monomorphisms with exact cokernel.

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. (a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
be a dg-injective complex in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
.
The object
H(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime},\mathbf{P})_{n}=\underset{p}{\prod}H(\mathbf{P}_{p},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{p+n})
is injective by
(the proof of)
3.1.2
and
1.2.1
(6).
From
6.1.9
we have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})

hence
H(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{\perp_{h}}
.
(b)
\Rightarrow
(a) Let
E^{\prime\prime}
be an injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
.
By
6.1.9
and
4.2
we have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime})))\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}),S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime}))=\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})_{0-},E^{\prime\prime})

Since
S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime})
is dg-injective we obtain that

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})_{0-},E^{\prime\prime})
is exact.
Since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has enough injective objects it follows
(
1.1.2
(1)) that
\mathbf{P}
is h-flat. Let now

A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail B^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C^{\prime}
be an
exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Applying

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}(-,H(\mathbf{P},S^{0}(E^{\prime\prime})))

to the exact sequence
S^{0}(A^{\prime})\rightarrowtail S^{0}(B^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow S^{0}(C^{\prime})
we obtain from
8.1.1
(1)
and
4.2
the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(C^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P}_{-},E^{\prime\prime}))\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(B^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P}_{-},E^{\prime\prime}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(A^{\prime},H(\mathbf{P}_{-},E^{\prime\prime}))

where
H(\mathbf{P}_{-},E^{\prime\prime})_{n}=H(\mathbf{P}_{-n},E^{\prime\prime})
has
differential
H((-1)^{n+1}d_{-n+1},E^{\prime\prime})
,
d
being the differential of

\mathbf{P}
. Using adjunction and
1.1.2
(1) we then have
that
\mathbf{P}_{n}\in\mathcal{F}
for all integers
n
.
(a)
\Rightarrow
(c) Let
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be an exact sequence in

Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
with
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
exact. Then

T(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})\rightarrowtail T(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})
is an exact sequence
as required. (c)
\Rightarrow
(a) Applying
T(-,\mathbf{P})
to the exact
sequence
0\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
we obtain that

\mathbf{P}
is h-flat. Let now
