A^{\prime}\rightarrowtail B^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C^{\prime}
be an exact sequence in

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
; then we have the exact sequence

T(D^{1}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})\rightarrowtail T(D^{1}(B^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})\twoheadrightarrow T(D^{1}(C^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})
. Since

T(D^{1}(A^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})_{n}=T(A^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{n-1})\oplus T(A^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{n})
for all integers
n
we obtain that

T(A^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{n})\to T(B^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{n})
is a
monomorphism.

(2) We shall use
2.2.2
. We take
\mathcal{K}_{1}=ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathcal{X}_{2}=Ch(\mathcal{F}),\mathcal{X}_{3}=ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2}=\{D^{m}(Y)\}
, where
m
is an integer and

Y\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
,
\mathcal{S}_{3}=\{S^{n}(E^{\prime\prime})\}
, where
n

is an integer and
E^{\prime\prime}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and

\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{P}

be an exact sequence in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
with

\mathbf{P}\in Ch(\mathcal{F})
. By
3.1.6
(1) we have the exact
sequence
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rightarrowtail T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})
. We now claim that

Ch(\mathcal{A})
has enough
\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
objects.
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. By
4.1.2

there is an epimorphism
\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{C}
with

\mathbf{P}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
. By
4.2.6
and
8.3.3
(3)
we have
ex[\mathcal{F}]\subset^{\perp_{h}}ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]\subset\mathfrak{F}_{h}
, hence the claim. Left exactness of

\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
follows from
2.2.3
and
3.1.5
.
The description of
\mathfrak{F}_{dg}^{\perp}
follows from
8.2.6
(2).

(3) Let
\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{P}

be an exact sequence in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathbf{P}
dg-flat.
By
3.1.6
(1) we have the exact sequence

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\rightarrowtail T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})
with

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})
exact.
∎

10.3.2.

We define (
2.2.2
)

tex(\mathcal{F})=T(S^{0}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}),-)^{-1}(ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}))\cap ex(\mathcal{F})

where
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the class of injective objects of

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
; the objects of
tex(\mathcal{F})

are often called
F
-totally acyclic complexes. If
\mathcal{A}

has enough
\underline{\mathcal{F}^{\perp}}
objects then

(tex(\mathcal{F}),tex(\mathcal{F})^{\perp})
is a left exact
cotorsion theory and
tex(\mathcal{F})^{\perp}=Ch(\mathcal{F}^{\perp})\cap tex(\mathcal{F})^{\perp_{h}}
.

Proof.

We shall use
2.2.2
. We take
\mathcal{K}_{1}=\{S^{0}(E^{\prime})\}
,
where
E^{\prime}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}_{2}=ex(\mathcal{F})
,
\mathcal{X}_{3}=ex(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathcal{S}_{2}=\{D^{m}(C(A^{\prime},E^{\prime\prime})),S^{n}(\underline{Y^{\prime}})\}
,
where
m,n
are integers,
A^{\prime}\in Ob(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
,
E^{\prime\prime}
is an
injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
,
\underline{Y}\in\underline{\mathcal{F}^{\perp}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{3}=\{S^{p}(I^{\prime\prime})\}
,
where
p
is an integer and
I_{3}
is an injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
E^{\prime}
be an injective object of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and

\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{P}

an exact sequence in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathbf{P}\in ex(\mathcal{F})
.
By
3.1.6
(1) we have the exact sequence

T(S^{0}(E^{\prime}),\mathbf{A})\rightarrowtail T(S^{0}(E^{\prime}),\mathbf{B})\twoheadrightarrow T(S^{0}(E^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})
. We now claim that

Ch(\mathcal{A})
has enough
tex(\mathcal{F})
objects.
Let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
. By
4.1.2

there is an epimorphism
\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{C}
with

\mathbf{P}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
, therefore it suffices to show
that
\mathbf{P}\in tex(\mathcal{F})
. We have
T(S^{0}(E^{\prime}),\mathbf{P})=T(E^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{-0})
, where
T(E^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{-0})_{n}=T(E^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{n})
has differential
T(E^{\prime},d)
,
d
being the
differential of
\mathbf{P}
. But then
T(E^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{-0})
is exact
by
3.1.6
(1). Left exactness of
tex(\mathcal{F})

follows from
2.2.3
and
3.1.5
. The description of

tex(\mathcal{F})^{\perp}
follows from
8.2.6
(2).
∎

11.
Cotorsion theories and corner morphisms for categories
of complexes

11.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category and let

\mathbf{A}\overset{u}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{B}\overset{\pi}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{C}
,

\mathbf{K}\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{E}\overset{v}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{F}
be
exact sequences in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
. We denote
by
uHv
the natural morphism

(16)

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{E})\to\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E})\times_{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{F})}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{F})

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
and assume

\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{K}),\mathbf{K}\in dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

(
10.1
). Then we have an exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})\overset{\bar{\pi}\bar{i}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{E})\overset{uHv}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E})\times_{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{F})}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{F})

If, moreover,
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
or
\mathbf{K}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]

then
uHv
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\pi}}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\pi}}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{K})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{i}}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{E})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{F})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{F})}}

There is a natural morphism
\alpha:\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})\to{\rm Ker}\,uHv
such that the triangular diagram

\displaystyle{{{\rm Ker}\,uHv}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{E})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{E})\times_{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{F})}\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})}
\displaystyle{{\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\pi}\bar{i}}

commutes.

Step 1

We show that
\alpha
is an epimorphism. Let
n,p

be integers and
f_{p}:\mathbf{B}_{p}\to\mathbf{E}_{p+n}
a morphism
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
f_{p}u_{p}=0
and
v_{p+n}f_{p}=0
.
Then there are morphisms
g_{p}:\mathbf{C}_{p}\to\mathbf{E}_{p+n}

and
h_{p}:\mathbf{B}_{p}\to\mathbf{K}_{p+n}
such that
g_{p}\pi_{p}=f_{p}

and
i_{p+n}h_{p}=f_{p}
. We obtain the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{B}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{p}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{K}_{p+n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{p+n}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{p}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{E}_{p+n}}}

This diagram has a diagonal filler
w_{p}:\mathbf{C}_{p}\to\mathbf{K}_{p+n}
,
therefore
\alpha_{n}((w_{p}))=(f_{p})
.

Step 2

We show that
uHv
, defined as

uHv((f_{p}))=(f_{p}u_{p},v_{p+n}f_{p})
, is an epimorphism.
Let
n,p
be integers and

f_{p}:\mathbf{A}_{p}\to\mathbf{E}_{p+n},g_{p}:\mathbf{B}_{p}\to\mathbf{F}_{p+n}
morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
such that

g_{p}u_{p}=v_{p+n}f_{p}
. By
1.2.1
(4) the
commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{K}_{p+n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{A}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{p}}
\scriptstyle{u_{p}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{E}_{p+n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{p+n}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{B}_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{p}}
\displaystyle{\mathbf{F}_{p+n}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{p}}}

has a diagonal filler
h_{p}:\mathbf{B}_{p}\to\mathbf{E}_{p+n}
.
But this means that
(uHv)_{n}((h_{p}))=((f_{p}),(g_{p}))
.

Step 3

If
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{K}]
or

\mathbf{K}\in ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
then

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{K})
is
exact, hence
uHv
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

11.2.

Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC–situation. Assume that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}

satisfies AB4 and has enough injective objects. Consider
the standard THC–situation (
6.1
)

T:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

H:Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

C:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

associated to the given one. Let
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{A}\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
be exact sequences

Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and
Ch(\mathcal{A})
, respectively. We denote by
i^{\prime}Tj

the natural morphism

T(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})\bigcup_{T(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})}T(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\to T(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})

11.2.1.

If
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in dg(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{F})

then
i^{\prime}Tj
is a monomorphism that is a quasi-isomorphism
if either
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]
or
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
.

Proof.

By
6.1.4
and
3.1.7
the morphism
i^{\prime}Tj
is a monomorphism.
By
7.3.5
we have the exact sequence

T(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})\bigcup_{T(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})}T(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{A})\overset{i^{\prime}Tj}{\rightarrowtail}T(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{B})\twoheadrightarrow T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})

By
8.3.3
(3) we have
dg(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
.
Suppose
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]
. By
10.3.1
(3) applied
to
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and the exact sequence
\mathbf{A}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
we obtain that

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact, which is what we wanted.
The case
\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{F})
is dealt with similarly.
∎

11.2.2.

Let
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}\overset{p^{\prime\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
be an exact sequence in
Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
.
We denote by
jHp^{\prime\prime}
the natural morphism

H(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\to H(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})\times_{H(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})}H(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})

and by
i^{\prime}Cp^{\prime\prime}
the natural morphism

C(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\to C(\mathbf{B}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})\times_{C(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{D}^{\prime\prime})}C(\mathbf{A}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})

(1) If
\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{F})
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
is
dg-injective then
jHp^{\prime\prime}
is an epimorphism with kernel in

dg(\mathcal{F}^{\prime\perp})
. If, moreover,
\mathbf{C}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
or
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
is injective
then
{\rm Ker}\,jHp^{\prime\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]
.

(2) If
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in dg(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
is dg-injective
then
i^{\prime}Cp^{\prime\prime}
is an epimorphism with kernel in
dg(\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
.
If, moreover,
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}]
or
\mathbf{K}^{\prime\prime}
is
injective then
{\rm Ker}\,i^{\prime}Cp^{\prime\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
.

Proof.

Both 1 and 2 are equivalent to
11.2.1
.
∎

11.2.3.

[
13
, Theorem 5.1]

Suppose now that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and for all

X,Y,Z\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is a natural isomorphism
T(X,T(Y,Z))\cong T(T(X,Y),Z)
. Then (
3.3.1
)
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})

is
T
-closed. The cotorsion theory
(dg(\mathcal{F}),ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}])

is
T
-closed. Moreover, if
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
and

\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{F})
then
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\in ex[\mathcal{F}]
.

Proof.

For the first part, by
3.3.2
it suffices to show
that if
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})\in dg(\mathcal{F})
and

(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime\prime})\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
then

C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
.
Let
P
be a flat object of
\mathcal{A}
.

Step 1

We show that
H(S^{r}(P),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
for all integers
r
. We have

H(S^{r}(P),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})=H(P,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{r+})
, where

H(P,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{r+})_{n}=H(P,\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}_{r+n})
has
differential
H(P,d^{\prime\prime}_{r+n})
. By
2.1.1
(1) the functor

H(P,-)
preserves epimorphisms with kernel in

\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
, hence
H(S^{r}(P),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
is exact.
By
2.1.1
(2bis) we have
B_{n}(H(S^{r}(P),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))=H(P,B_{r+n}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.

Step 2

We show that

C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\in ex(\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
.
By
2.1.1
(2bis) and
1.2.1
(6) we have

C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})\in Ch(\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
.
Since (
6.1.9
)

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{r}(P),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},H(S^{r}(P),\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

we obtain from Step 1 that the complex

\mathcal{A}(P,C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})_{r+})
(
4.2
) is
exact, therefore (
1.1.2
(1))
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})
is
exact.

Step 3

We show that
Z_{n}(C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
for all integers
n
. Let

A\rightarrowtail B\twoheadrightarrow P
be an exact sequence in

\mathcal{A}
. Applying the functor

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(-,C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

to the exact sequence
S^{n}(A)\rightarrowtail S^{n}(B)\twoheadrightarrow S^{n}(P)
we obtain from
8.1.1
(1) the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{n}(P),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{n}(B),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{n}(A),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

By Step 2 the complex
\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(S^{n}(P),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

is exact, hence, upon applying the functor
Z_{0}
to the previous
exact sequence, we obtain the exact sequence

Ch(\mathcal{A})(S^{n}(P),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\rightarrowtail Ch(\mathcal{A})(S^{n}(B),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\twoheadrightarrow Ch(\mathcal{A})(S^{n}(A),C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))

By adjunction it follows that
Z_{n}(C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}))\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
. Therefore the cotorsion theory

(dg(\mathcal{F}),ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}])

is
T
-closed.
Suppose now that
(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},d^{\prime})\in ex[\mathcal{F}]

and
\mathbf{C}\in dg(\mathcal{F})
. Since

dg(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
we have that

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})
is exact. Since

(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}^{\perp})
is
T
-closed we have

T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})\in Ch(\mathcal{F})
. Let
n

be an integer and
Y\rightarrowtail A\twoheadrightarrow B

an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
with
Y\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
.
By
8.1.1
(1bis) we have the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(Y))\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(A))\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(B))

From
6.1.9
we have

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(Y))\cong\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y)))

We claim that
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y))\in ex[\mathcal{F}^{\perp}]
.
One has
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y))=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n-},Y)
, where

C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n-},Y)_{m}=C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n-m},Y)
has
differential
C((-1)^{m+1}d^{\prime}_{n-m+1},Y)
. By
2.1.1
((2bis) and (1bis))
the functor
C(-,Y)
preserves monomorphisms with cokernel in

\mathcal{F}
, therefore
C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y))
is exact.
By an argument similar to the proof of
6.2.3
we have

B_{m}(C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y)))=C(B_{n-m}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Y)
.
By
2.1.1
(2bis) we have
C(B_{n-m}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}),Y)\in\mathcal{F}^{\perp}
, hence the claim is proved. Consequently, the complex

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{C},C(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},S^{n}(Y)))

is exact. Applying the functor
Z_{0}
to the last displayed exact sequence
we obtain the exact sequence

Ch(\mathcal{A})(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(Y))\rightarrowtail Ch(\mathcal{A})(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}A))\twoheadrightarrow Ch(\mathcal{A})(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}),S^{n}(B))

hence by adjunction the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(T_{n}/B_{n},Y)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(T_{n}/B_{n},A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(T_{n}/B_{n},B)

where
T_{n}/B_{n}=T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C})_{n}/B_{n}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))
.
It follows that
Z_{n-1}(T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{C}))\in\mathcal{F}
.
∎

12.
Some resolutions of complexes

12.1.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. Recall (
5.1.6
)
that a cotorsion theory
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
in

\mathcal{A}
is complete if it is left and right complete. This is
equivalent to
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
being left
complete and
\mathcal{A}
having enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

objects and to
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

being right complete and
\mathcal{A}
having enough

\mathcal{K}
objects.

12.1.1.

[
18
, Proposition 5.4]
Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be
a complete cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
. Every morphism of

\mathcal{A}
can be factored into a monomorphism with cokernel in

\mathcal{K}
followed by an epimorphism with kernel
in
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism. Choose a monomorphism
A\to Y

with
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and form the pushout diagram

\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PO}}

This diagram is exact (
1.2.8
) hence we have an exact
sequence
A\rightarrowtail B\oplus Y\twoheadrightarrow PO

and
f
is the composite
A\rightarrowtail B\oplus Y\twoheadrightarrow B
, where the second arrow is a projection
morphism. We can find an exact sequence

Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail X\twoheadrightarrow PO
with
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

and
X\in\mathcal{K}
. Form then the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\displaystyle{{B\oplus Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{PO}
\displaystyle{{B}}

where
PB
means pullback. Since the epimorphisms with kernel in

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
are closed under composition, we obtain
the desired factorization.
∎

12.1.2.

[
24
, Lemma 2.3]
(1) Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

be a left exact and left complete cotorsion theory
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has enough

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects. Then
ex(\mathcal{A})={\rm Coker}\,(ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]\rightarrowtail ex[\mathcal{K}])
.

(1bis) Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a right exact and right
complete cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has enough

\mathcal{K}
objects. Then

ex(\mathcal{A})={\rm Ker}\,(ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]\twoheadrightarrow ex[\mathcal{K}])
.

Proof.

(1) Let
\mathbf{C}
be an exact complex and
n
an integer. We can find an
exact sequence
B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})\rightarrowtail B_{n}(\mathbf{X})\twoheadrightarrow B_{n}(\mathbf{C})
with
B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp},B_{n}(\mathbf{X})\in\mathcal{K}
. We shall complete the solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}}

with the dotted arrows, first to the left of the vertical sequence and then to the right.
Continuing we obtain the desired exact sequence
\mathbf{Y}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Step 1

Form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB^{\prime}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}}

where
PB_{n+1}
and
PB^{\prime}_{n+1}
mean pullback and
B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow PB_{n+1}
is an exact sequence with

\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{K}
and
B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
We put
B_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})=PB^{\prime}_{n+1}
and we have
B_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})\in\mathcal{K}
.
Consider now the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}}

where
\mathbf{Y}_{n+1}
is the kernel of the composite morphism

\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\to PB_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n+1}
.
By the universal property of kernel we have the induced dotted arrows
that make the resulting square diagrams commute; moreover, by the snake
diagram the dotted sequence of the previous diagram is a short exact sequence.

Step 2

Factor (
12.1.1
) the composite morphism

B_{n}(\mathbf{X})\to B_{n}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
into a
monomorphism
B_{n}(\mathbf{X})\to\mathbf{X}_{n}
with cokernel

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{X})\in\mathcal{K}
followed by an epimorphism

\mathbf{X}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}
with kernel
\mathbf{Y}_{n}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
We obtain the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})}}

The dotted arrows
B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})\to\mathbf{Y}_{n}
and

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{X})\to B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})
are induced by the universal property
of kernel and cokernel. Letting
B_{n-1}(\mathbf{Y})
be the kernel of

B_{n-1}(\mathbf{X})\to B_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})
, we obtain as before that the
dotted horizontal sequence of the previous diagram is a short exact sequence.
By
1.2.10
we then have that
B_{n-1}(\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
∎

12.1.3.

[
23
, Theorem 3.4]
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4. Let
\mathfrak{K},\mathfrak{L}

be classes of complexes in
\mathcal{A}
such that

(a)
\mathfrak{L}
is closed under extensions;

(b) for every right bounded complex
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}
there are
a complex
\mathbf{X}\in\mathfrak{L}
and a quasi-isomorphism

\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
;

(c)
\mathfrak{L}\subset^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K}
;

(d)
\mathfrak{K}\subset\mathfrak{L}^{\perp_{e}}
.

Then for every complex
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}

there are
\mathbf{X}\in^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K}
and
a quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

Let
k
be an integer and consider the complex
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})

(
4.2.4
). We can find a complex
\mathbf{X}^{k}\in\mathfrak{L}

and a quasi-isomorphism
v^{k}:\mathbf{X}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\tau_{k}(\mathbf{X})
. Let
u
be the composite

\mathbf{X}^{k}\overset{v^{k}}{\to}\tau_{k}(\mathbf{X})\to\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{X})
;
then
u
is a morphism of right bounded complexes, therefore

Con(u)
and
Cyl(u)
are right bounded. Consider the diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{k}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime\prime}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{\prime}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{u}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\displaystyle{{Cyl(u)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\displaystyle{Con(u)}
\displaystyle{{\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C})}}

where
\tilde{u},\tilde{\pi},\beta
are as in
4.1.3
,

\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
,
v^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism and

\mathbf{X}^{k-1}
is the pullback of
v^{\prime}
along
\tilde{\pi}
.
Then
v^{\prime\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism and
\mathbf{X}^{k-1}\in\mathfrak{L}
.
Since
\tilde{u}
is degreewise split, so is
u^{k}
. We put

v^{k-1}=\beta v^{\prime\prime}
and we obtain the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{k}}
\scriptstyle{v^{k}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{k-1}}
\displaystyle{{\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C})}}

The morphism
H(\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}))\to H(\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C}))

is
0\to H_{k}(\mathbf{C})
in degree
k-1
and the identity in degrees

\geqslant k
and
\leqslant k-2
, therefore in the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{X}^{k})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(u^{k})}
\scriptstyle{H(v^{k})}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{X}^{k-1})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H(v^{k-1})}
\displaystyle{{H(\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C}))}}

the morphism
H(u^{k})
is degreewise split.
Using the same procedure for
v^{k-1}
in place of
v^{k}

we obtain a commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{k-1}}
\scriptstyle{v^{k-1}}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}^{k-2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v^{k-2}}
\displaystyle{{\tau_{k-1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\tau_{k-2}(\mathbf{C})}}

and so on. The resulting direct system of right bounded complexes

\mathbf{X}^{k}\overset{u^{k}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{X}^{k-1}\overset{u^{k-1}}{\rightarrowtail}\mathbf{X}^{k-2}\rightarrowtail...

has the properties that
u^{i}
is degreewise split,

{\rm Coker}\,u^{i}\in\mathfrak{L}
and
H(u^{i})
is degreewise
split for all
i\leqslant k
. Let
\mathbf{X}=\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}\mathbf{X}^{i}
; we then obtain an epimorphism
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}

and a commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}H(\mathbf{X}^{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\underset{i\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant k})^{op}}{{\rm Colim}\,}H(\tau_{i}(\mathbf{C}))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{H(\mathbf{C})}}

It is standard that the bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism.
The left vertical arrow is an isomorphism since so is
H(v^{i})
.
By
4.2.7
the top horizontal arrow is an isomorphism,
therefore
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{C}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
Let
\mathbf{D}\in\mathfrak{K}
; then we have the exact sequence

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}({\rm Coker}\,u^{i},\mathbf{D})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{X}^{i-1},\mathbf{D})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbf{D})

hence the inverse system

(\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbf{D}))_{i\geqslant k}

satisfies the Mittag-Leffler condition. It follows that

\mathbf{X}\in^{\perp_{h}}\mathfrak{K}
.
∎

We note that
12.1.3
admits a dual formulation
whose proof uses the ‘mapping path factorization’ of a
morphism of complexes instead of the factorization given
in
4.1.3
.

12.1.4.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4. Let
\mathcal{K}

be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
such that for every

A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A

with
X\in^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
. For every complex
\mathbf{C}

in
\mathcal{A}
ther are
\mathbf{X}\in Ch(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})\cap^{\perp_{h}}ex[(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})^{\perp}]
and a
quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.
In particular, let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a
cotorsion theory in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has
enough
\mathcal{K}
objects. For every complex
\mathbf{C}

in
\mathcal{A}
there are
\mathbf{X}\in dg(\mathcal{K})
and a
quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

In
12.1.3
we take
\mathfrak{K}=ex[(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})^{\perp}]
and
\mathfrak{L}

to be the class of right bounded complexes that are degreewise
in
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{K}
. Condition (a) follows from
1.2.1
(5),
condition (b) is standard and follows from assumption, condition (c)
follows from
4.2.3
and condition (d) from
8.1.1
(1)
since
\mathfrak{K}\subset Ch((^{\perp}\mathcal{K})^{\perp})
.
The construction of
\mathbf{X}
and
1.2.1
(7) show that

\mathbf{X}\in Ch(^{\perp}\mathcal{K})
.
∎

12.1.5.

[
23
, Section 5 A]

Let

T:\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\ H:\mathcal{A}^{op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\ C:\mathcal{A}^{\prime op}\times\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{A}

be an abelian THC–situation. Suppose that

\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}
satisfy AB4 and have
enough flat objects and that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has enough
injective objects. Consider the
standard THC–situation (
6.1
)

T:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\times Ch(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})

H:Ch(\mathcal{A})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

C:Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{op}\times Ch(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

associated to the given one.

(1) For every complex
\mathbf{C}
in
\mathcal{A}
there
are a dg-flat complex
\mathbf{P}
and quasi-isomorphism

\mathbf{P}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

(2) For every complex
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

(a)
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P})
is exact whenever
\mathbf{P}\in\mathfrak{F}_{h}\cap ex(\mathcal{A})
;

(b) the functor
T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},-)
preserves quasi-isomorphisms
between h-flat objects.

Proof.

(1) Since (
8.3.3
(3))
dg(\mathcal{F})\subset\mathfrak{F}_{dg}
,
the assertion follows from
12.1.4
applied to

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{F}
.

(2) (a) By part 1 we can find a dg-flat complex
\mathbf{P}^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and a quasi-isomorphism
u^{\prime}:\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
.
The assertion follows then from
8.3.2
.

(b) Let
u:\mathbf{P}_{1}\to\mathbf{P}_{2}
be a quasi-isomorphism
between h-flat objects in
Ch(\mathcal{A})
and let

u^{\prime}:\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be a quasi-isomorphism with

\mathbf{P}^{\prime}
dg-flat; we have then the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{1})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},u)}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{1})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{P}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(u^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{2})}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{1})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},u)}
\displaystyle{{T(\mathbf{C}^{\prime},\mathbf{P}_{2})}}

By
8.3.2
the top horizontal and the vertical arrows are
quasi-isomorphisms, therefore so is the bottom horizontal arrow
by the two out of three property of quasi-isomorphisms.
∎

12.1.6.

Let
A
be a left noetherian ring that has finite injective dimension
as a left
A
-module and
\mathcal{I}
be the class of injective
left
A
-modules. For every complex
\mathbf{C}
of left
A
-modules
there are
\mathbf{X}\in^{\perp_{h}}(Ch(\mathcal{I})\cap\{S^{0}(E),E\in\mathcal{I}\}^{\perp_{h}})
and a quasi-isomorphism

\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

Let
{}_{A}Mod
be the category of left
A
-modules.
We denote
\mathrm{Homgr}_{{}_{A}Mod}
by

\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}
.
In
12.1.3
we take
\mathfrak{K}=Ch(\mathcal{I})\cap\{S^{0}(E),E\in\mathcal{I}\}^{\perp_{h}}

and
\mathfrak{L}
to be the class of right bounded complexes of
left
A
-modules that are degreewise of finite injective dimension.
Condition (a) follows from horseshoe lemma, condition (b) follows
from assumptions on
A
and condition (d) from
8.1.1
(1).
We show (c). Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L},\mathbf{C}\in\mathfrak{K}

and let
i
be an integer such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{n}=0

for
n<i
. By
4.2.3
it suffices to show that

{}_{A}Mod(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})
and

{}_{A}Mod(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbf{C})
are exact.
Let

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{E_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}}
\displaystyle{{E_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\displaystyle{{...}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{n-1}}
\displaystyle{{E_{n}}}

be an exact sequence with
E_{0},E_{1},...,E_{n}
injective.
By
8.1.1
(1) we have exact sequences

\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(E_{n}),\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(E_{n-1}),\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1}),\mathbf{C}),

\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1}),\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(E_{n-2}),\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{n-2}),\mathbf{C}),

...,\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{1}),\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(E_{0}),\mathbf{C})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i}),\mathbf{C})

hence the implication (
\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{i}),\mathbf{C})
exact)
\Rightarrow
(
\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{i-1}),\mathbf{C})
exact) for
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.
Since
\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}({\rm Ker}\,d_{n-1}),\mathbf{C})

is exact we conclude that
\mathrm{Homgr}_{A}(S^{0}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{i}),\mathbf{C})
is exact. The proof that

{}_{A}Mod(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}_{r},\mathbf{C}_{r+})
is exact is similar.
∎

12.1.7.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4. Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a right exact and right complete cotorsion
theory in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has enough

\mathcal{K}
objects. Then the cotorsion theory

(dg(\mathcal{K}),ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])
is left complete.

Proof.

This follows from
12.1.4
,
12.1.2
(1bis), a standard
pushout argument and
9.1.5
(2).
∎

12.1.8.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4
∗
or is a
Grothendieck category. Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})

be a left exact and left complete cotorsion theory
in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has enough

\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects. Then the cotorsion theory

(ex[\mathcal{K}],dg(\mathcal{K}^{\perp}))
is right complete.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{A}
satisfies AB4
∗
the proof is dual to the proof
of
12.1.7
. Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck category
and let
\mathbf{C}
be a complex in
\mathcal{A}
.
By
[
19
, Théorème 2]
there are a dg-injective (
10.3
)
complex
\mathbf{Y}
and a quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{C}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{Y}
. In particular
\mathbf{Y}\in dg(\mathcal{K}]^{\perp})
.
The rest is a standard pushout argument using
12.1.2
(1) and

9.1.5
(2).
∎

12.1.9.

[
17
, Lemma I.4.6 2)]

Let
\mathcal{K}
be a class of objects of
\mathcal{A}
such that
(i)
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects, meaning that
for each
A\in Ob(\mathcal{A})
there is an epimorphism
X\to A

with
X\in\mathcal{K}
, and (ii)
\mathcal{K}
is closed under
extensions and left exact. For every complex
\mathbf{C}
in

\mathcal{A}
there are
\mathbf{X}\in Ch(\mathcal{K})

and a quasi-isomorphism
\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

Choose an epimorphism
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}

with
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
and then choose,
successively, epimorphisms
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\times_{\mathbf{C}_{0}}\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}
,
…,
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n}\to\mathbf{C}_{n}\times_{\mathbf{C}_{n-1}}\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n-1}
,…
with
\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1},...,\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n}\in\mathcal{K}
.
We obtain a complex
\mathbf{C}^{0}
and a quasi-isomorphism (
4.1.1
)

u^{0}:\mathbf{C}^{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
such that

\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n}=\mathbf{C}_{n}
and
u^{0}_{n}=1_{\mathbf{C}_{n}}

for
n<0
. Next choose an epimorphism
\mathbf{C}^{1}_{-1}\to\mathbf{C}^{0}_{-1}=\mathbf{C}_{-1}
with

\mathbf{C}^{1}_{-1}\in\mathcal{K}
, then choose an epimorphism

\mathbf{C}^{1}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}\times_{\mathbf{C}_{-1}}\mathbf{C}^{1}_{-1}
with
\mathbf{C}^{1}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
and put

\mathbf{C}^{1}_{1}=\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1}\times_{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}}\mathbf{C}^{1}_{0}
. The square diagram

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{1}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{1}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{0}}}

is exact hence (
1.2.8
)
\mathbf{C}^{1}_{1}\in\mathcal{K}
.
We obtain a complex
\mathbf{C}^{1}
and a quasi-isomorphism

u^{1}:\mathbf{C}^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{0}

such that
\mathbf{C}^{1}_{n}=\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n}
for
n\geqslant 2
,

\mathbf{C}^{1}_{n}=\mathbf{C}_{n}
for
n\leqslant-2
and

u^{1}_{n}
is an identity morphism except for
n\in\{-1,0,1\}
.
Continuing like in the preceding step we obtain, for every integer
k>0
,
a complex
\mathbf{C}^{k}
and a quasi-isomorphism

u^{k}:\mathbf{C}^{k}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{k-1}

such that
\mathbf{C}^{k}_{n}=\mathbf{C}^{k-1}_{n}
for
n\geqslant-k+3
,

\mathbf{C}^{k}_{n}=\mathbf{C}_{n}
for
n\leqslant-k-1
and

u^{k}_{n}
is an identity morphism except for
n\in\{-k,-k+1,-k+2\}
.
Below is the diagram
\mathbf{C}^{3}\overset{u^{3}}{\to}\mathbf{C}^{2}\overset{u^{2}}{\to}\mathbf{C}^{1}\overset{u^{1}}{\to}\mathbf{C}^{0}\overset{u^{0}}{\to}\mathbf{C}
.

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{1}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{2}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{3}_{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{3}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}^{0}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The resulting inverse system
...\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{2}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{0}

has the property that for every integer
n
the inverse system

...\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{2}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{0}_{n}

is eventually constant and eventually in
\mathcal{K}
. Thus,
if we put
\mathbf{X}=\underset{k\in\mathbb{N}^{op}}{{\rm Lim}\,}\mathbf{C}^{k}
,
then
\mathbf{X}\in Ch(\mathcal{K})
and there is a quasi-isomorphism

\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}
.
∎

12.2.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian category. We denote by
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})

the full subcategory of
Ch(\mathcal{A})
with objects
\mathbf{C}
such that

\mathbf{C}_{n}=0
for
n<0
. The objects of
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})

will be called nonnegative complexes.

The inclusion functor
e_{0}:Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})\to Ch(\mathcal{A})

has a right adjoint
\tau_{0}
, where
\tau_{0}(\mathbf{C})
is the complex
defined in
4.2.4
. The functor
e_{0}
has a left adjoint
t_{0}

that sends
\mathbf{C}
to the complex
...\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}\to 0\to...
. The functor
t_{0}
has a left adjoint

q_{0}
that sends a nonnegative complex
\mathbf{C}
to

...\to\mathbf{C}_{1}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}\to\mathbf{C}_{0}/B_{0}(\mathbf{C})\to 0\to...
.

For each integer
n\geqslant 0
we denote by
(-)_{n}:Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}
the evaluation at
n
functor; for
n>0
the functor

(-)_{n-1}
has the
n
th disk functor
D^{n}
as right adjoint and
D^{n}
has

(-)_{n}
as right adjoint.

We put
ex_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})=ex(\mathcal{A})\cap Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})

and for a class
\mathcal{K}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
we put

Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})=Ch(\mathcal{K})\cap Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})

and
ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}]=ex[\mathcal{K}]\cap Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
.

12.2.1.

[
17
, Lemma I.4.6 1)]
Let
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
be a cotorsion
theory in
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathcal{K}
objects.
Then
(Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K}),ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])

is a cotorsion theory in
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
that is left exact (left complete)
if
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}^{\perp})
is left exact (left complete). If
\mathcal{A}

has enough
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
objects then
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})

has enough
ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
objects. If

\mathcal{K}
is left exact then
ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}]=Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})\cap ex_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof.

We apply
2.1.2
(1bis) to the adjoint pair
(t_{0},e_{0})

and
(dg(\mathcal{K}),ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])
.
We have
e_{0}^{-1}(ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])=ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
. Let
\mathbf{C}\in^{\perp}(ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])
. Applying the functor

Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})(\mathbf{C},-)
to the exact sequence

D^{n}(Y)\rightarrowtail D^{n}(A)\twoheadrightarrow D^{n}(B)
with
n>0
and
Y\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}

we obtain by adjunction and the fact that

D^{n}(Y)\in ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]

the exact sequence

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n-1},Y)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n-1},A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{C}_{n-1},B)
.
This implies that
\mathbf{C}_{n-1}\in\mathcal{K}
for
n>0
. Conversely,
we have
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})\subset^{\perp_{h}}ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]

by
4.2.2
, so it suffices (
8.2.5
(3)) to show
that if
\mathbf{D}\in Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})
then

\mathrm{Homgr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{D},-)
preserves
epimorphisms with kernel in
ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
.
But this is true by
8.1.1
(1bis). Therefore

(Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K}),ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])

is a cotorsion theory. Left exactness is straightforward.
We show that
(Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K}),ex[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}])

is left complete. Let
\mathbf{C}
be a nonnegative complex.
We use induction over
n\geqslant 0
to construct a commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n}}}

with exact columns and with
\mathbf{X}_{n}\in\mathcal{K},B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
When
n=0
we can find an exact sequence

\mathbf{Y}_{0}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{X}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}_{0}
with
\mathbf{X}_{0}\in\mathcal{K},\mathbf{Y}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and we put

Z_{0}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{X}_{0},B_{0}(\mathbf{Y})=\mathbf{Y}_{0}
.
When
n>0
and given the previous diagram, we put

B_{n}(\mathbf{X})=B_{n}(\mathbf{X})\times_{Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})}Z_{n}(\mathbf{X})

and then we form the commutative diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB^{\prime}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{PB_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}}

where
PB_{n+1}
and
PB^{\prime}_{n+1}
mean pullback and
B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\twoheadrightarrow PB_{n+1}
is an exact
sequence with
\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\in\mathcal{K}
and

B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
.
We put
Z_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})=PB^{\prime}_{n+1}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{n+1}

to be the kernel of the composite morphism

\mathbf{X}_{n+1}\to PB_{n+1}\to\mathbf{C}_{n+1}
.
We obtain the commutative solid arrows diagram

\displaystyle{{B_{n+1}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Y}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{Y})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n+1}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{X}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{X})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{Z_{n+1}(\mathbf{C})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{\mathbf{C}_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle{{B_{n}(\mathbf{C})}}

By the universal property of kernel we have the induced dotted arrows that
make the resulting square diagrams commute; moreover, by the snake diagram
the dotted sequence of the previous diagram is a short exact sequence.
This finishes the inductive step. We have constructed an exact sequence

\mathbf{Y}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{X}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{C}

with
\mathbf{X}\in Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})
and

\mathbf{Y}\in ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
.
The fact that
Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
has enough

ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}^{\perp}]
objects follows in
the same way as for
Ch(\mathcal{A})
(
4.1.2
).
Finally, we show that
ex_{\geqslant 0}[\mathcal{K}]=Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})\cap ex_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
.
For this, let
\mathbf{C}\in Ch_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{K})\cap ex_{\geqslant 0}(\mathcal{A})
. For every
n\geqslant 1
we have
the exact sequence
Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow Z_{n-1}(\mathbf{C})
. Since
Z_{0}(\mathbf{C})=\mathbf{C}_{0}\in\mathcal{K}
, we obtain inductively that

Z_{n}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{K}
for all
n\geqslant 0
.
∎
